two mechanisms produce identical mean station error and mission outcomes differing by a factor of
two. Any mission-level claim inherited from a path-error result inherits that ambiguity, and the
right response is the one this section performs, which is to measure the transfer rather than assume
it. This is the paper's only place where it bounds its own inheritance with a number, and the
negative is worth more than the positive it replaced.

**A third reading, and it retires the behaviour layer as a design variable in this scenario.** Under
the cut-off, the fleet spends 47%, 72% and 84% of its follower-time NOT following at $a$ = 80, 160
and 320, having reverted to waypoint navigation, and mission success across those three points is
0.8643, 0.8613 and 0.8726 against 0.8872 with no disturbance at all. Leader-following can be off
five sixths of the time at no measurable cost. By the project's own test, that a knob which cannot
change a mission outcome is not a design variable, the follow behaviour is not one here, and the
scenario that would make it one has to be named before the layer is worth further work: a mission
whose success depends on the force being concentrated when it arrives rather than on its arriving.

## CR-59. THE ESTIMATOR REQUIREMENT: WHAT A DIFFICULTY ESTIMATE HAS TO BE GOOD AT

Registered before its battery. CR-56 priced difficulty information and found a closed form for it,
$\mathrm{VoI} = \mu\,cv$, measured against a console that either sees the realization exactly or
sees nothing but the population mean. Both ends are fictions. What a sponsor actually procures is an
estimator with a residual error, and the question a procurement document has to answer is which part
of that error to pay to remove. This conjecture separates the error into the two parts that are
separately purchasable and asks which one is binding.

`admit_bias` is a multiplicative calibration offset the console does not know is there, so it cannot
divide it out. `admit_sigma` is the log-scale spread that remains after any offset has been removed.
At $(1.0, 0.0)$ the estimate is the realization and every CR-56 number reproduces bit for bit.

**The mechanism the predictions come from, which is visible in the code and not inferred.** A refusal
costs the console no service time at all: `n_abandon_queue` increments and the loop `continue`s to
the next request on the same step. An admitted job that overruns its deadline costs the full draw:
the slot is claimed, `t_worked_late` accumulates it, and nothing is delivered. The two errors an
estimator can make are therefore priced differently by construction, and the price of a false
refusal is the opportunity cost of the freed slot, which is zero when there is nothing else to serve.

- **F1. The optimum is pessimistic.** Sweeping $b \in \{0.6, 0.8, 1.0, 1.25, 1.6, 2.0\}$ at
  $\sigma = 0$, $cv = 0.5$, sixteen assets, served per step has an interior maximum at $b^\star > 1$.
  FALSIFIER: the gated maximum sits at $b \le 1.0$, in which case the asymmetry argument above is
  wrong about this console and the whole conjecture is withdrawn.
- **F2. The pessimism is a property of the LOAD, not of the estimator.** At four assets, where the
  console is not saturated and a freed slot buys nothing, $b^\star = 1$. FALSIFIER: any $b > 1$ beats
  $b = 1$ at four assets at full tier. This is the prediction that makes the result a requirements
  statement rather than a calibration constant: a vendor cannot be given a target without being told
  the span of control.
- **F3. Spread and offset are substitutes, in one direction only.** $b^\star$ increases with
  $\sigma$: an estimator that cannot be made precise should be deliberately shifted high, because
  its own spread manufactures overruns. Registered at $\sigma \in \{0, 0.35, 0.7\}$. FALSIFIER:
  $b^\star(0.7) \le b^\star(0)$.
- **F4. Only spread is binding.** At its own best offset, an estimator with $\sigma = 0.7$ recovers
  most of the value that $\sigma = 0$ recovers at $b = 1$. Registered: the re-tuned $\sigma = 0.7$
  console lands within one third of the CR-56 acuity gain of the exact console. FALSIFIER: it lands
  below half, in which case offset does not compensate spread and the two errors are not tradeable.
- **F5, the whole-conjecture falsifier.** If the served-per-step surface over $(b, \sigma)$ is flat
  to within the gate's rate floor of 0.0090 at every load in the sweep, then estimator calibration
  is not a design variable in \sandtable{} by the project's own test, and CR-59 is retracted with a
  recorded negative: the paper then says that the value of triage is insensitive to how the triage
  is produced, which is a stronger and cheaper recommendation than any calibration target.

**What is NOT claimed.** That $b^\star$ transfers to a real operator. The asymmetry is a property of
this console's accounting, and the accounting is the model's, not the world's. The transferable
object is the SHAPE: which error is free, which is binding, and what sets the trade.

**Added to CR-59 after a four-block local pilot, and registered before the full battery.** The pilot
put served per step at sixteen assets at 0.1949 for $b = 1$, 0.2186 at $b = 2.5$, 0.1700 at $b = 4$
and 0.0116 at $b = 12$, and at four assets at 0.0873, 0.0747, 0.0409 and 0.0022 for the same ladder.
That is F1 and F2 in the same table at pilot power, so the full battery is confirmatory for those
two and the registered content moves to what the pilot cannot settle.

- **F6. THE ACCURACY PARADOX, STATED AS A MEASUREMENT.** An estimator's accuracy in its own units is
  not monotone in the mission quantity it feeds. The console holding the most accurate possible
  estimate, the realization itself, is beaten at sixteen assets by a console whose estimate is wrong
  by a factor of 2.5 on every single job, by more than the gate's rate floor of 0.0090. FALSIFIER:
  the full-tier gap is inside the floor. This is the sentence the battery exists for, and it is the
  same genus as CR-57 D1: a component-level metric that a programme would naturally write into a
  specification is not monotone in the system-level metric the mission is scored on.
- **F7. THE MECHANISM IS LENGTH SELECTION, AND THE CR-56 W1 INSTRUMENT READS IT.** A feasibility
  test with an inflated estimate is a shortest-job filter wearing a deadline's clothes, so
  `svc_serviced_mean` must fall monotonically with $b$ over the rising region while `busy_total`
  stays equivalent: the console is not working more, it is working shorter. FALSIFIER: served per
  step rises while `svc_serviced_mean` is flat to within its own noise, which would put the gain
  somewhere other than job selection and would break the instrument's claim to read exactly that.
- **F8. TIGHTER IS NOT THE SAME AS TIGHTER-AND-SELECTIVE.** `admit_refuse` at its own best fraction,
  which is CR-56's information-free null, must not reach the best $b$. Registered: it recovers under
  a third of the bias gain. FALSIFIER: it reaches two thirds or more, in which case the gain is an
  admission-rate artefact and F6 is a restatement of B6 rather than a new result.

**The dual reading, which is why the knob is worth having in the model.** $b$ is an estimator's
uncorrectable calibration offset when the console does not know it is there, and it is the console's
own commanded safety factor when it does. The two readings share every number and separate only in
what a programme would do about them: procure a different estimator, or write a different rule.

## CR-60. THE DISSOCIATION HAS A DIRECTION, AND IT RESCUES THE METRIC INSTEAD OF DISCARDING IT

Registered with its pilot disclosed. CR-57 D1 established that station-keeping error does not predict
mission outcome and left it there, as a bare null. A bare null is the weakest thing an experiment can
return, and I let it stand for two rounds without asking the obvious follow-up: WHICH PART of the
error is the mission blind to.

Station error is $\|\mathbf{p}_i - \mathbf{s}_i\|$, a quadrature sum of two displacements that mean
entirely different things. Resolved onto the reference's heading, the along-track component says the
vehicle is LATE on a route that is still the route. The normal component says it has LEFT it. A
scalar norm adds them and a programme reading the scalar cannot tell which its autonomy stack is
producing. `station_along_mean` and `station_cross_mean` are added at the same accumulation site as
`station_err_mean` and cost one dot product each. `fleet_spread_mean` is added in the sim loop as an
independent check with no commanded geometry in it at all: the RMS distance of the living blue
assault force from its own centroid.

**The pilot, 24 replications, supervisory star follow at eight assets, disclosed before the battery.**

    arm        st_err    along    cross    fleet    succ
    base         13.1     11.4      3.0     92.3   0.958
    out80       212.2    204.4     22.9    120.0   0.917
    out320      222.3    194.0     53.8    124.3   0.833
    sp60         18.0     14.3      6.3    132.7   0.917
    sp300       191.2     97.8    145.3    565.3   0.667
    sp600       816.1    315.4    724.4   1103.1   0.375

Station error inverts across the two families exactly as CR-57 said: out320 carries 222 m and scores
0.833, sp300 carries 191 m and scores 0.667. The cross-track component does not invert anywhere.

- **J1. Cross-track error orders mission outcome and the scalar does not.** Registered: across the
  pooled six-arm set at full tier, the cross ordering has ZERO inversions against success, and the
  scalar has at least one. FALSIFIER: any inversion in the cross ordering, or none in the scalar's.
- **J2. The ordering is not the gap in disguise.** Within the outage family the commanded gap is
  FIXED, so any correlation there cannot be the commanded geometry. Registered: cross rises
  monotonically with outage length at fixed gap while success falls, at full tier. FALSIFIER: cross
  flat within the family, in which case J1 is a re-reading of the spread knob and nothing more.
- **J3. THE MECHANISM, AND THE ONE THAT CAN FAIL WHILE J1 HOLDS.** Cross-track error costs mission
  outcome because it takes vehicles out of a survivable corridor. Remove the corridor and the
  penalty must shrink. Registered: the success cost per 100 m of cross-track error at least halves
  from the banded terrain to open terrain. FALSIFIER: it does not fall, in which case cross-track
  error is predictive here and the corridor explanation is not established, and only the correlation
  may be written.
- **J4. The constructive claim, stated as an acceptance rule.** A programme that accepts an autonomy
  stack at station error under 250 m accepts sp300 at 0.667 and cannot distinguish it from out320 at
  0.833. Registered: some cross-track threshold separates every arm above 0.83 from every arm below
  it, at full tier, across both families. FALSIFIER: no single threshold does.
- **J5. The independent instrument.** `fleet_spread_mean` has no commanded geometry in it and is
  cheaper to instrument on a real range than a resolved error. If it orders as cleanly as cross
  does, it is the better recommendation; the pilot already shows it inverting once (out320 at 124.3
  scoring 0.833 against sp60 at 132.7 scoring 0.917), so the registered expectation is that cross
  wins and dispersion is reported as the runner-up.

**What this changes about CR-57.** D1 stays true and stops being the finding. The finding becomes
that the tracking metric a programme would naturally specify is a quadrature sum over a direction the
mission is not indifferent to, and that the fix is a projection rather than a new sensor.

## CR-59 AND CR-60 AMENDED ON REVIEW: THE DEFENCE WAS A REBRAND AND THE PROSE WAS WRONG

Round 28. Both amendments are the reviewer's and both are conceded in full.

**CR-59. My defence of F6 does not survive.** I wrote that the console "does not know it is doing
shortest-job-first, it believes it is doing a feasibility test." What a console believes is not a
measurable quantity, and a claim that rests on it is a rebrand. The attack that actually kills the
sentence is not Conway 1967 at all, it is the operating point: at $\sigma = 0$ the console's ranking
of jobs is EXACT, so a multiplicative factor on a threshold rule slides the operating point along a
perfect ROC curve and destroys no information whatsoever. "The most accurate estimator is beaten"
then reads as "a default threshold is beaten by a re-tuned threshold," which is one line from any
referee and correctly so.

What survives is better than what I registered. **$b$ is the omitted shadow price of a saturated
console.** The feasibility test `start + svc + lat <= deadline` prices the slot at zero: it asks
whether this job fits and never asks what the slot is worth to the next job. At saturation the slot
has an opportunity cost, and $b$ is a price knob wearing an error costume. The pilot already carries
the signature: the ladder is ASYMMETRIC about $b = 1$, with optimism at $b = 0.6$ costing 0.0808 and
matched pessimism at $b = 1.6$ paying 0.0237, which is the free-refusal against paid-overrun
asymmetry showing up as a price; and the four-asset column peaks at $b = 1$, which is a shadow price
of zero at an idle server. Registered additions, all falsifiable:

- **F9.** $b^\star(n)$ crosses 1 at the collapse span, the same $n^\star$ the queue law gives.
  FALSIFIER: it crosses somewhere else.
- **F10.** At four assets the ladder is FLAT below $b = 1$ rather than falling, because an idle
  server loses nothing by admitting a long job. FALSIFIER: a gated fall.
- **F11.** At $cv = 0$ the ladder degenerates to a step: every job is the same length, so the price
  knob either admits all or refuses all. FALSIFIER: an interior optimum at $cv = 0$.

**THE ONE CONTROL, and F6 is held out of the manuscript until it lands.** The honest rule-repair is a
console that admits only if the job is BOTH feasible and shorter than a cap, using an unbiased
estimate: it is wrong about nothing and has simply been told a slot has a price. `admit_cap`
implements exactly that. If the capped console at its best cap recovers the inflated console's
0.2186, F6 dies as worded and becomes a rule-repair sentence, which is a smaller but honest finding.
If it does not, the residue is a real coupling between the cap and the deadline slack and that is its
own result. Without the arm, "you beat a strawman rule" ends the discussion, so nothing about F6
reaches the manuscript before it runs.

**The T&E sentence that is not a rebrand**, and it comes off the $b \times \sigma$ ridge rather than
the $b$ ladder alone: an accuracy specification conflates DISCRIMINATION, which is expensive and
monotone in what it buys, with CALIBRATION, which given a fixed decision rule is a free constant and
is NOT monotone. The right report is the frontier value at the best $b$ for each $\sigma$.
**Rider, which must sit beside any version of F6:** $b = 2.5$ starves long jobs by design. Throughput
bought this way is bought from a subpopulation, and CR-54's own fairness column is the place to say
so.

**CR-60. Two corrections, one of which is a prose error I would have shipped.**

First, the reference-frame blindness is DEFINITIONAL. Station error is measured against the commanded
station, so it cannot see the commanded geometry; that needs no arms and must be labelled as an
identity rather than a result. What the arms establish is that the blindness BITES, and specifically
that it orders the two failure modes BACKWARD: 172.6 m at 0.4341 against 215.7 m at 0.8643.

Second, my sentence that the spread arm is "holding its slot well" is simply wrong. 172.6 m of error
against a 300 m gap is 57 percent of the commanded spacing, so that arm has BOTH failure modes at
once. The exhibit is not that one arm tracks well and the other badly, it is that the scalar cannot
RANK them.

**The genus has a name and this is its third instance: REFERENCE SET BY TREATMENT.** Operator goodput
is referenced to the asking, which the policy changes. Station error is referenced to the commanded
station, which the spread knob moves. Mean backlog is referenced to the queue, which the discipline
reshapes. In all three the treatment moves the yardstick.

**The mediator panel, pre-registered, four candidates and a constructed pair.** RMS dispersion has a
known failure mode: it grows with extent even when every vehicle still has a neighbour inside weapon
range. So the panel is `fleet_spread_mean` (RMS), its exact decomposition into `fleet_along_mean` and
`fleet_cross_mean` on the force's own mean heading, `support_frac` (the share of the force with
another blue inside blue weapon range), and mission duration. `station_cross_mean` sits beside them
as the treatment-referenced comparator.

- **J6.** Exactly one construct orders ALL families monotonically against success, and the
  constructed equal-RMS pair separates it from RMS. FALSIFIER: none does, or more than one
  independent construct does, in which case the audit rule is published WITHOUT naming a replacement
  invariant, which is the reviewer's stated fallback and is accepted in advance.
- **J7, the reviewer's boundary law tested on new instruments.** Internal metrics whose reference is
  set by the treatment are ceteris-paribus only; metrics referenced to something the treatment does
  not set will travel. `fleet_cross_mean` is referenced to the force's own centroid and heading,
  which no knob in this battery commands. Registered prediction: it travels and
  `station_err_mean` does not. FALSIFIER: it inverts anywhere the scalar does not.

**Pilot for the amended panel, 24 replications, disclosed before the battery.**

    arm        st_err st_cross    fleet  f_along  f_cross  support     succ
    base         13.1      3.0     92.3     91.9      3.8    1.000    0.958
    out80       212.2     22.9    120.0    117.5     10.3    1.000    0.917
    out320      222.3     53.8    124.3    121.6     12.3    1.000    0.833
    sp60         18.0      6.3    132.7    131.7      7.8    1.000    0.917
    sp300       191.2    145.3    565.3    549.2     74.9    0.988    0.667
    sp600       816.1    724.4   1103.1   1050.6    181.5    0.929    0.375

Two constructs order all six without inversion and both are cross-track components; both quadrature
sums and both along-track components invert once or twice; `support_frac` is saturated at 1.000 for
four of the six arms and cannot rank the outage family at all. That is one construct measured two
ways rather than two constructs, which is why the full battery has to add the constructed pair
before J6 can be called either way.

## CR-59 F6 IS RETRACTED ON ITS REGISTERED CONTROL, AND WHAT IS LEFT IS BETTER

The control was registered this round and its verdict is a retraction, so it is recorded before the
full battery lands rather than after. 32 replications at sixteen assets, cv = 0.5, exact estimator:

    arm       served/step   svc_serviced   refused
    bias2.5      0.2180         4.181       0.5382
    bias3.2      0.2034         3.766       0.6080
    cap7         0.2167         4.366       0.4814
    cap8         0.2096         4.654       0.4691
    cap10        0.1989         4.952       0.4610

The honest capped console, which is wrong about nothing and has merely been told a slot has a price,
reaches 0.2167 against the inflated console's 0.2180. The gap is 0.0013 against a rate floor of
0.0090. **F6 as worded is dead**: "the most accurate estimator is beaten" is exactly the re-tuned
threshold the reviewer said it was, and the accuracy paradox comes out. The battery `capped`
(9449936) runs it at full tier to confirm rather than to rescue.

**What survives, and it is a better result than the one that died.**

1. **The feasibility test is the wrong rule at saturation, and this is now shown twice over.** It
   asks whether a job fits and never asks what the slot is worth. TWO mechanically unrelated repairs,
   inflating the estimate and capping the job, land within 0.0013 of each other and both beat the
   unrepaired rule by about 0.022, which is eleven percent of throughput. Two different knobs
   arriving at the same value is the evidence that they are one repair.
2. **The honest repair is STRICTLY better at matched throughput.** At essentially equal delivery the
   capped console refuses 0.481 of requests and the inflated one refuses 0.538. The miscalibrated
   console buys the same throughput by turning away eleven percent more of the force. That is a
   clean recommendation with no paradox in it and no estimator specification attached.
3. **The T&E sentence survives untouched because it lives on the other axis.** An accuracy
   specification conflates DISCRIMINATION, which is expensive and monotone in what it buys, with
   CALIBRATION, which given a fixed decision rule is a free constant and is not monotone. That
   claim is measured on the $b \times \sigma$ ridge in 9449800, not on the $b$ ladder, and nothing
   about the retraction touches it.
4. F9, F10 and F11 stand as registered. They test whether $b$ behaves like a shadow price, and a
   shadow price is what BOTH repairs are implementing.

## CR-61. THE OPERATOR CAN NOW BE WRONG, AND D7 IS COMPUTABLE

Registered with its pilots disclosed. Every console in this paper so far has been an ORACLE: its
answers are never wrong, only late, lost or refused. That is a real limitation and it has been
carried unlabelled through eleven batteries. It also makes one of the project's own recorded
literature gaps uncomputable. `doc/lit-ideas.md` D7 states that no study computes a discrimination
score for operator trust against agent ground-truth correctness at formation scale under degraded
comms, and I11 states that what this project calls trust is properly *trust resolution* in the sense
of Lee and See. Neither can be measured against a console that cannot be wrong.

**The mechanism, four knobs and a confusion matrix.** `auto_error` $q$ is the probability the
autonomy's proposed answer is wrong. `check_rate` $r$ is the base rate at which an uninformed
operator checks rather than shipping it. The operator's decision is a
textbook signal-detection one and is written that way deliberately, because the claim it feeds is a
discrimination score and a score computed off anything else is not comparable to the literature it
answers. The operator observes a cue drawn from a unit normal shifted by `check_dprime` when the
answer is wrong, and checks when the cue clears a criterion named by the false-alarm rate it would
produce at zero sensitivity, which is what `check_rate` means. An earlier version had the cued
operator check EXACTLY the wrong ones, which puts it on a chord between two ROC corners rather than
on an ROC, conflates sensitivity with the decision rule, and would not survive a referee who works
in signal detection. It was replaced before its battery ran. `comply_frac` is what
shipping costs as a fraction of checking, default a quarter, and it is the whole reason the choice is
interesting: at a saturated console a check is not free, it is a slot. The four cells are counted in
the operator's own decision units, which is what makes $d'$ a property of the operator rather than of
the scenario, and both console models carry it. `bad_answer_hold` is what a wrong answer costs the
vehicle that acted on it, as a multiple of the patience an unanswered vehicle waits: 1.0 is the
weakest available assumption and is the default. At $(q, r) = (0, 1)$ nothing is drawn and the console
is exactly the one every earlier battery measured.

**Pilot 1, 24 replications, sixteen assets, $q = 0.3$, disclosed.**

    arm                      served  correct   dprime    check
    always check             0.1152   1.0000    0.000    1.000
    check half, no cue       0.1834   0.8400    0.030    0.496
    never check              0.3220   0.7045    0.000    0.000
    check half, cue 0.5      0.2271   0.9252    1.361    0.403
    check half, cue 1.0      0.2913   1.0000    5.645    0.306

**Pilot 2, the span dependence, same $q$.** At six assets the check-rate ladder runs 0.1042, 0.1095
and 0.1054 served per step with mission success identical at 0.625 throughout. At sixteen it runs
0.1152, 0.1834 and 0.3220 with success 0.958, 1.000 and 1.000.

- **P1. Checking is free at an idle console.** At four and six assets the ladder is flat in served
  per step and in success. FALSIFIER: a gated effect at either span.
- **P2. Checking is expensive at a saturated one, and at the weakest assumption about what a wrong
  answer costs, it is not worth its price.** At sixteen assets served per step is monotone
  decreasing in $r$ and success is non-increasing. FALSIFIER: success rising with $r$ at
  `bad_answer_hold` 1.0.
- **P3. THE REQUIREMENTS NUMBER.** There is a break-even wrong-answer cost $h^\star$ above which
  checking pays at sixteen assets. Registered: $h^\star$ is finite and lies in $[1, 8]$. FALSIFIER:
  no value in the sweep makes always-check beat never-check, in which case over-trust is never
  punished in this model, the knob cannot change a mission outcome, and by the project's own test it
  is not a design variable and CR-61 is withdrawn.
- **P0, THE INSTRUMENT CHECK, WHICH IS NOT A RESULT.** The measured $d'$ must recover
  `check_dprime`, and the measured false-alarm rate must equal `check_rate` and not move with
  sensitivity. Verified at 32 replications: set 0.0, 0.5, 1.0 and 2.0 recover $-0.026$, 0.523, 1.029
  and 2.006 at false-alarm rates 0.502, 0.495, 0.493 and 0.489 against a criterion set at 0.5. At
  `check_dprime` 3.0 the hit rate saturates at 0.997 and the log-linear correction compresses the
  estimate to 2.559, which is the standard ceiling; sensitivities above 2 are therefore not quoted
  as point estimates.
- **P4. D7, AND THE REASON IT NEEDS A DISCRIMINATION SCORE RATHER THAN A RATE.** Sensitivity and
  checking rate are separately purchasable and only one of them is expensive. At a fixed criterion,
  raising $d'$ from 0 to 2 leaves served per step flat, 0.1804 to 0.1833 across the ladder, while
  the hit rate runs 0.492 to 0.976 and the false-alarm rate does not move at all. Registered:
  throughput is equivalent across the sensitivity ladder while correctness gates up, so sensitivity
  is free in throughput and checking rate is not. FALSIFIER: `check_frac` orders the arms as well as
  $d'$ does, which would make the extra instrument unnecessary and would be worth saying.
- **P5. THE ONE THAT MAKES THIS THE CENTREPIECE AND NOT A SIDE RESULT.** $h^\star$ moves with the
  comms ladder, through the console's load and not through anything about the autonomy. Under a
  degraded link fewer requests arrive per step, the console is less saturated, a slot is cheaper,
  so checking is cheaper and $h^\star$ FALLS. Registered: $h^\star$ at C4 is at most half $h^\star$
  at C0. FALSIFIER: flat across the ladder, or moving the other way.

**Why P5 is worth the battery.** It says the correct level of trust in an autonomous subordinate is
not a property of the autonomy. It is set by how loaded the operator is, which is set by the jamming.
That is a claim about the interaction of three things this project was funded to cross and that
`doc/lit-ideas.md` records as uncrossed anywhere in the literature: a graded EW ladder, a graded
control modality, and trust calibration at formation scale.

**What is NOT claimed.** That any of these numbers describe a human. `comply_frac` is a modelling
constant and $q$ is a stipulation. The transferable object is the SHAPE: that the price of checking
is the price of a slot, that the slot's price is set by load, and that load is set by the channel.

## CR-58 E1 VERDICT: THE BRIDGE IS RETRACTED ON ITS REGISTERED WINDOW, AND THE ARITHMETIC FAILED FOR A REASON THIS PAPER HAS A NAME FOR

256 blocks of 64 replications, eight times the standard tier, job 9449639.

    contrast                      effect       t   blocks agreeing   verdict
    cut - nocut, GE-60           -0.0013   -0.63       104/256       equivalent (one-sided 25.48)
    cut - nocut, GE-240          -0.0009   -0.62       101/256       equivalent (one-sided 35.62)

The registered window was $[-0.021, -0.007]$ about a predicted $-0.014$. The measurement is
$-0.0013$, outside it by an order of magnitude. **E1's falsifier is met and the bridge is retracted.**
The deterministic sawtooth does not predict the stochastic channel, and CR-57's effect is reported as
a property of its own stimulus, which is what the registration said would follow.

**E3, the control, passes perfectly and that is what makes the retraction interpretable.** Under the
Bernoulli ladder the two arms are BIT-IDENTICAL at both rungs, 0.0000 with zero of 256 blocks
differing. The cut-off fires on track age and on nothing else. And under Gilbert-Elliot it DOES fire:
`follow_lost_frac` gates up to 0.0841 at mean bad run 60 and 0.0985 at 240, in 256 of 256 blocks. So
the mechanism is live, it reverts a tenth of the fleet's follower-time, and it changes the mission by
nothing. The retraction is of the PREDICTION, not of the instrument.

**Why the arithmetic missed, and it is instrument defect 11 of the same genus as the other ten.** The
bridge computed the deep-stale fraction as $0.55 \times e^{-40/60} = 0.282$. The 0.55 is the drop rate
at **C5**. The battery ran at **C3**, where this model's stale fraction is 0.1999. Carrying a rate
from the rung it was derived on to a rung where it does not hold inflated the deep fraction by 3.4
times. With the measured deep fraction of 0.0841 the prediction becomes $0.051 \times 0.0841 =
0.0043$, which is BELOW the materiality floor of 0.06 and therefore was never a testable prediction
at all. The residual factor of three between 0.0043 and 0.0013 is inside the uncertainty of a cost
rate read off a single point.

**Defect 11: a rate carried from the rung it was derived on to a rung where it does not hold.** It is
the same shape as the other ten, and it is the same shape as the paper's own result: a quantity
correctly derived against one reference, then used against another. The bridge was an attempt to
audit an instrument, and it was itself defeated by a reference error. That is worth one sentence in
the discussion and no more, because a paper that congratulates itself on its own failure modes is
not reporting them.

**What the retraction costs and what it does not.** The manuscript's sentence about the cut-off is
unchanged and is now supported at eight times the power: it is inert under every channel this study
models, at a one-sided equivalence statistic of 25.48 under the burstiest channel available, and
decisive only against a severed link. What comes out is any claim that the forced-staleness sweep
FORECASTS a channel. It does not, and no sentence in the manuscript should say it does.

## CR-56 A3 AND B4 VERDICT: THE COUNTER-ONLY RUNG IS MEASURED, AND THE COUNTER CHANGES SIGN

Full tier, 32 blocks of 64, job 9449506. The rung the requirements ladder recommended had never been
measured against triage, because every earlier bounded arm carried a deadline test as well.

    arm                            served/step at sixteen assets
    deadline field alone                   0.1185
    counter alone                          0.1402
    counter, foresight switched on         0.1402   (identical to the digit)
    counter plus deadline field            0.1413
    counter plus triage                    0.1545
    triage alone                           0.1949

- **B3 held, exactly.** `bound` and `boundf` are equal in every cell and `policy_admit` reads 0 for
  both, so `admit_foresight` is bit-inert on a counter-only console. The construction argument the
  published caveat rested on is now a measurement: the gap is 0.0000 in every block, not merely
  small. Defect 11 did not fire here.
- **B4 held, and hard.** Adding the counter to a triaging console costs 0.0404 served per step
  ($t = 234$, 32/32). Adding it to a console that cannot triage gains 0.0217. **The counter changes
  sign.** It is not a weaker substitute for discernment; on a console that already has discernment
  it is a net harm, because a console holding $k^\star$ requests has almost nothing left to choose
  between and the counter throws away the pool the selection was going to be made from.
- The manuscript's caveat is replaced by the measurement rather than kept beside it.

**The recommendation this changes.** A program office told that a backlog bound and operator triage
both help would naturally buy both. In this model buying both is worse than buying either: the pair
delivers 0.1545 where triage alone delivers 0.1949. The ladder's ordering stands, but the rungs are
alternatives and not increments, and that sentence has to be in the guidance rather than in a
footnote.

## CR-61 AMENDED BEFORE ITS BATTERY: THE COST OF SCRUTINY IS NOT THE SCRUTINY

Round 29's review and three pilots between them took the registration apart. Everything below
replaces P1 through P5, and the pilots are disclosed.

**First, what the reviewer was right about and I was not.** The `bad_answer_hold` sweep cannot reach
the failure mode it was built for. That knob prices wrongness in the requester's TIME, and the harm
a programme worried about over-trust holds is structurally different at any value of it: it is
absorbing rather than transient, it falls partly on the neighbour, and it lives in the tail. No time
price ever makes a vehicle FIGHT worse. So the sweep provably cannot arrive, and sweeping harder
would have been a way of not noticing. The reviewer's repair is taken in full and adds no dial:
wrong answers may instead be DELIVERED and resolve at a degraded control quality, with two endpoints
both derived from constants this paper already prices. `weak` resolves at $q_{\text{auto}} = 0.70$,
so a wrong answer leaves the vehicle no better off than its own autonomy; `reflect` resolves at
$2q_{\text{auto}} - q_{\text{operator}} = 0.45$, as far below autonomy as a right answer is above it.
An interval cannot be fitted to a conclusion, which is why it is an interval.

**Second, the closed form I derived from the pilot is wrong, and finding out how it is wrong is the
result.** At saturation, checking delivers $1/s$ correct answers and shipping delivers $(1-q)/(cs)$,
so shipping should win exactly while $q < 1 - c$, which at $c = 0.25$ predicts a break-even autonomy
error rate of 0.75, and 0.375 under the reflecting endpoint. Measured at eight assets over 64
replications, shipping wins at EVERY error rate on both endpoints, including $q = 0.9$ under
`reflect`, where nine answers in ten actively degrade the vehicle that receives one. The closed form
is not slightly off. It is missing a term.

**The missing term, isolated.** Same configuration, $q = 0.5$, `reflect`, 64 replications:

    arm                              served/step   success   blue loss
    no operator at all                   0.0000     0.891      0.2734
    operator checks everything           0.1165     0.719      0.4062
    operator ships everything            0.2497     0.797      0.3223
    checks everything, waiting free      0.1244     0.922      0.2285
    ships everything, waiting free       0.2764     0.969      0.2051

Two things are in that table and the second explains the first. **A conscientious operator at the
collapse span is worse than no operator at all**, 0.719 against 0.891. And the whole of that deficit
is the WAITING: pricing a waiting asset at $q_{\text{auto}}$ instead of $q_{\text{stall}}$ takes the
same always-checking console from 0.719 to 0.922, above the no-operator baseline. The closed form
omitted the queue of vehicles idled behind each check. Scrutiny is not expensive because scrutiny is
expensive. It is expensive because it is a slot, and every vehicle waiting on that slot is stalled.

**What is registered now.**

- **Q1. THE CROSSING.** There is a span at which adding an operator who checks every recommendation
  makes the mission WORSE than having no operator, and it is at or below the collapse span. Pilot
  has it at eight assets. FALSIFIER: no span in the sweep where always-check falls below the
  supervisory-autonomous baseline at full tier.
- **Q2. THE DECOMPOSITION, which is the claim and not Q1.** The crossing is carried by the stall and
  not by the checking. Registered: setting $q_{\text{stall}} = q_{\text{auto}}$ removes the crossing
  at every span where Q1 finds it, and the stall term is at least twice the correctness term
  (measured 0.203 against 0.047 in the pilot). FALSIFIER: the crossing survives free waiting, or the
  two terms are within a factor of two, in which case the cost of scrutiny is genuinely the
  scrutiny and Q2 is withdrawn while Q1 stands.
- **Q3. THE REQUIREMENT, stated where a programme can act on it.** The binding parameter on a
  supervisory console is neither answer quality nor answer rate but what an asset may DO while it
  waits. Registered as a threshold: there is a value of $q_{\text{stall}}$ above which an
  always-checking operator beats no operator at eight assets, and it is strictly between
  $q_{\text{stall}}$ and $q_{\text{auto}}$. FALSIFIER: no interior threshold.
- **Q4, THE HONESTY CLAUSE, because Q2 is a comparison of two stipulated constants.** That
  $q_{\text{stall}} < q_{\text{auto}}$ is an assumption, not a finding, and a result that only said
  "waiting is costly because we priced waiting as costly" would be worthless. What is NOT stipulated
  is the ORDERING of the two mechanisms, nor where the crossing falls, nor that either exists. Those
  three are what is reported. The transferable object is the shape.
- **Q5. THE HARM CHANNELS ARE COMPARED, NOT CHOSEN.** Both quality endpoints and the time channel
  run on the same grid. Registered null: identical ordering of the design space across all three.
  Inversion cells, if any, are the over-trust exhibit and are the only place the model can say
  anything about F5 at all. FALSIFIER of the null: any inversion, which is a result rather than a
  failure.
- **Q6, replacing P3, on the reviewer's correction that corners are the wrong comparison.** The
  pilot already has check-rate 0.5 beating 1.0 at sixteen assets, so always-check against
  never-check is not the contrast. Registered instead: the OPTIMAL check rate $r^\star(u)$ as a
  function of console utilization, with the claim that it falls monotonically in $u$ and reaches
  zero only above the collapse span. FALSIFIER: $r^\star$ pinned at zero across the whole range, or
  non-monotone in $u$.
- **Q7, replacing P5, on the reviewer's correction that "jamming makes checking cheaper" reads as an
  artefact.** The claim is re-registered as SUFFICIENCY: $r^\star$ collapses onto a single curve in
  console utilization, tested by matched-utilization cells drawn from different rungs of the comms
  ladder. If it holds, rational trust is scheduled by console slack whatever sets the slack, which
  is artefact-proof and welded to the effort-invariance family. If it fails, the rung residual is a
  genuinely communications-specific effect on trust and is the more interesting of the two. Both
  branches publish; the earlier wording survived only one. Conservative assumption stated once: the
  autonomy's error rate is taken to be comms-invariant.

**Instrument defect 12, found while diagnosing the above.** `arrived_frac` is described in the
metric set as the continuous quantity `success` thresholds. It is that only below the threshold. The
run breaks as soon as enough vehicles reach the objective, so a succeeding mission records
`arrived_frac` AT `survive_fraction` and never above it: measured 0.3125, 0.5000 and 0.7500 against
thresholds of 0.3, 0.5 and 0.7. The metric separates only on the failing side, a contrast between two
succeeding arms reads zero whatever the arms did, and a contrast across the threshold is a LOWER
bound. No published claim is overturned, because every reported contrast on it is of the third kind
and is therefore understated rather than overstated; the manuscript now says so. Guarded by a test
asserting the pinning at three thresholds.

    b        n=4      n=6      n=8     n=12     n=16
    0.60  0.0849   0.1066   0.1176   0.1201   0.1137
    0.80  0.0864   0.1109   0.1293   0.1471   0.1520
    1.00  0.0873   0.1174   0.1428   0.1762   0.1949
    1.25  0.0862   0.1164   0.1457   0.1822   0.2030
    1.60  0.0847   0.1165   0.1462   0.1876   0.2111
    2.00  0.0791   0.1112   0.1425   0.1892   0.2174
    2.50  0.0711   0.1009   0.1330   0.1856   0.2193
    4.00  0.0400   0.0588   0.0829   0.1308   0.1706
    argmax  1.00     1.00     1.60     2.00     2.50

- **F1 HELD.** At sixteen assets $b = 1.6$, 2.0 and 2.5 all gate up over $b = 1$, at $+0.0161$,
  $+0.0225$ and $+0.0244$.
- **F2 HELD.** At four assets nothing beats $b = 1$: every offset is equivalent or gates down, and
  $b = 2.5$ gates DOWN at $-0.0163$. The optimum is a property of the load.
- **F9 RETRACTED on the finer grid run as its own control (job 9449936, M2 below).** On this coarse
  grid the argmax leaves $b = 1$ between six and eight assets and the first GATED improvement is at
  twelve ($+0.0115$ at $b = 1.6$), which looked like a bracket around the collapse span
  $n^\star = 6.45$. It was a grid artefact: with rungs at 1.10 and 1.25 added, the argmax leaves
  unity between FOUR and FIVE. The crossing location is grid-dependent, the gate does not resolve it
  below twelve assets, and $n^\star$ does not locate it. What survives is that $b^\star(n)$ is
  monotone non-decreasing in span on both grids.
- **F10 HELD.** At four assets the ladder is flat BELOW $b = 1$ rather than falling: $b = 0.6$ is
  $-0.0025$ and $b = 0.8$ is $-0.0009$, both equivalent. The same $b = 0.6$ gates down at $-0.0108$,
  $-0.0252$ and $-0.0812$ at six, eight and sixteen. Optimism is free at an idle server and ruinous
  at a busy one, which is the shadow price of a slot appearing on the cheap side of the ladder.
- **F11 HELD, and exactly rather than approximately.** At $cv = 0$ the ladder runs 0.1490, 0.1481,
  0.1416 and then **0.0000**, with `svc_serviced_mean` at exactly 6.6667 for the first three and
  0.0000 for the last. With every job the same length a price knob admits all or refuses all. It is
  a step.
- **F7 HELD.** `svc_serviced_mean` falls monotonically with $b$ at every span, 5.0923 to 3.3186 at
  sixteen assets. The console is not working more, it is working shorter.

**F3 and F4, PARTIAL, and the mechanism explains which half failed.** The requirements curve over
$(b, \sigma)$ at sixteen assets peaks at $b = 2.5$, 2.0, 2.5 and 3.2 for $\sigma = 0$, 0.35, 0.70 and
1.40, which is not the clean rise F3 registered; the peaks are broad and the location barely moves.
The peak VALUE falls hard: 0.2193, 0.2021, 0.1790, 0.1493. Offset therefore recovers 60 percent of
the exact console's advantage at $\sigma = 0.7$, above F4's falsifier of half but outside the
registered two thirds, so **F4 is partial**. The instrument says why: `svc_serviced_mean` under
$b$ falls 5.09 to 3.32 at $\sigma = 0$ and RISES 5.20 to 5.35 at $\sigma = 1.40$. A noisy estimator
cannot select on length at all, so no offset can buy back what spread destroys. Spread is binding
and offset is not, which is what F4 claimed; it simply compensates less than registered.

**F8 HELD EMPHATICALLY, and it is the cleanest section in the battery.** Random refusal gates DOWN
at every fraction and every span: $-0.0111$, $-0.0271$ and $-0.0524$ at sixteen assets for refusal
rates of 0.2, 0.4 and 0.6. Selective pessimism at a MATCHED refusal rate gates up $+0.0244$. Refusing
0.54 of requests selectively delivers 0.2193; refusing 0.58 of them at random delivers 0.1679. The
entire gain is in the selectivity and none of it is in the tightness.

**The requirements ladder that comes out of it, priced, at sixteen assets.**

    rung                                              served/step   against nothing
    no admission rule at all                             0.1185           .
    refuse at random, best fraction                      0.1074          gates DOWN
    budget a quantile: select on the REQUESTER's slack   0.1407          +0.0222 gated
    see this job's length: select on the JOB             0.1949          +0.0764 gated
    see it and price the slot as well                    0.2193          +0.1008 gated

**H6, and it is the finding a programme should act on rather than the throughput number.** The
offset does NOT reach mission success at full tier: $b = 1.6$ at eight assets is $+0.0288$ and
INCONCLUSIVE, and every other cell is equivalent. But $b = 4.0$ at four assets gates DOWN at
$-0.1206$. **The knob's downside reaches the mission and its upside does not.** So the honest
recommendation is asymmetric: pricing the slot is a console-level gain that this study cannot show
converting into mission outcome, while over-tightening at low span is a mission-level loss that it
can. That asymmetry is the sentence, and it is the opposite of the sentence a throughput number
alone would have produced.

## CR-59 CONTROL VERDICT: THE HONEST CONSOLE RECOVERS THE INFLATED ONE, THE PRICE TURN IS NOT WHERE I SAID, AND THE THROUGHPUT IS PAID FOR IN ABANDONMENT

Full tier, 32 blocks of 64, job 9449936, battery `capped`. This is the control I registered before
running: *if the capped console at its best cap recovers the inflated console's 0.2186, F6 dies as
worded.* It does, and three further things came out of the same battery.

**M1. F6 IS DEAD AT FULL TIER, CONFIRMED RATHER THAN RESCUED.** `cap7`, an honest console that sees
the true service time and refuses any job longer than seven steps, is TOST-equivalent to `bias250`,
a console that misjudges every job by a factor of 2.5, at all three spans: $-0.0004$, $+0.0030$ and
$-0.0034$, with one-sided statistics 11.06, 11.46 and 16.20. Two mechanically different
implementations, one a lie about the estimate and one a truthful rule about the slot, deliver the
same throughput. There is no accuracy paradox. What the offset was doing was pricing the slot, and
a cap prices it too.

The rider matters for how this is written. At matched throughput the two consoles are not
interchangeable in what they refuse: `bias250` refuses 0.5423 of requests and `cap7` 0.4822, and
`cap7` serves the LONGER jobs of the two (`svc_serviced_mean` 4.3927 against 4.1618). The honest
rule reaches the same throughput by refusing less and working longer. That is a better console to
recommend even though the metric cannot tell them apart, and it is the reason the ladder's top rung
is now written as *price the slot*, not as *be wrong about the estimate*.

**M2. THE PRICE TURN IS NOT AT THE COLLAPSE SPAN, AND THE EARLIER READING WAS A GRID ARTEFACT.** On
the finer grid ($b \in \{0.8, 0.9, 1.0, 1.1, 1.25, 1.6\}$, spans 4 through 16) the argmax runs

    n         4      5      6      7      8     10     12     16
    b*     1.00   1.10   1.10   1.25   1.60   1.60   1.60   1.60

so the argmax leaves unity between FOUR and FIVE assets, not between six and eight. The coarse H1
grid had no rung between 1.00 and 1.25 and therefore could not see it. Gated against $b = 1.00$ at
the same cells, $b = 1.10$ is equivalent everywhere ($+0.0007$ at five, $+0.0008$ at six, $+0.0037$
at sixteen); $b = 1.25$ is equivalent everywhere; $b = 1.60$ is equivalent through ten assets and
gates up only at twelve ($+0.0115$) and sixteen ($+0.0161$).

**So F9 is retracted as worded.** It predicted that $b^\star(n)$ crosses one AT the collapse span
$n^\star = 6.45$. On the argmax the crossing is at five, below $n^\star$; on the gate it is at
twelve, above it. The two readings bracket $n^\star$ between them only in the sense that any pair of
numbers on opposite sides of it does. The collapse span does not locate the price turn, and the
earlier "bracketing" sentence in the H1 verdict above is withdrawn with it.

What survives, and it is the part worth keeping: $b^\star(n)$ is monotone non-decreasing in span on
both grids, and at full tier the optimum is distinguishable from unity only at high span. **The
existence of an interior optimum is a load effect; its location is not resolvable by this
instrument.**

**M3. F11 HELD, AND THE STEP IS AT UNITY EXACTLY.** With no spread to price ($cv = 0$) the ladder
runs 0.0957 at $b = 0.8$, then 0.1490, 0.1486, 0.1484, 0.1481, 0.1468, 0.1416 for $b$ from 1.0 to
2.5: flat above unity within the rate floor of 0.0090, and a cliff below it. At $cv = 0.10$ the same
ladder is 0.1030, then 0.1518 through 0.1530: already non-degenerate, but the whole interior spans
0.0012 and there is nothing to select on. The interior optimum is a property of service-time spread
and it needs real spread to exist, which is what F11 registered.

**M4. WHO PAYS. THE THROUGHPUT IS BOUGHT WITH ABANDONMENT, AND THE EXCHANGE RATE IS THE DESIGN
VARIABLE.** Against a plain console at three spans:

    arm     n     served/step   abandoned/step   answer_rate_v   abandonments per extra job
    b160    8      +0.0034 eq      +0.0163 G       -0.0589 G          4.8
    b160   12      +0.0115 G       +0.0219 G       -0.0292 eq         1.9
    b160   16      +0.0161 G       +0.0260 G       -0.0132 eq         1.6
    b250    8      -0.0098 G       +0.0569 G       -0.2026 G          --
    b250   12      +0.0094 G       +0.0722 G       -0.1145 G          7.7
    b250   16      +0.0244 G       +0.0838 G       -0.0644 G          3.4
    cap5    8      -0.0558 G       +0.1249 G       -0.4383 G          --
    cap5   16      -0.0059 eq      +0.1711 G       -0.1682 G          --

Every abandonment column gates, at $t$ from 36 to 205. Pricing the slot is not free: it converts
waiting into abandonment, and the rate at which it does so is the number a sponsor should be shown.
A modest offset at high span buys a served job for about 1.6 to 1.9 abandoned requests; a large one
costs 3.4 to 7.7; and an over-tightened honest console (`cap5`) pays 0.17 abandonments per step for
throughput that is equivalent or worse, which is strict domination and the clearest argument in the
battery against setting the rule too hard.

The `answer_rate_v` column carries the one genuinely favourable cell. At twelve and sixteen assets
$b = 1.6$ gates UP on throughput while remaining EQUIVALENT on the per-vehicle answer rate. There is
therefore a setting at which the console serves measurably more without measurably lowering any
vehicle's chance of an answer, and the whole of its cost appears in abandonment instead. That is a
sharper recommendation than the throughput number alone and it is the one to carry into the paper.

**Instrument defect 13, found by this battery and CORRECTED two sections later: `starved_frac`
reads zero here, and I drew the wrong conclusion from that.** The correction is in the third CR-62
amendment and should be read with this paragraph: the metric is not broken, it is reporting a true
zero in a fleet whose deadlines are uniform, and the error was asking a distributional question in
the one configuration where the distribution is degenerate. What follows is the original entry,
kept because the reasoning in it is the reasoning the correction overturns.

**As originally written: `starved_frac` is saturated and cannot audit anything here.** It is defined as the fraction of vehicles that asked and received exactly zero
answers over the run. Its largest value anywhere in the battery, over four arms, three spans and 32
blocks, is 0.0001, and it is exactly 0.000000 in all but one of those cells. Over a run of this
length no vehicle is ever excluded outright, so the metric sits on its floor by construction
and a null read from it says nothing about distribution. Had M4 been read off `starved_frac` alone
it would have reported that nobody pays, which is the opposite of what `abandoned_per_step` shows at
$t = 205$. The genus is the familiar one in a new dress: **a metric whose extreme value is
structurally unreachable at the operating point cannot detect the harm it was added to detect.**
The guard and the distributional replacements are recorded with the defect.

## CR-62. THE AGGREGATE CANNOT SEE WHO PAYS: IS A THROUGHPUT RULE REGRESSIVE?

**Registered before the battery. Job to follow.**

M4 established that pricing the slot buys throughput with abandonment at a rate of about 1.6 to 7.7
abandoned requests per extra decision delivered. It could not say WHO abandons, because the only
distributional metric in the model was saturated (defect 13). With `answer_rate_min` and
`answer_rate_gap` added, the question is answerable, and it is the question a sponsor asks second:
*a rule that raises the fleet's throughput, does it raise everyone's chance of an answer, or does it
take from some vehicles to give to others?*

The mechanism the model actually contains, checked in the code rather than assumed. Latency is a
scalar and identical for every vehicle, so link quality cannot be the sorting key. Two things can:

1. **The deadline.** The admission test is `start + b*svc + lat <= deadline`, and `deadline` comes
   from `patience_v`, a per-vehicle value. `patience_spread` disperses it about an exactly fixed
   mean, so it is a pure second-moment manipulation. Raising $b$ multiplies the service term, which
   shrinks the slack `deadline - lat - b*svc` fastest for the vehicles with the smallest deadline.
   The offset should therefore sort refusals onto the impatient vehicles, and it should do so more
   as $b$ rises. **This is a static key: the same vehicles are disadvantaged all run.**
2. **The retry loop.** Under `admit_response = "aimd"` a refusal halves a vehicle's request rate and
   each answer adds back an eighth, so eight good answers undo one refusal. A refused vehicle then
   asks less often, is answered less often, and stays slow. **This is a feedback key, and it needs
   no per-vehicle parameter at all.**

The point of registering both is that they predict the same aggregate and different distributions,
which is exactly the situation in which an aggregate metric is the wrong instrument.

**G1 (the null that has to hold first).** In a UNIFORM fleet with AIMD off, `answer_rate_gap` does
not grow with $b$. With identical deadlines the admission test has no per-vehicle key, so which
vehicle is refused is queue-position luck and is not persistent.
*Falsifier: the gap gates UP with $b$ in the uniform non-AIMD fleet. If that fires the mechanism
story is wrong and any concentration found later is an artefact of something I have not identified.*

**G2 (the static key).** With `patience_spread` large, `answer_rate_gap` gates UP with $b$, and
`answer_rate_min` falls by strictly more than `answer_rate_v` does.
*Falsifier: the gap is equivalent across $b \in \{1.0, 1.6, 2.5\}$ in the dispersed fleet, or the
min falls by no more than the mean.*

**G3 (the feedback key is stronger).** With AIMD on, the gap grows MORE with $b$ than the dispersed
fleet's does, because a feedback loop compounds and a static key does not.
*Falsifier: AIMD's gap growth is equivalent to or smaller than the dispersed fleet's. If it fires,
the model's own congestion-control response is not a concentrator and CR-26 needs the note.*

**G4 (the one that matters, and the reason to run this at all).** At matched $b$, the uniform and
dispersed fleets are EQUIVALENT on `served_per_step` and on `answer_rate_v`, while separating on
`answer_rate_gap`. An operator console reporting throughput and mean answer rate would show no
difference between a fleet the rule treats evenly and one it does not.
*Falsifier: `served_per_step` gates between the two fleets at matched $b$. That would mean
`patience_spread` is not second-moment-only at this operating point, and G4 is not a statement about
blindness but about a confound.*

**G5 (does it reach the mission).** If the concentration matters, mission success under a large $b$
should be worse in the dispersed fleet than in the uniform one. If it does not, the concentration is
a console-level fact and gets written as one, exactly as the throughput gain was.
*Falsifier: success is equivalent between fleets at every $b$. Registered as the expected outcome
given H6, and reported either way.*

**What the answer is worth.** If G1, G2 and G4 hold, the finding is that a throughput rule with no
per-vehicle term in it is nonetheless regressive, that it is regressive through the deadline rather
than through anything the rule mentions, and that the two aggregate metrics a console would display
are both blind to it. That is a design requirement (report the minimum, not the mean) and it is the
kind of statement the reference-set-by-treatment genus keeps producing: the instrument a treatment
is judged by has to be chosen so the treatment cannot hide in it.

### CR-62 AMENDED ON A FOUR-BLOCK PILOT, BEFORE THE BATTERY: I HAD THE SIGN OF THE MECHANISM WRONG

A four-block pilot at sixteen assets, run to check the arms would launch, contradicts the mechanism
I registered above. It is recorded here rather than folded silently into the battery, and G1 to G5
stand as written and will be adjudicated against these numbers.

    arm         answer_rate_v   answer_rate_min   answer_rate_gap   served_per_step
    uni100         0.5177           0.3265            0.1912            0.1967
    uni250         0.4573           0.2992            0.1580            0.2189
    disp100        0.4738           0.0098            0.4639            0.1695
    disp250        0.4249           0.0140            0.4109            0.1874

**G2 is going to fire, and in the interesting direction.** I predicted the offset would concentrate
harm because it shrinks the slack `deadline - lat - b*svc` fastest for the smallest deadline. The
gap does not rise with the offset. It FALLS, in both fleets (0.1912 to 0.1580, 0.4639 to 0.4109),
and in the dispersed fleet the worst-served vehicle is absolutely better off under the aggressive
offset than under none (0.0098 to 0.0140) while the fleet mean falls (0.4738 to 0.4249). **Pricing
the slot is progressive, not regressive.**

The mechanism I registered was wrong about what the rule sorts on. The offset multiplies the
SERVICE term, so it refuses long jobs whoever asked for them; it has no deadline term in it at all.
The vehicle that benefits from a shorter queue is the one with the least slack to spend waiting in
it. So the offset does not sort refusals onto the impatient; it removes the long jobs the impatient
were stuck behind.

**G4's premise also fails, and that is a finding rather than a nuisance.** `served_per_step` is not
equivalent between the fleets: 0.1967 uniform against 0.1695 dispersed at $b = 1$, a fourteen
percent loss from dispersing deadlines about an exactly fixed mean. Throughput is concave in the
deadline, so a mixed fleet is worse than a uniform one with the same average patience. G4 as
registered claimed the aggregate is blind to the fleet difference; it is not blind to it. What the
aggregate IS blind to is the size of the harm: at `disp100` the fleet mean answer rate is 0.4738
while one vehicle is answered 0.0098 of the time, a factor of forty eight. That is the instrument
argument and it does not depend on the offset at all.

Three predictions added, registered now, tested by the same battery at full tier.

**G6.** In a dispersed fleet `answer_rate_min` rises with $b$ while `answer_rate_v` falls. The
console gives up mean answer rate and buys the worst-served vehicle a better one.
*Falsifier: the min falls with $b$, or its rise does not gate.*

**G7 (the mechanism, and the control that separates it from tightness).** The rise in the minimum
comes from LENGTH SELECTION shortening the queue, not from refusal as such. So `admit_cap`, which
selects on length with no offset anywhere in it, should reproduce the rise, and `admit_refuse` at a
matched refusal rate, which shortens the queue without selecting, should not.
*Falsifier: random refusal raises the minimum as much as length selection does. That would mean any
refusal helps the worst-served vehicle, the finding is about tightness rather than selectivity, and
it is the opposite of what F8 established for throughput.*

**G8.** `served_per_step` falls monotonically along the `patience_spread` ladder at a fixed mean.
*Falsifier: equivalent across the ladder, which would make the fourteen percent a four-block
artefact.*

**Why the amendment is worth more than the original.** If G6 and G7 hold, the sentence a programme
gets is not "the throughput rule takes from the weak" but its opposite: *the rule that raises
console throughput also raises the floor, and the metric that would have told you so is not the one
a console displays.* Both halves are actionable and the second is the reusable one.

### CR-62 AMENDED A SECOND TIME: THE MIN AND THE QUARTILE DISAGREE, AND THE INTERESTING ARM IS THE CAP

Twelve blocks of eight, dispersed fleet (`patience_spread` 12 about a fixed mean of 20), sixteen
assets. I ran this to test my own worry that the minimum is an order statistic over sixteen vehicles
and might be moving for reasons unrelated to the mechanism. It is, and the worry was right.

    arm      mean    min      p25      stratification: answer rate by the vehicle's own patience
    b100    0.4704  0.0076   0.1311    p8:0.013  p10:0.049  p13:0.132 ... p30:0.930  p32:0.967
    b250    0.4228  0.0160   0.1181    p8:0.027  p10:0.062  p13:0.109 ... p30:0.850  p32:0.880
    cap7    0.4716  0.1203   0.3699    p8:0.132  p10:0.281  p13:0.359 ... p30:0.598  p32:0.606
    rand60  0.2934  0.0688   0.1985    p8:0.092  p10:0.124  p13:0.166 ... p30:0.397  p32:0.396

**The offset's "progressive" reading does not survive the quartile.** Going from `b100` to `b250`
the minimum RISES (0.0076 to 0.0160) and the twenty-fifth percentile FALLS (0.1311 to 0.1181). One
order statistic says the rule helps the worst-off and the next one up says it does not. G6 as
registered is therefore going to be adjudicated on a quantity I no longer think should carry the
claim, and the honest verdict when the battery lands will be that the offset does not have a
distributional story worth telling.

**What the profile shows instead is far more interesting, and it was invisible until the answer rate
was keyed on the vehicle's own deadline.** A plain console serves the patient and abandons the
impatient: at `b100` the shortest-deadline vehicle is answered 1.3 percent of the time and the
longest 96.7 percent, a stratification of seventy-four to one, produced entirely by dispersing
deadlines about an exactly fixed mean. Nothing in the console's rule mentions the deadline. The
stratification is emergent: an impatient vehicle times out while waiting behind work the console
accepted, so the console's queue silently sorts the fleet by who can afford to wait.

**And the admission rule is the instrument that controls it, with one arm getting it for free.**
`cap7` cuts the stratification from seventy-four to one down to 4.6 to one, raising the least
patient vehicle's answer rate by a factor of ten, AT AN UNCHANGED FLEET MEAN (0.4716 against
0.4704). `rand60` flattens comparably (4.3 to one) and pays 38 percent of the mean to do it. The
offset barely flattens and pays. So the selectivity-against-tightness result that F8 established for
throughput reappears in the distribution, and it is the same console that wins both times.

**This supersedes G6 and sharpens G7.** The registered falsifier for G7 was that random refusal
raises the minimum as much as length selection does. It does raise the minimum, so read literally
G7 fires; but it raises it by levelling down, and the comparison the falsifier should have named is
at a MATCHED MEAN rather than at a matched refusal rate. Recorded as a defect in my own falsifier
rather than repaired quietly: **G7 is superseded by G9, and G7's verdict will still be reported.**

**G9.** At a matched fleet-mean answer rate, length selection compresses the answer-rate spread
across the fleet and tightness does not. Measured as `answer_rate_iqr`, `cap7` gates DOWN against
`b100` while `answer_rate_v` between them is equivalent; `rand60` reaches a comparable IQR only by
gating DOWN on the mean as well.
*Falsifier: `cap7`'s IQR is equivalent to `b100`'s, or its mean is not equivalent, in which case
there is no free compression and the fairness claim collapses into the throughput one.*

**G10.** The stratification exists at all only because deadlines differ. In a uniform fleet the IQR
is small and no admission rule moves it much.
*Falsifier: the uniform fleet shows a comparable IQR, which would mean the spread is queue luck
rather than deadline sorting and the whole mechanism is misattributed.*

**Instrument note, and the reason for the second amendment.** `answer_rate_min` was added one
amendment ago to replace a saturated metric and is itself the wrong instrument: a single order
statistic over sixteen vehicles is noisy, and here it moves opposite to the quartile immediately
below it. `answer_rate_p25`, `answer_rate_p75` and `answer_rate_iqr` are added and are what the
claims are registered against. This is instrument defect 13 recurring one level up, which is worth
saying plainly: **replacing a bad metric is not the same as choosing a good one, and the replacement
needs its own falsifier.**

### CR-62 THIRD AMENDMENT: DEFECT 13 WAS MY ERROR, NOT THE METRIC'S, AND THE CORRECTED READING IS THE HEADLINE

I recorded `starved_frac` as instrument defect 13 on the grounds that it is pinned to its floor and
cannot audit a policy. That was wrong, and the way it was wrong matters more than the original
claim. Eight blocks of eight, sixteen assets, dispersed fleet (`patience_spread` 12 about a fixed
mean of 20):

    arm                     n=8       n=12      n=16      what it is
    disp (plain)          0.0039    0.0299    0.0762     no admission rule
    dispBias250           0.0254    0.0260    0.0361     price the slot
    dispRand60            0.0020    0.0000    0.0059     refuse at random
    dispCap7              0.0000    0.0000    0.0010     cap the service length
    uniform fleet, any    0.0000    0.0000    0.0000

**`starved_frac` was reporting a true zero.** Every arm of the `capped` battery was a workable
admission rule at a load the console could carry, and under those conditions no vehicle is excluded,
so zero is the right answer. The metric was never broken. What was broken was my inference: I asked
a distributional question where the distribution happened to be degenerate, got a null, and
concluded the instrument could not see.

**Corrected again after the `maps` battery (9449946), which had already falsified the reason I first
gave.** My first correction said the zero came from the fleet being uniform, and that a uniform
fleet cannot exclude anyone. That is false and the project's own data says so: on a UNIFORM fleet, a
plain FIFO console with an unbounded backlog starves **0.8125** of its vehicles at sixteen assets,
replicated at 0.8125 and 0.8117 on two other maps. Uniformity is not what makes the metric read
zero; a console that can cope is. Both of my explanations for the null were wrong before this one,
which is itself the lesson: the
genus is the one this loop keeps finding and this is a cleaner instance of it than the original:
**a null measured where the phenomenon cannot occur is not evidence about the phenomenon, and the
configuration is part of the instrument.** Defect 13 is rewritten as that, and the replacements
(`answer_rate_min`, the quartiles, the IQR) stay, because they are the right instruments anyway and
they are what caught this.

**The corrected reading is the strongest single number the project has.** With no admission rule, a
mixed-deadline fleet at sixteen assets leaves 7.6 percent of its vehicles with ZERO answers over the
whole run, and total exclusion grows with span (0.0039, 0.0299, 0.0762 at eight, twelve and
sixteen). A cap on service length takes that to 0.0010 at sixteen and to exactly zero at eight and
twelve, at a fleet-mean answer rate that is unchanged (0.4716 against 0.4704 in the twelve-block
profile above). Random refusal also nearly eliminates it, at the cost of 38 percent of the mean.
Pricing the slot is the worst of the three: it halves exclusion at sixteen assets and CREATES it at
eight, where the plain console had almost none (0.0254 against 0.0039).

**And worry (b) is dead: the stratification is not a denominator effect.** Per-vehicle ask counts
are flat across the deadline range in every arm (ratio of most-patient to least-patient asks is
0.96, 0.97 and 1.02 for plain, cap and random). The whole of the stratification is in the numerator:
under a plain console the least-patient vehicle asks 28.4 times over the run and is answered 0.4
times, while the most-patient asks 27.3 times and is answered 26.4. Under the cap the same vehicle
asks 32.9 times and is answered 4.5, while the most patient falls from 26.4 answers to 19.1. Total
answers are about unchanged. **The cap redistributes service at constant total.**

**G11, registered.** At full tier, `starved_frac` in a dispersed fleet rises with span under a plain
console, and a service-length cap drives it to zero at every span with `answer_rate_v` equivalent to
the plain console's.
*Falsifier: the cap's `starved_frac` is equivalent to the plain console's, or the cap's mean answer
rate gates DOWN, in which case the exclusion is bought rather than removed and the recommendation is
a trade rather than a free lunch.*

**G12, registered, and it is the one that decides whether this reaches guidance.** The exclusion is
span-dependent, so there should be a span below which no admission rule is needed and above which
one is. The plain console's `starved_frac` at eight assets is 0.0039, near zero.
*Falsifier: exclusion is flat in span, which would make it a property of the fleet alone and the
admission rule's role incidental.*

### CR-62 FOURTH AMENDMENT: THE DISCIPLINE DOES MORE THAN THE ADMISSION RULE, AND MY ATTRIBUTION WAS PREMATURE

The reviewer asked for a fifth explanation of the stratification and named one I had not considered:
the console walks its pending requests in FCFS order, so a request's death in queue is
patience-dependent BY CONSTRUCTION, and part of the seventy-four to one may belong to the
DISCIPLINE rather than to admission control. The codebase's own docstring anticipates this, which is
why `_patience_vector` permutes: without the permutation `edf` would be `fcfs` by construction.
The reviewer's verdict, accepted: until the EDF arm runs, attributing the stratification to
admission control is premature. Twelve blocks, sixteen assets, spread 12, run before the battery:

    arm        answers, least patient    most patient    tail ratio    half-to-half ratio
    b100                        0.417          26.417          63.4                  4.97
    b250                        1.000          24.667          24.7                  3.77
    cap7                        4.500          19.083           4.2                  1.49
    edf                        10.417          12.417           1.2                  0.92
    edfCap7                    15.417          15.667           1.0                  0.91

(Counts over a fixed run length, so within an arm the ratio IS the per-step ratio; the battery's
`answer_step_impatient` and `answer_step_patient` carry the normalized levels.)

**The attribution was wrong and the corrected finding is stronger.** Simply working the queue in
deadline order takes the stratification from 63.4 to 1.2, essentially removing it, where the best
admission rule takes it to 4.2. **The console has two independent levers on who gets served, and the
one nobody reaches for, the order it works its queue in, dominates the one everyone does.** The
least-patient vehicle goes from 0.4 answers to 10.4 under EDF alone, a factor of twenty five, while
the most patient falls from 26.4 to 12.4. It is a redistribution and the discipline performs it
without refusing anybody.

**And the reviewer's own gate passes, once it is measured against the contrast it was calibrated
on.** The registered threshold was twenty to one per step or the effect is bookkeeping. The per-step
half-to-half ratio is 4.97, which looks like a failure, but the seventy-four to one it was
calibrated against is a tail-to-tail figure and the matching per-step tail-to-tail ratio is 63.4.
Half-to-half averages over a fleet that is mostly middle and compresses any monotone gradient; the
two statistics are not comparable and the apparent failure was the comparison, not the effect. With
ask counts flat across the deadline range (0.96 to 1.02) and the per-ask and per-step tail ratios
agreeing at 74 and 63, **worry (b) is closed: the stratification is not a denominator artefact.**

**G13, registered, and it supersedes the admission framing of G9.** In a dispersed fleet, deadline
ordering compresses `answer_step_ratio` further than any admission rule does, and does so with
`served_per_step` and `answer_rate_v` equivalent to FCFS.
*Falsifier: EDF's ratio is equivalent to `cap7`'s, in which case the two levers are the same lever
and the admission framing stands; or EDF's throughput gates DOWN, in which case the compression is
bought and the sentence is a trade rather than a free swap.*

**G14, registered, and it is what a programme would actually be told.** The two levers compose
rather than substitute: `edfCap7` reaches a ratio no lower than `edf` alone.
*Falsifier: `edfCap7` gates below `edf`, which would mean each lever removes a different part of the
harm and both are needed.*

**What this does to the sentence.** Before: *a service cap removes exclusion at no cost to the
mean.* After: *a console that works its queue in deadline order removes almost all of it, a service
cap removes most of it, and the two do not add.* The second is a better recommendation because
deadline ordering requires only the requester's own slack, which the quantile-budget rung already
established is the cheap information. It is also a worse headline for admission control, which is
the correct outcome of running the control.

### CR-62 FIFTH AMENDMENT: EDF DOMINATES RATHER THAN TRADES, AND THE DIRECTION CONTROL CAME FREE

Eight blocks of eight, sixteen assets, dispersed fleet, run before the battery to find out whether
deadline ordering costs anything. It does not.

    arm             served   answer_rate   abandoned   starved   answer_iqr   strat ratio
    b100 (fcfs)     0.1691      0.4776      0.1965     0.0762      0.668          63.4
    cap7            0.2082      0.4752      0.2337     0.0010      0.239           4.2
    edf             0.1981      0.5258      0.1828     0.0000      0.152           1.2
    patient_first      .           .        0.2141     0.0771      0.564            .

**EDF is not a fairness-for-throughput trade. It dominates FCFS on every quantity measured**: more
served per step, a higher fleet-mean answer rate, FEWER abandonments, no total exclusion at all, and
the lowest spread. The mechanism is the classical one and the model contains it exactly: under FCFS
the console spends its service on requests whose requester can afford to wait, while the requests
about to expire sit behind them and are abandoned. Serving the most urgent first converts
would-be abandonments into answers, so total delivered service RISES. Fairness here is not bought;
it is what the efficient order happens to produce.

**And the cap's cost is now visible where M4 predicted it.** `cap7` reaches its compression by
abandoning MORE than the plain console (0.2337 against 0.1965), which is the exchange M4 priced.
EDF reaches a better compression while abandoning LESS (0.1828). Between the two levers the
discipline is strictly better and the admission rule is the one that charges for its fairness.

**The direction control arrived without being registered, and it is the one that matters.**
`patient_first` sorts on the same key as `edf` and in the opposite direction. It leaves exclusion
exactly where FCFS had it (0.0771 against 0.0762), barely moves the spread (0.564 against 0.668),
and abandons MORE (0.2141). So the result is not "any informed sort helps" and it is not an artefact
of sorting at all: **it is specifically urgency, and the wrong direction on the right key is worse
than no key.** Registered as G16, with the full discipline ladder added to the battery so the claim
is tested against every ordering the model has rather than against the one that flatters it.

**G16.** Across the six disciplines the model implements, the compression of `answer_step_ratio`
tracks how well the ordering approximates urgency: `edf` best, `patient_first` no better than
`fcfs`, and `random` between them.
*Falsifier: `easy_first` or `hard_first`, which sort on the job's difficulty and not on the
requester's deadline, compress as much as `edf` does. That would mean any sort correlated with
service length is sufficient, the deadline is incidental, and the finding collapses back into the
length-selection result the cap already gives.*

`easy_first` is the interesting one, because serving short jobs first is the discipline analogue of
the cap and is what the scheduling literature would reach for. If it compresses as well as `edf`,
the honest sentence is about length and not about deadlines. The battery decides it.

### CR-62 SIXTH AMENDMENT: THE ELEMENTARY CORE IS CONCEDED, AND THE LADDER GROWS A SECOND CURRENCY

Two things from the reviewer's Round 31, both of which change what may be claimed rather than what
was measured.

**The elementary core, conceded before anyone makes us concede it.** Under FCFS with reneging a
customer is served if and only if its wait is shorter than its own patience, so a longer deadline
is a higher service probability BY ARITHMETIC. That is Barrer 1957 (`10.1287/opre.5.5.650`,
CrossRef-verified) and it is the core of the seventy-four to one. A referee arriving from the
impatience-queue literature rather than from scheduling will see it immediately, and they will be
right. Barrer is cited the moment the stratification is stated, and the claim is narrowed to the
three things the arithmetic does not give: the axis rotating from the work to the client, the sign
of size-based control flipping from starvation-cause to starvation-cure, and the comparison of the
two levers. Similarly, EDF beating FCFS is a known optimality result for capacitated due-date
scheduling with non-completion penalties (Xu, Shi and Duenyas 2016, `10.1111/poms.12606`,
CrossRef-verified) and is written as reproduced rather than found.

**My argument for keeping the cap was backward, and the reviewer's correction is the reason the
result gets bigger rather than smaller.** I argued the cap survives because a console may not know
deadlines. But a deadline is a cheap, specifiable message field, and a service-time estimate is the
expensive graded capability CR-56 already priced. A console is MORE likely to know deadlines than
lengths, so the cap's surviving case is narrow: a frozen message format with a skilled crew, where
the cap is the only fairness lever available. The requirements ladder does not lose the cap. It
gains a second column: **each rung now has an input requirement, a throughput price and a fairness
price, and the two prices are not paid by the same rung.**

**G17, the reviewer's registration, and it is the sharpest prediction in the battery.** The
quantile budget reads the SAME message field EDF reads, the requester's own slack, and should spend
it in the opposite direction: it refuses tight slack by rule, and tight slack is exactly the
impatient vehicle. So in a dispersed fleet the quantile budget should be REGRESSIVE,
