`answer_step_ratio` above the plain console's 63.4 at a fleet-mean answer rate no lower.
*Falsifier: the quantile budget compresses, or its mean gates down so the comparison is not at
matched information value.*

If G17 lands the sentence is: **the deadline field is fairness-neutral, EDF spends it progressively
and a conservative budget spends it regressively, so a specification must name the rule and not the
field.** That is the same-information-opposite-rule genus for the fourth time in this project, and
it is the most transferable thing the loop has produced.

**G18, the mechanism watch item.** Serving the most urgent first means serving requests closer to
expiry, so EDF's `worked_late_per_step` should sit slightly ABOVE the plain console's, with
`edfCap7` suppressing it again.
*Falsifier: EDF's late bin comes back BELOW the plain console's. That would mean the compression is
not produced by the mechanism claimed and the whole attribution needs re-opening, exactly as the
FCFS attribution did.*

**Where CR-62 stands, for the eventual write-up.** Registered: G1 to G18. Adjudicated on pilots and
awaiting the full tier: G2 and G6 expected to fire (the offset has no distributional story), G4's
premise already failed and was replaced, G7 superseded by G9, and the admission-control framing
superseded by G13. Surviving and expected to hold: the stratification exists and is large, the
discipline dominates the admission rule, the two levers compose, and total exclusion is real in a
mixed fleet and removable. Nothing from this conjecture goes near the manuscript until job 9450238
lands.

## CR-62 VERDICT, PART ONE: THE DISCIPLINE DOMINATES AT FULL TIER, AND TWO OF MY TESTS COULD NOT MOVE

Full tier, 32 blocks of 64, jobs 9450238 (P1 to P4) and 9455454 (P5, P6). Contrasts against the
plain FCFS console in a dispersed fleet, at sixteen assets.

    arm             ratio       starved     iqr         answer_rate   served
    edf            -3.3687 G   -0.0692 G   -0.5062 G   +0.0380 G     +0.0300 G
    cap7           -2.6835 G   -0.0680 G   -0.4221 G   -0.0071 eq    +0.0400 G
    hardFirst      -2.4049 G   -0.0643 G   -0.3329 G   -0.0226 eq    -0.0052 eq
    random         -2.3171 G   -0.0634 G   -0.3188 G   -0.0253 eq    -0.0054 eq
    easyFirst      -2.2532 G   -0.0645 G   -0.3111 G   -0.0268 eq    -0.0057 eq
    patientFirst   +1.4052 G   +0.0043 G   -0.0911 G   -0.0828 G     -0.0219 G

**G13 HELD, and it is the strongest result in the project.** Deadline ordering gates DOWN on every
inequality measure and UP on both aggregate ones at the same time: the stratification ratio falls
from 4.33 to 0.96 ($t = -130.6$, 32 of 32 blocks), total exclusion goes from 6.92 percent of
vehicles to exactly zero ($t = -57.5$), the interquartile spread falls 0.5062 ($t = -342.6$), and
the console nonetheless serves $+0.0300$ more per step ($t = 137.3$) and answers $+0.0380$ more of
what it is asked ($t = 48.8$). **Nothing is traded.** A ratio below one means the least patient half
of the fleet is now answered MORE per step than the most patient half, which is what serving by
urgency means when the urgent are the ones who cannot wait.

**G16 HELD on its registered falsifier, and the full tier adds something I did not predict.** The
falsifier was that `easy_first`, which sorts on the job's length rather than the requester's
deadline, compresses as much as `edf`. It does not: $-2.2532$ against $-3.3687$, and where `edf`
gates UP on throughput and mean answer rate `easy_first` is equivalent or slightly down on both. And
`patient_first`, the same key in the wrong direction, is worse than FCFS on every column. So the
compression tracks urgency and not sorting.

What I did not predict is that **RANDOM order recovers about two thirds of it** ($-2.3171$, gating).
FCFS is not a neutral baseline that EDF improves on; it is actively regressive, because working
requests in arrival order makes the wait a vehicle faces correlate with nothing about whether it can
survive the wait. Merely breaking that correlation removes most of the harm. Only deadline order
removes it entirely, and only deadline order pays nothing for it.

**The two levers separate into two currencies, as the reviewer predicted.** `cap7` has the best
throughput (+0.0400) and buys it with abandonment (+0.0397, gated); `edf` has the best fairness and
its abandonment change is equivalent ($-0.0074$). Neither dominates the other and they are answers
to different questions.

**Mission success is equivalent for every arm at both spans.** This is a console-level result and
gets written as one, exactly as the throughput ladder did. Registered as the expected outcome.

### Two registered tests came back as nulls that were not nulls

Instrument defect 13's genus, third and fourth instances, both mine.

**G17 could not act.** The quantile-budget arms returned BIT-IDENTICAL numbers to the plain console,
0 of 32 blocks differing, in every metric. `_admit_budget` is consulted only when `_sees_the_draw`
is False, and every arm ran at `admit_acuity` 1.0, so the console always saw the job's true length
and never read the budget at all. The quantile was inert by construction. Reported as untested, not
as falsified.

**G18 could not move.** Every effort bin came back exactly 0.0000 for every arm, with
`worked_per_step` equal to `served_per_step` to the last digit. At `comms_level` 0 with a
feasibility test, a console never starts work that ends lost, late or stale, so the decomposition
has no support at this operating point. The reviewer's watch item (does EDF's late bin rise, which
would confirm it is serving the dying) is unanswered rather than answered negatively.

**The repair, and it is procedural rather than technical.** Both failures are the same mistake:
registering a test in a configuration where the quantity under test is structurally pinned, then
being ready to read the resulting zero as evidence. Before job 9456257 was submitted, every arm in
it was piloted locally at four blocks and confirmed to move. That check costs four minutes and it
is now the precondition for any arm entering a battery. The pilot already shows G17 will hold: at
`admit_acuity` 0 the mean-budget console reads 6.03, `q80` reads 7.08 and `q95` reads 8.90, so the
quantile budget is regressive and monotonically so in the quantile, which is the reviewer's
prediction exactly.

## CR-61 VERDICT: THE INSTRUMENT IS SOUND, ONE PREDICTION IS FALSIFIED, AND WHETHER TO VERIFY THE AUTOMATION IS DECIDED BY THE HARM CHANNEL RATHER THAN BY THE ERROR RATE

Full tier, 32 blocks of 64, job 9450073.

**P0, the instrument check, CONFIRMED at full tier.** Setting `check_dprime` to 0.0, 0.5, 1.0 and
2.0 recovers a measured $d'$ of $-0.0034$, 0.4969, 0.9950 and 1.9928 at sixteen assets, and the two
arms that hold sensitivity at 1.0 while moving the criterion (`d10hi`, `d10lo`) recover 0.9946 and
0.9954. **The measured discrimination is invariant to the decision rule**, which is the property a
signal-detection instrument must have and the reason the earlier chord-between-corners version was
replaced before it ran. D7 is now computable.

**P1 FALSIFIED on its registered falsifier, and in the more interesting direction.** P1 predicted
the ladder is flat at four and six assets, with a gated effect at either span as the falsifier. At
four assets every check rate gates UP over never-checking, monotonically: $+0.0405$, $+0.0640$,
$+0.0752$ and $+0.0859$ at rates of 0.25, 0.50, 0.75 and 1.00. Served per step at the same span is
not flat either, falling 0.1149 to 0.0844. So checking is NOT free at an idle console: it costs
throughput at every span we ran. What is true at four assets is that the correctness it buys is
worth more than the throughput it costs, which is a stronger claim than the one registered and it
required the falsifier to fire to find it.

**P2 HELD.** At sixteen assets served per step is monotone decreasing in the check rate (0.5235,
0.3191, 0.2168, 0.1575, 0.1183) and success is monotone non-increasing (0.9985, 0.9883, 0.9873,
0.9824, 0.9790). Nothing rises with $r$ at the weakest assumption about what a wrong answer costs.

**The reversal, and both ends of it gate.** Full verification is worth $+0.0859$ of mission success
at four assets ($t = 5.5$, 30 of 32 blocks) and $-0.0640$ at eight ($t = -5.1$, 28 of 32). Unlike
the admission-offset ladder, this optimum is RESOLVABLE: the tolerance band, every check rate within
one rate floor of the best, has width one at four, eight, twelve and sixteen assets and widens to
three only at six, which is the transition. So a point location is reportable here by the same
sharp-cell doctrine that forbids it on the offset ladder: **check everything at four assets, check
nothing at eight and above, and six is the crossing.**

**P3 HELD, conditionally, and the condition is the finding.** The break-even was registered as a
wrong-answer cost $h^\star$ in $[1, 8]$. The amended battery rotated the coordinate and swept the
automation's ERROR RATE across the three harm channels instead, which is the same question asked in
units a programme can measure. Always-check against never-check, at eight assets:

    channel   q=0.1      q=0.3      q=0.5      q=0.7      q=0.9
    time    -0.1167 G  -0.0376 .  +0.0264 .  +0.1133 G  +0.1577 G
    reflect -0.1235 G  -0.0991 G  -0.0640 G  -0.0391 .  +0.0000 eq
    weak    -0.1318 G  -0.1152 G  -0.1084 G  -0.0947 G  -0.0835 G

**Verification pays only when a wrong answer costs the vehicle TIME.** In that channel the sign
reverses inside the sweep and both ends gate, with break-even between 30 and 70 percent automation
error. Where a wrong answer merely produces a weaker result, checking NEVER pays, not even when the
automation is wrong nine times in ten ($-0.0835$, $t = -7.2$, gated). Where it actively misleads,
break-even sits at the very top of the range and is reached exactly at $q = 0.9$.

So the knob does change a mission outcome, gated, in both directions, and CR-61 is not withdrawn.
But the sentence it produces is not the one the conjecture expected. **A programme cannot decide
whether its operators should verify the automation from the automation's accuracy. It has to know
what a wrong answer does downstream, and the same accuracy argues for opposite policies in two of
the three channels this model implements.** That is the same-information-opposite-rule genus for
the fifth time.

**S4, and it settles which axis schedules the check rate.** Across comms levels 0 to 3 the ordering
of never-check against always-check does not flip at eight or sixteen assets; never-check wins at
every rung. The check rate is scheduled by SLACK, that is by span, and not by the channel. A
degraded link lowers everything and reorders nothing, which is worth stating because the obvious
programme instinct is to verify more when the link is bad.

**What an operator is worth, which is the uncomfortable part and is reported anyway.** Against a
no-operator baseline, an operator who never verifies raises blue losses at every span ($+0.0425$,
$+0.0268$, $+0.0319$, $+0.0203$, all gated) and an operator who always verifies raises them further
($+0.0968$, $+0.1039$, $+0.0792$ at eight, twelve and sixteen). Only the free-checking operator at
four assets gates UP on mission success against no operator at all ($+0.1499$, $t = 10.0$). In this
scenario, at this operating point, the console earns its place only where checking is cheap and the
fleet is small. That is a limitation of the scenario as much as a finding about operators, and it is
written with the scenario attached.

## CR-62 VERDICT, PART TWO: SAME FIELD, OPPOSITE SIGN, AND THE FREE LUNCH HAS A PRECONDITION

Full tier, 32 blocks of 64, job 9456257, the corrective battery. Every arm in it was piloted at
four blocks first and confirmed to move, which is the repair for the two nulls that were not nulls.

**G17 HELD, and it is the most transferable result in the conjecture.** Against a mean-budget
console, which reads the requester's deadline field and cannot see the job:

    arm          ratio            starved          answer_rate      served
    q80         +0.5463 G        +0.0215 G        +0.0051 eq       +0.0074 eq
    q95         +2.6763 G        +0.0932 G        -0.0164 eq       +0.0087 inco
    edfBudget   -5.3488 G        -0.1380 G        -0.0766 G        -0.0167 G

The quantile budget is REGRESSIVE, monotonically in the quantile, and `q80` is regressive **at
matched aggregate performance**: its mean answer rate and its throughput are both equivalent to the
mean-budget console's while its stratification gates up $+0.5463$ and its total exclusion gates up
$+0.0215$. The registered falsifier, that the mean gates down so the two are not compared at
matched information value, does not fire for `q80`. In levels, a 95th-percentile budget leaves
**23.3 percent of a mixed fleet with no service at all**, against 14.0 percent for the mean budget
and 0.0 percent for deadline ordering.

**So the sentence is confirmed: the deadline field is fairness-neutral and the RULE carries the
sign.** Two consoles with identical information, neither able to see the job, differ by a factor of
six in how unequally they distribute service (`edfBudget` 1.12 against `meanBudget` 6.47 at sixteen
assets), and one of them gates progressive while the other gates regressive. A specification that
mandates the deadline field and leaves the rule to the implementer has specified nothing about
fairness. This is the same-information-opposite-rule genus for the sixth time and it is the cleanest
instance the project has.

**And the free lunch has a precondition I had not isolated.** EDF's dominance holds for a console
that SEES the job: `edfSees` gates up on throughput and mean answer rate while gating down on every
inequality measure. For a blind console it is a genuine trade: `edfBudget` gates down $-0.0766$ on
mean answer rate and $-0.0167$ on throughput to buy its $-5.3488$ of compression. **Deadline
ordering is free only when the console can also see what it is being asked.** That qualification
ships with the claim.

**G18 HELD.** At C3, where the console can waste work, EDF's late bin sits $+0.0037$ above the
plain console's ($t = 37.3$, 32 of 32). Serving the most urgent first does mean serving requests
closer to expiry, which is the mechanism the compression was attributed to. The second half of the
watch item, that a cap suppresses the late bin again, is untested: `admit_cap` is inert at C3
because the feasibility test already refuses everything the cap would have, and `admitEdfCap` is
identical to `admitEdf` on every metric to four decimals.

**A finding that came with it: the compression weakens as the link degrades.** At C0 deadline
ordering takes the ratio from 4.33 to 0.96. At C3 it takes 7.06 to 4.54. The plain console is more
stratified on a bad link and the discipline recovers less of it, because latency, which is the same
for every vehicle, becomes a larger share of the deadline that separates them.

**G14, the composition, PARTIAL and the partial is the useful part.** At sixteen assets, adding a
cap to a deadline-ordered console buys $+0.0185$ of throughput ($t = 65.0$) and costs $+0.0227$ of
abandonment ($t = 32.1$), with the stratification unchanged (inconclusive at $-0.0502$). Adding
deadline ordering to a capped console buys $-0.7355$ of stratification ($t = -85.7$), $+0.0341$ of
mean answer rate, and $-0.0243$ LESS abandonment, at equivalent throughput.

**So the two levers are not symmetric and the ordering is a recommendation.** Deadline ordering is a
strict improvement to a capped console on every axis at sixteen assets. The cap on top of deadline
ordering is a throughput-for-abandonment trade, and at eight assets it is a losing one
($-0.0121$ served and $-0.1814$ mean answer rate, both gating). **Adopt the discipline first and
unconditionally; add the cap only where throughput is worth abandonment and the span is high.**
G14's falsifier fires at eight assets, where `edfCap7` does gate below `edf` on stratification
($-0.2246$), so at low span both levers contribute to fairness and only at high span does the
discipline saturate it.

## CR-60 VERDICT: THREE OF FIVE FIRED, THE MECHANISM IS NOT ESTABLISHED, AND THE METRIC TO SPECIFY IS NOT THE ONE I REGISTERED

Full tier, 32 blocks of 64, job 9449945. Ten arms, two families: `out*` holds the commanded gap
fixed and varies an outage length, `sp*` varies the commanded spread.

    arm      success   station_err   cross    fleet_spread   fleet_cross
    base      0.8872        12.99      2.99          86.45          3.71
    sp60      0.8652        17.79      6.36         127.61          7.46
    out40     0.8726       186.84     26.37          95.04          6.79
    out80     0.8643       215.68     26.82         106.85          9.42
    sp120     0.8242        45.72     28.42         250.22         24.38
    out160    0.8613       231.91     35.88         119.75         11.66
    out320    0.8726       221.06     54.70         121.64         12.25
    sp300     0.4341       172.56    130.27         596.72         78.88
    sp450     0.4434       366.41    300.08         855.30        133.57
    sp600     0.4468       818.80    727.33        1078.35        174.94

**The counterexample that motivated the conjecture survives and is now gated.** `out40` carries 187 m
of station error and scores 0.8726; `sp300` carries LESS, 173 m, and scores 0.4341, a gap of
$-0.4385$ ($t = -32.5$, 32 of 32 blocks). Meanwhile `out80`, `out160` and `out320` span 187 to 232 m
of station error with mission success statistically indistinguishable from `out40`. **A programme
reading the scalar sees a 24 percent spread in tracking error that costs nothing, and a lower value
that costs half the mission.**

**J1 FALSIFIED as worded, HELD on the reading it should have registered.** Registered: zero
inversions in the cross ordering across the pooled set. Counted over all fifteen arm pairs, cross
has three inversions and the scalar has five, so the falsifier fires. But two of cross's three are
between arms whose mission success is statistically equivalent, and an ordering error between two
outcomes the experiment cannot distinguish is noise being scored as a mistake. Counted over the
eight pairs that DO differ at the gate: **cross-track has 0 inversions, fleet dispersion has 0,
fleet cross-dispersion has 0, and the scalar has 2.** The repair is principled and it is recorded as
a repair rather than as the original claim.

**J2 HELD.** Within the outlier family the commanded gap is fixed, and cross-track rises 26.4 to
54.7 across it, so J1 is not a re-reading of the spread knob. What must be said alongside it:
success does NOT fall within that family, every arm being equivalent to `out40`, so the rank
correlation of cross with success WITHIN the outlier family is $-0.105$ against the scalar's
$-0.632$. **The predictive power is between families and not within them.** Cross-track separates a
formation that has left its route from one that is merely late; it does not grade how late.

**J3 FALSIFIED, and with it the mechanism.** Registered: the success cost per 100 m of cross-track
error at least halves from banded to open terrain, because the penalty is supposed to come from
leaving a survivable corridor. Measured by OLS over the five arms carried onto all three maps, the
cost is $-0.0534$ per 100 m on the band, $-0.0780$ at the chokepoint and $-0.0432$ in the open. The
open-to-band ratio is 0.81, not the registered 0.50 or less. On a relative reading, as a fraction of
each map's own baseline success, open is WORSE than band ($-7.70$ percent against $-6.02$ percent
per 100 m), which is the opposite of the prediction. **The corridor explanation is not established
and only the correlation may be written**, which is exactly what the registered falsifier said to do.

**J4 FALSIFIED for cross-track and HELD for a metric I registered as the runner-up.** The
acceptance rule was to be a cross-track threshold separating every arm above 0.83 success from every
arm below. No such threshold exists: `sp120` sits at 28.4 m of cross-track, inside the 26.4 to 54.7
band occupied by four arms that all score above 0.86. The scalar fails too, as expected. But
**`fleet_spread_mean` separates the two groups cleanly at 189 m and `fleet_cross_mean` at 18 m**,
with no overlap on the full ten-arm set.

**J5 FALSIFIED in the useful direction, and it changes the recommendation.** The registered
expectation was that resolved cross-track error wins and fleet dispersion is reported as the
runner-up, on the strength of a single pilot inversion. At full tier that inversion is between arms
whose success is equivalent, dispersion ties cross-track at zero gated inversions, and dispersion
alone supports the acceptance threshold cross-track cannot.

**So the recommendation inverts.** The metric a programme should specify is **fleet dispersion, the
RMS distance of the living assault force from its own centroid**, which contains no commanded
geometry at all, needs no knowledge of the formation the autonomy was trying to hold, and is
recoverable from bare position tracks on a range. Resolving station-keeping error onto the heading
is a real improvement over the scalar and it is now the runner-up rather than the answer. What
CR-57's bare null becomes is not "project the error" but "**stop measuring the error against the
commanded formation and measure the formation itself.**"

## MAPS VERDICT: THE STARVATION ORDERING IS MAP-INVARIANT, AND IT FALSIFIED MY OWN CORRECTION

Full tier, 32 blocks of 64, job 9449946. The five console levers replicated on all three terrain
profiles. `starved_frac`, the fraction of vehicles that asked and never received a single answer:

    arm       n=4      n=6      n=8      n=12     n=16    n=16 on chokepoint / open
    admit   0.0000   0.0000   0.0000   0.0000   0.0000        0.0000 / 0.0001
    bound   0.0000   0.0000   0.0000   0.0000   0.0002        0.0002 / 0.0002
    lifo    0.0000   0.0000   0.0000   0.0000   0.0001        0.0001 / 0.0002
    rand    0.0000   0.0000   0.0385   0.2821   0.4420        0.4168 / 0.4503
    fifo    0.0000   0.0000   0.2581   0.7404   0.8125        0.8125 / 0.8117

**The replication is essentially exact.** FIFO's exclusion at sixteen assets is 0.8125, 0.8125 and
0.8117 on band, chokepoint and open. The ordering of the five levers is identical on every map and
the onset span is the same. This is the precondition the reviewer set for the Discussion's
boundary-law paragraph: a console-level result that does not depend on the ground.

**The magnitude is the thing to carry.** A plain FIFO console with an unbounded backlog leaves
**81 percent of its vehicles with no answer at all** over the whole run, while serving a backlog of
132 requests. LIFO holds a LARGER backlog, 146, and starves 0.0001, because it answers the request a
vehicle just made rather than the one it gave up on. The two policies differ by a factor of eight
thousand in exclusion at nearly identical backlog.

**And it falsified my own correction to instrument defect 13, which is recorded rather than quietly
fixed.** Two sections above I wrote that `starved_frac` read zero in the `capped` battery because a
uniform fleet cannot exclude anyone. The arms here are a uniform fleet and FIFO excludes 81 percent
of it. The true reason for the earlier zero is simpler and duller: every arm in that battery was a
workable admission rule at a load the console could carry. **I have now given two explanations for
one null and the first was wrong**, which is worth stating plainly because it is the same failure
the defect names: reaching for an explanation of a zero without checking the configurations in which
it is not zero. The project's own earlier battery contained the counterexample the whole time.

**It also independently corroborates CR-62 on a different axis.** CR-62 found that FCFS ORDERING
within a served queue stratifies a mixed-deadline fleet. This battery, run before it and on a
uniform fleet, finds that FIFO SERVICE of an unbounded backlog excludes most of the fleet outright.
Different knob, different fleet, same villain: **working the oldest request first is the single most
harmful thing this console does, and it is the default everywhere.**

## CR-63. THE VERIFICATION FRONTIER, AND THE DEGENERATE CORNER THAT WAS CARRYING CR-61's HEADLINE

Registered before the battery. The reviewer's Round 32 ruling was that the CR-61 sentence
"verification pays only when a wrong answer costs time" may not ship until the quality channel has
its positive control, because a null in a channel that cannot move the mission is a fact about the
instrument. **Applying his own guard, which is to grep the landed batteries before registering an
arm, found the positive control already present and also found something worse.**

**The positive control fires, so the channel is not inert.** In the landed `trust` battery (9450073)
the two never-checking quality arms differ only in what a wrong answer resolves at: `weak` leaves the
vehicle at its own autonomy, `reflect` puts it as far below autonomy as a right answer is above. At
90 percent automation error the difference on mission success is **-0.0835, t = -8.53, 28 of 32
blocks, gated**. Control quality reaches the mission. The channel comparison is not void.

**But the reference arm in that comparison is a constant.** At `check_rate` 1.0 the console checks
every answer and `_supervise` returns a caught answer as a right one, so no wrong answer is ever
shipped. Automation error and harm channel are then both inert by construction, and the data says so
without ambiguity: the six always-checking cells, three channels crossed with five error rates from
0.1 to 0.9, are **byte-identical to one another**, all at success 0.7764. Every one of them.

So the sentence I sent the reviewer, and the whole three-price comparison, was carried entirely by
the never-checking arm. **This is instrument defect 15 and it is the thirteenth's genus once more, in
its most expensive form yet: not a metric that cannot move, but a REFERENCE that cannot move.** A
contrast against a constant is a level dressed as a difference, and the paper came within one round
of publishing three of them.

The correct reading of the landed grid, stated in levels because that is all it supports:

    auto_error         0.1      0.3      0.5      0.7      0.9
    never check, time  0.8931   0.8140   0.7500   0.6631   0.6187
    never check, refl  0.8999   0.8755   0.8403   0.8154   0.7764
    never check, weak  0.9082   0.8916   0.8848   0.8711   0.8599
    always check, any  0.7764   0.7764   0.7764   0.7764   0.7764

Never checking beats always checking almost everywhere, and the reason is throughput, not
correctness: a checked answer costs a full console slot and a shipped one costs `comply_frac` of
one, so at a saturated console verification is paid for on every job and collected on the wrong ones
only. That is a real and sponsor-relevant statement, and it is not the statement CR-61 recorded.

**R1. The interior.** The grid has two corners and no middle. Check rates 0, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75 and 1.0
against automation error 0.1 to 0.9 in each of the three channels, on mission success and blue
losses. Prediction: the best check rate is interior somewhere, and it rises with `auto_error`.
FALSIFIER: if the tolerance band contains a corner in every cell of the grid, there is no frontier,
the finding is a binary "check or do not", and the word frontier does not appear in the paper.

**R2. The stakes ladder, which is the reviewer's registration.** `g = q_operator - q_auto` is what a
right answer is worth. Sweep `q_auto` over 0.50 to 0.90 at fixed `q_operator` 0.95, so g runs 0.45
down to 0.05, at three check rates in the reflect channel. Prediction: the check rate that wins
rises with g, because the benefit of catching a wrong answer scales with g while the throughput
price does not. FALSIFIER: if the gated ordering of the three check rates is the same at every g,
the stakes do not set the policy, and the frontier is one number rather than a design curve.

**R3. The decomposition, which is the cheap decisive test.** If the throughput reading above is
right, then setting `comply_frac` to 1.0, so that shipping an unchecked answer costs exactly what
checking it costs, must destroy the never-checking arm's advantage. Sweep `comply_frac` 0.25 to 1.0
at fixed error and channel. Prediction: the advantage falls monotonically and reverses by 1.0.
FALSIFIER: if never-checking still gates above always-checking at `comply_frac` 1.0, throughput is
not the mechanism and I have no second explanation ready, which is the point of registering it.

**R4. The operator's own worth, on the metric the sponsor reads.** The reviewer refused the
"operator is net-harmful" sentence as a calibration echo. R2 answers it directly: the sweep contains
the g at which a console with an operator beats no console, and that number, not the sign at the
default, is what a programme can use. Reported as a frontier with the default marked on it.

Nothing from CR-61's third prediction goes near the manuscript until this battery lands. The
first two, the instrument recovery and the falsified free-check prediction, stand: neither of them
reads the degenerate corner.


## CR-60 VERDICT, CORRECTED AND SHARPENED BY ITS OWN NUMBER SCRIPT

Two corrections and one new result, all from `tools/make_disp_numbers.py`, which is the first time
the L2 and L5 sections were read by a script rather than by eye.

**Correction one, the corridor ratio.** I reported 0.81 to the reviewer and called it the open-map
to banded-map ratio of the dispersion penalty. It is not that number. 0.81 is the RELATIVE cost on
the open map alone, the penalty as a fraction of what the base formation achieved there. The ratio
the falsifier actually asked about is 1.04 on absolute cost and 1.65 on relative cost, both against a
falsifier of 0.50. **The verdict does not change and the correction strengthens it**: J3 is falsified
by a wider margin than I reported, and on the relative reading a force with no cover to funnel it
pays MORE for dispersion than one moving through a corridor, not less. The corridor is not the
mechanism.

**Correction two, and it is a real finding rather than a slip.** The registered blindness test asked
whether a formation metric moves when route bias moves the force off its commanded line. Every
candidate moves; scoring them on how much they move ranks them, but ranks them on the wrong thing. A
requirement is a LINE, so the only nuisance failure that matters is one that carries a cell across
it. Scored that way, against the thresholds section L1 fixes:

    reading               inversions   threshold   route-bias misclassifications
    station_err_mean         8 / 24    none            (no line to cross)
    station_cross_mean       2 / 24    none            (no line to cross)
    fleet_cross_mean         0 / 24    18 m            1 of 10
    fleet_spread_mean        0 / 24    189 m           0 of 10

The one misclassification is the interesting cell. At route bias 0 the dispersed force reads
**2.4 m** of fleet cross-track, comfortably inside an 18 m line, while its mission success in that
cell is the worst in the section. A force strung out ALONG its axis of advance has almost no
cross-track spread, and cross-track is exactly the component that cannot see it. `fleet_spread_mean`
does not have that hole because it is not a component of anything: it is the whole distance.

**So the recommendation tightens from "measure the formation" to a specific reading with a number.**
Specify the RMS distance of the living assault force from its own centroid, hold it under about 190
metres, and do not decompose it. Both decompositions tested here, one against the commanded line and
one against the axis of advance, have a blind family that the undecomposed distance does not.

**And an honest debt, recorded rather than fixed.** `fleet_spread_mean` was not logged in section L3,
so the reading this conjecture ends up recommending is the one whose threshold has NOT been checked
against column length. The other two hold their separation at all four spans, over threshold ranges
of 22 to 116 metres and 35 to 218 metres, which is itself a warning: a threshold that moves by a
factor of five across column length is not a requirement. Until L3 is rerun with the recommended
reading logged, the 190 metre figure is a single-span number and the paper must say so.

## CR-64. THE FAIRNESS FIX THAT NEEDS NO MESSAGE FIELD

Registered before the battery, with the pilot disclosed below because it is what prompted the
conjecture and pretending otherwise would be dishonest.

CR-62 ended with a recommendation a program office can only half act on. Deadline order removes the
whole of the console's exclusion and improves its aggregates, and it needs a semantic deadline field
somebody has to define, populate and maintain, which the requirements ladder already prices as the
expensive rung. The recovery decomposition then showed something the conjecture did not predict:
random order, a discipline that reads nothing at all, recovers 92 percent of the exclusion and 69
percent of the stratification. **Nobody fields a coin flip.** So the question the decomposition
actually raises is whether there is a DEPLOYABLE rule with the same information cost, and there is
an obvious candidate the discipline set did not contain: serve whoever has been served least.

It reads nothing about the request. Not its deadline, not its length, not when it arrived. It reads
a counter the console keeps about its own past behaviour, which no message format has to carry and
no requester has to populate. It is what a fair-queueing scheduler approximates and it is the
natural zero-information member of the family.

**The pilot, disclosed.** Twenty-four replications, sixteen assets, two-point deadline fleet,
local, no gate:

    discipline     starved   ratio    iqr      rate     served
    fcfs            0.2344   32.980   0.8733   0.4681   0.1589
    random          0.0156    5.313   0.6577   0.4492   0.1599
    round_robin     0.0000    1.670   0.4182   0.4905   0.1858
    edf             0.0000    1.694   0.4459   0.5159   0.1932

That is why this is registered rather than reported. Six predictions, four of which can kill the
recommendation.

**S1.** Round-robin gates DOWN against first come, first served on all three inequality readings at
sixteen assets and gates down on NEITHER aggregate. FALSIFIER: it fails to gate on exclusion, or it
gates down on mean answer rate or throughput. Then the fix is not free and must be reported as a
trade like the service cap.

**S2.** Round-robin and deadline order are equivalent on exclusion and on stratification, so the
semantic field buys nothing distributional. FALSIFIER: deadline order gates apart from round-robin
on either reading. Then the field buys fairness after all and CR-62's recommendation stands
unchanged.

**S3.** Deadline order gates UP against round-robin on the aggregates, so the field buys throughput
and only throughput. FALSIFIER: equivalent on both. Then the deadline field buys nothing measurable
anywhere and the recommendation is round-robin unconditionally, which is a stronger claim than the
one being registered and would need its own replication before it ships.

**S4.** The fix survives a UNIFORM fleet, where there is no stratification to remove and only
exclusion is at stake. FALSIFIER: no gated movement on exclusion against first come, first served.
Then the result is specific to a mixed fleet and must be stated that way.

**S5.** Below the collapse span the rule is inert rather than costly: at four assets round-robin is
equivalent to first come, first served on every reading. FALSIFIER: it gates DOWN on an aggregate at
four or six assets. A rule that costs something where it is not needed is not free insurance and
would have to be conditioned on span, which is the property CR-62's recommendation was praised for
not having.

**S6, and it is the sharpest.** CR-62 welded a precondition onto deadline order: without job sight
it costs -0.0766 of answer rate and -0.0167 of throughput. Round-robin should escape that
precondition entirely, because it never consults the job. Run both at `admit_acuity` 0 and the
prediction is that round-robin's distributional gain survives intact while deadline order's does
not. FALSIFIER: round-robin's gain also collapses without job sight. Then the precondition is a
property of the console rather than of the rule, and the whole free-lunch framing is wrong.

Nothing from this goes near the manuscript until the battery lands at full tier. The recovery
paragraph already in the manuscript is unaffected either way: it reports the random arm, which has
landed.

### CR-64 AMENDED BEFORE ITS BATTERY, TWICE, AND THE SECOND AMENDMENT IS THE CONJECTURE

**Amendment one: S4 as written was untestable, caught by this session's own new guard.** S4 said the
fix must survive a uniform fleet "where only exclusion is at stake". Piloted, the uniform fleet on an
ADMITTING console produces `starved_frac` 0.0000 in every arm at every span, because an admitting
console does not build the backlog that excludes anyone. That is instrument defect 13 for the
fifteenth time and I was about to commit it in a registration written the same day I registered the
guard against it. The guard is what caught it: name the configuration in which the quantity is known
to be nonzero, and check it before writing the arm. The landed `maps` battery names it. Exclusion on
a uniform fleet needs a BLIND console with an unbounded backlog, which is where it reads 0.8125.

**Amendment two, and it inverts the conjecture.** Piloting that blind configuration to fix S4 found
something the conjecture did not contain. Sixteen replications, sixteen assets, two-point fleet:

    discipline    ADMITTING console            BLIND console
                  starved   ratio    rate      starved   ratio      censored   rate
    fcfs           0.1719   24.385   0.4725     0.8398   undefined   0.6250    0.0109
    random         0.0195    5.886   0.4468     0.5586   undefined   0.1875    0.0300
    round_robin    0.0000    1.816   0.4896     0.8398   undefined   0.6250    0.0102
    edf            0.0000    1.716   0.5136     0.9531   undefined   0.3750    0.0024

The blind column's ratios were first recorded as 10.750, 7.812, 10.750 and 6.000. Those were the
old sentinel leaking into a mean and the table above is the corrected one, re-derived after the
sentinel was replaced with NaN in the same session. **On a console that cannot refuse, the
stratification ratio is not large, it is UNDEFINED**, because the impatient half of the fleet
receives nothing at all in a majority of replications, and `answer_step_censored` is the honest
report of how often. The correction does not change the verdict and sharpens it. Note also that
deadline order's censoring is LOWER than arrival order's, 0.3750 against 0.6250, while its exclusion
is HIGHER, 0.9531 against 0.8398: it starves the patient half too, so the impatient half is no longer
uniquely zeroed. A ratio would have called that an improvement.

**The recommendation reverses with admission control.** On an admitting console the ordering is
round-robin and deadline order best, random middling, arrival order worst. On a blind console it is
random best by a factor of three on the answer rate, arrival order and round-robin tied and inert,
and **deadline order the worst of the four**, starving 95 percent of the fleet against arrival
order's 84.

The mechanism is legible in both cases and neither is a bug. Round-robin reads a counter that only a
DELIVERED answer increments, so on a console whose answers arrive after their requesters have gone,
the least-served vehicle stays least-served and the rule spends every slot re-serving the same
doomed request. It is a feedback loop whose feedback never arrives. Deadline order fails worse and
for a related reason: it always picks the tightest deadline, which on a blind console is exactly the
request most likely to be already dead, so it selects for hopelessness. Random has no feedback and no
selection, which is why it is the only rule that helps the console that cannot refuse.

**S7, registered on that pilot.** On a blind console the discipline ordering inverts: random gates UP
against arrival order on the mean answer rate, and deadline order gates DOWN against arrival order on
exclusion. FALSIFIER: either half fails at the full tier. The claim being registered is a CONDITIONAL,
that the discipline recommendation is void without admission control, and it is worth more to a
program office than the unconditional one it replaces because it names an order of adoption:
admission control first, discipline second, and never the second without the first.

**S4 as reworded.** On a uniform fleet with a blind console, which is the configuration the `maps`
battery already showed excludes 81 percent under arrival order, round-robin does NOT repair the
exclusion. FALSIFIER: it does. The prediction is now that the repair is specific to a fleet whose
deadlines differ AND a console that can refuse, and registering the negative is the honest form since
the pilot already points that way.

**S2 restated, because it too rests on a floor.** Round-robin and deadline order both read
`starved_frac` 0.0000 on the admitting console, so equivalence there is a comparison between two
floors and is not evidence. S2 is therefore tested on `answer_step_ratio` and `answer_rate_iqr`
only, both of which move, and the exclusion column is reported as uninformative rather than as
agreement.

### CR-64 AMENDMENT THREE: THE DISCRIMINATOR LANDED BEFORE THE BATTERY DID, AND IT SETTLES THE QUESTION

Adversarial review's Round 33 named the arm I asked for, and it is not another aggregate. **A request
is FEASIBLE at entry when an idle console could have answered it inside its deadline**, that is,
`recv + 1/mu + lat <= deadline` at the moment it joins the backlog. One that was feasible at entry
and then died at a deadline was killed by the ORDER the console chose, not by its capacity or its
link. That is the quantity a deadline-scheduling optimality theorem is actually about, and it is the
only counter in this model that reports the size of the scheduling PROBLEM rather than an outcome.

The registered discrimination: if the tie between round-robin and deadline order is a ceiling
effect, then at the tied operating point the counter reads near zero, because a queue that never
presents a binding choice gives the ordering nothing to order. If the counter is large and the tie
survives, the tie is about the information and not about the ceiling, which review called the more
surprising and still publishable reading.

Implemented and piloted at sixteen replications before submission:

    n     rule           died_feasible/step   died_feasible_frac   ratio     served
    8     fcfs                 0.0732               0.3795         3.078     0.1164
    8     round_robin          0.0704               0.3428         2.281     0.1290
    8     edf                  0.0685               0.3317         2.381     0.1324
    16    fcfs                 0.2095               0.5618        24.385     0.1595
    16    round_robin          0.2147               0.5336         1.816     0.1810
    16    edf                  0.2187               0.5313         1.716     0.1885
    24    fcfs                 0.3452               0.6509           NaN     0.1761
    24    round_robin          0.4073               0.6664         1.264     0.1960
    24    edf                  0.4046               0.6424         1.121     0.2192

**The ceiling reading is dead at the pilot.** At the tied operating point **56 percent of all
requests that were servable when they arrived die at a deadline anyway**, rising to 65 percent at
twenty-four assets. The queue is presenting an enormous binding choice and the two rules still tie on
both inequality readings. The information reading therefore wins in its strong form, and the claim it
licenses is the one I was most suspicious of: **a counter the console keeps about its own past
behaviour is worth as much, distributionally, as a deadline field the message has to carry.**

Two things the counter exposes that were not asked for. First, `entered_feasible_frac` is 1.0000 in
every cell at C0, so no request in this configuration is born dead and the counter is currently
measuring pure ordering loss; its discriminating power against a link is untested and T5 now logs it
so that gap closes in the same battery. Second, FCFS at twenty-four assets returns NaN on the ratio,
which is the censoring guard added this session firing on live data: **at twenty-four assets arrival
order excludes the entire impatient half of the fleet**, so the stratification is not large, it is
undefined. That is a stronger statement than any ratio and it is only sayable because the sentinel
was removed.

**Review's ruling on CR-63's R3, recorded and accepted.** A falsifier whose failure would require a
bug tests plumbing, so R3 is an AUDIT rather than a conjecture and the ledger should say so at
registration time rather than score it as a survived prediction. The battery is already running and
the audit is worth having, since an audit of exactly this kind is what caught defect 15. The
content-bearing replacements review named, to be registered only if the audit returns something to
explain: R3b, an equality prediction, and R3c, linearity of the gap in saved slots, with two
second-mechanism candidates named in advance.

### CR-64 AMENDMENT FOUR: THE COUNTER'S OWN NEGATIVE CONTROL, WHICH WAS NOT FREE TO FIND

The discriminator above is only a discriminator if it can read zero. Piloting it found that in the
PUBLISHED configuration it cannot: at two assets on a clear link with a metronome console it still
reads 0.0989. That is not a floor and not an artefact. One service is 6.67 steps against the
impatient half's deadline of 8, so two vehicles asking on the same step already exceeds it, and the
tightest deadline in this fleet is barely longer than a single service. **The console this paper has
been studying is one where the ordering ALWAYS has something to order.** That is worth knowing on its
own and it is not something any earlier metric could have said.

It also means the registered discrimination was one-sided as written, which is the failure the null
guard was added to catch, caught this time in a conjecture rather than in an explanation. Doubling
the patience supplies the missing branch and supplies it exactly:

    patience   n     died_feasible_frac    answer_step_ratio
    20         2           0.0989               1.016
    20         8           0.3921               4.169
    40         2           0.0000               1.127
    40         8           0.2483               1.536
    80         4           0.0000               1.084
    80         8           0.0495               1.201

**T6, registered.** At patience 40 with two assets the counter is exactly zero, so no request that
was servable at entry dies and the ordering has genuinely nothing to order. Every discipline must
then be equivalent on every reading. FALSIFIER: any two disciplines gate apart on any reading in a
cell where the counter is zero. That would mean a rule is winning where there is no scheduling
problem to solve, which no mechanism in this model permits and which would invalidate the counter
rather than the rule.

That makes the discrimination two-sided: the counter reads exactly zero where the ordering cannot
matter and 0.53 at the operating point where two rules tie, so a tie at 0.53 is a statement about the
information the rules read and not about the console's ceiling.

### INSTRUMENT DEFECT 16, FOUND BY BUILDING THE NEGATIVE CASE RATHER THAN BY TRUSTING THE METRIC

Replacing the ratio's sentinel with NaN left a second problem: the ratio is undefined exactly where
the harm is worst, and one censored replication in sixty-four takes a whole block with it. The fix
is to invert it. `answer_step_share` is the impatient half's answers per step over the patient
half's: one at proportionate service, zero at total exclusion, above one where the impatient half is
favoured. Bounded, defined wherever anybody is served, and it orders the design space identically
wherever the ratio is defined (their product is exactly one, tested).

**Then I ran it on the case it was not built for.** On a console with no admission rule:

    rule           share     starved
    fcfs           0.2500     0.8398
    random         0.1681     0.5586
    round_robin    0.2500     0.8398
    edf            1.0000     0.9531

**Deadline order scores a perfect 1.0000 while excluding 95 percent of the fleet.** Being a ratio of
two rates, the share reads one when both halves get almost nothing, and that is equal exclusion
rather than proportionate service. A table with a share column and no level column would have
reported the worst rule in the battery as the fairest.

This is the same genus one more time, and the guard is the reporting rule rather than a code change,
because the metric is correct and only its use is not: **`answer_step_share` never appears in a
table, a macro or a sentence without `starved_frac` or `answer_rate_v` beside it.** Pinned by a test
that asserts the share is 1.0 for a ruinous fleet and for a healthy one and that only the level tells
them apart, and stated in the metric's own docstring so it is read where it is used.

It is worth recording HOW this was found, because the method generalises. The metric was not audited.
It was run on the configuration where it was expected to look worst, which is the same move the null
guard prescribes for explanations: name the case that would embarrass the claim and run that first.
Three of this session's four defects were found that way and none by inspection.

### CR-64 AMENDMENT FIVE: THE CONTRADICTION IN THE MANUSCRIPT, AND THE SECTION THAT RESOLVES IT

Promoting the discipline result put two paragraphs next to each other that contradict one another.
The requirements-ladder paragraph ends "given the counter, the queue discipline stops mattering,"
and the paragraph after it now says the order is the largest single lever in the paper. Both are
true and the difference is the backlog: the ladder's claim was measured on a console bounded at its
derived capacity, where the backlog holds one or two requests and a queue of one has no order to
permute. The manuscript now says so explicitly in a bridging paragraph, which is the honest fix, but
the reconciliation raises a question neither result answers. **The counter and the order are both
being sold as repairs to the same console. Are they substitutes on FAIRNESS as well, and if a
program can only buy one, which?**

Piloted at sixteen replications, sixteen assets, two-point fleet, admitting console:

    arm                share   starved     iqr     rate      srv    aband
    unbounded fcfs    0.0608    0.1719   0.8589   0.4725   0.1595   0.2095
    unbounded edf     0.5993    0.0000   0.4455   0.5136   0.1885   0.2187
    unbounded rr      0.5865    0.0000   0.4191   0.4896   0.1810   0.2145
    bound k=2 fcfs    0.5119    0.0039   0.3691   0.4044   0.1543   0.2594
    bound k=2 edf     0.8173    0.0000   0.2038   0.4070   0.1663   0.2630
    bound k=2 rr      0.6732    0.0000   0.2839   0.3995   0.1583   0.2567
    bound k=1 fcfs    0.6989    0.0000   0.2524   0.3769   0.1506   0.2725

**Bounding the backlog is a fairness fix in its own right and nobody has reported it as one.** A
counter at the derived capacity takes the share from 0.0608 to 0.5119 and exclusion from 0.1719 to
0.0039, with no change to the message format and no order at all. It works because a bound truncates
the wait every request faces, and a truncated wait is exactly what the impatient half needs.

**And ordering dominates it on every column.** Against the bounded arm, the unbounded ordered console
is better on the share (0.5993 against 0.5119), on exclusion, on answer rate (0.5136 against 0.4044),
on throughput (0.1885 against 0.1543) AND on abandonment (0.2187 against 0.2594). The two buy the
same fairness by opposite means: the counter buys it by REFUSING more, and the order buys it by
SERVING more. That is why one is free and the other is not.

**T7, registered.** The counter and the order are substitutes on the distributional readings and not
on the aggregates: at the derived capacity the bounded arm and the ordered arm are equivalent on
exclusion, and the ordered arm gates UP on mean answer rate and throughput and DOWN on abandonment.
FALSIFIER: the bounded arm matches the ordered one on any aggregate, or beats it on any
distributional reading, either of which would make the counter the cheaper repair and the order an
optional refinement rather than the recommendation. The 2x3 of capacity against discipline is run
whole so the interaction is measured rather than inferred, since the pilot says the best share in the
grid belongs to the cell that has both.

### CR-64 SUBMITTED, and instrument defect 17: a comparison that stopped being valid

`roundrobin` is job `9459574` (T1-T3) and `roundrobin2` is job `9459575` (T4-T7), 32 cpus, 10 h,
both smoke-tested at 532 and 480 AR_ROWs before submission. Nothing from either goes near the
manuscript until it lands at full tier. The recovery paragraph already in the manuscript is
unaffected either way: it reports the random arm, which has landed.

### INSTRUMENT DEFECT 17, THE SAME GENUS, AND THE GUARD FOUND THE PART I MISSED

The genus is a convention correctly derived for its first use case and carried silently into a
second where it does not hold. Here the convention is `assert a == b` on two metric dicts.
`run_mission` returns a flat dict of floats; for most of this project's life every one of those
floats equalled itself, so a bare equality was a correct byte-identity check and twenty-four tests
across eighteen files used it. Then `answer_step_ratio` and `died_feasible_frac` became honestly
undefined where their denominators are zero, which is defect 16's repair. NaN does not equal itself.
Thirteen identity tests began failing on runs that were in fact bit-identical.

Note which side was wrong. The tests were right to be strict: byte-identity is the correct assertion
for an opt-in mechanism's off-path. The metrics were right to be undefined: a sentinel that averages
is what defect 16 was about. What had stopped being valid, silently and without any line of code
changing, was the comparison between them.

The repair is `conftest.same_run`, which requires the same key set and then requires every pair to
be either equal or both NaN. All twenty-four sites use it.

**The guard is `tests/test_no_bare_dict_equality.py` and it earned itself immediately.** It parses
every test file, walks each function, tracks which local names are bound to a call returning metrics,
and fails on any equality assertion where either side is one of those. It carries a planted-offender
negative control and a planted-clean control, and it runs in a tenth of a second because it never
executes a mission.

I wrote it after three greps had already found twenty-one of the twenty-four sites, expecting it to
confirm the sweep was complete. It found three more, in `test_behavior.py`, `test_channel.py` and
`test_crew.py`. Two of those carried an f-string failure message after the comparison and the third
split the equality across a line continuation, so none of them matched any pattern I had guessed.
That is the argument for the guard in one sentence: the hand search found what I thought to look
for, and the parser found what the language actually says. A defect whose sites cannot be enumerated
by inspection is one whose recurrence cannot be prevented by care.

---

### CR-65, REGISTERED. THE WASTE IS FREE TO REMOVE AND IT BUYS ALMOST NOTHING

**The mechanism I set out to build first, and why it was killed before a line of it was written.**
The obvious human-factors objection to serve-least-served is that it maximises switching, and task
switching costs a person time. I was about to add a per-switch setup cost to the console when the
defect-14 guard made me name the configuration in which the mechanism is nonzero and check it. It is
not nonzero anywhere. Instrumenting `_pick` over three disciplines at sixteen assets:

```
    discipline     services   switches   switch fraction   max pending from one asset
    fcfs               190        190          1.0000                19
    round_robin        190        190          1.0000                29
    edf                190        190          1.0000                19
```

Every discipline switches asset on every single decision, so a per-switch cost is a constant tax on
service and cannot interact with the order. The mechanism was inert by construction and was not
built. What the instrumentation did surface is the third column: up to twenty-nine requests from ONE
vehicle can sit in the buffer at once, which is not a queue of twenty-nine problems. It is one
problem and twenty-eight ghosts of it.

**The finding.** A request goes stale when its vehicle gives up waiting and asks again: `req_seq`
advances and the buffered record is superseded. The published console discovers this by working the
request, because the slot is claimed before the sequence numbers are compared. On the blind buffer
console that is where nearly the whole clock goes.

```
    n_blue   arm                     busy_serviced   busy_stale   busy_late   served/step
      8      blind, works the ghost      0.2491        0.2025       0.3776       0.0374
      8      blind, drops the ghost      0.2621        0.0000       0.5925       0.0393
     12      blind, works the ghost      0.0154        0.9498       0.0373       0.0023
     12      blind, drops the ghost      0.0608        0.0000       0.9286       0.0091
     16      blind, works the ghost      0.0147        0.9544       0.0318       0.0022
     16      blind, drops the ghost      0.0174        0.0000       0.9850       0.0026
     24      blind, works the ghost      0.0154        0.9654       0.0212       0.0023
     24      blind, drops the ghost      0.0157        0.0000       0.9869       0.0024
     16      admitting                   0.9926        0.0000       0.0000       0.1489
```

`stale_check = "before"` is a console that consults its own books before it starts work. It carries
NO new information: `req_seq` is the console's record of what each vehicle last asked, it is
maintained on the arrival path, and no message field carries it. The difference between the two
consoles is an interface, not a sensor.

**The repair is total and it is nearly worthless.** Waste does not fall, it goes to exactly zero.
And the freed clock does not become throughput: at sixteen assets `busy_stale` 0.9544 becomes
`busy_late` 0.9850 and `served_per_step` moves 0.0022 to 0.0026, which is inside this project's own
rate floor of 0.0090. The console was not wasting effort. It was short of the capacity to finish
what it had already accepted, and removing the ghosts only let it fail at the next stage instead.
The admitting console, at the same span, serves 0.1489: sixty times more, and it is not faster.

**Registered claim.** On the blind buffer console: (a) `busy_stale` gates DOWN to 0.0000 in every
arm at every span; (b) `busy_late` gates UP by substantially the amount `busy_stale` gave up; (c)
`served_per_step` is EQUIVALENT to the unrepaired console at every span at or above twelve; and (d)
the repaired blind console remains below the admitting console's `served_per_step` by more than a
factor of ten at every span at or above twelve. The deadline field on a console is therefore not
paying for order, which CR-62 already showed is second-order once any rule is used, and not paying
for waste, which is free to remove and buys nothing. It is paying for refusal.

**FALSIFIER.** (c) or (d) fails at any span at or above twelve. Either would put a zero-information
rung above the deadline field on the requirements ladder, which is the opposite of what the
manuscript now says, and the manuscript would have to change rather than the claim be salvaged.

**Second registered claim, U2, and it reaches back into CR-64.** On a blind console the ORDER decides
which ghosts are reached first, and the pilot at eight assets puts serve-least-served at 0.6177 of
the clock on ghosts against arrival order's 0.2670: the rule that seeks the least-served vehicle is
seeking exactly the vehicle whose old requests are still buffered. If that is why the discipline
ordering inverts on a console that cannot refuse, then dropping the ghost should COLLAPSE the
separation between the three rules rather than shift all three. Registered: with `stale_check =
"before"` the three disciplines are equivalent on `served_per_step` and on `answer_rate_v` at every
span. FALSIFIER: they separate by more than the gate anywhere, which would make CR-64's inversion a
property of the rules rather than of what a blind buffer contains, and both readings would then have
to ship side by side.

**U5 is the defect-14 guard and not an extra section.** The claim is about a console losing its clock
to ghosts, so it is checked where there are none: two to four assets on a fast console, where the
pilot reads `busy_stale` at zero. Every arm must be equivalent on every reading there. If the arms
separate where there is nothing to repair, the repair is not what is doing the work anywhere either.

Battery `hopper/batteries/ghost.sh`, six sections, 685 cells, smoke-tested at exit 0.

---

### CR-65 AMENDED, AND CR-66 REGISTERED: THE FREE RUNG IS MUCH BETTER THAN I SAID

Adversarial review made one demand that changed the result rather than the presentation: report the
dead clock as a SUM beside the bucket that falls, and put the zero-information BOUND in the same
table as the repair, because drop-oldest at capacity is refusal by overflow and it is the floor that
any claim about what the deadline field buys has to clear first. Both were right, and the second one
moved the science.

**Amendment to CR-65.** `busy_dead` is added: the console clock that produced no answer by any
route. It is now reported in every section of `ghost.sh`, because a repair can move dead time
between buckets without removing any of it and a reader shown only the bucket that fell would read a
relabelling as a recovery. Sixteen assets, blind, twelve seeds: `busy_dead` 0.9861 works the ghost,
0.9843 drops it. Conserved to three decimals. At twelve assets it is 0.9868 against 0.9315, which is
the one place the repair removes dead time rather than relabelling it, and that is the collapse edge.
The registered claim is unchanged and its exhibit is now honest.

**Amendment two, the cadence control.** The sharpest objection CR-65 faces is that the ghost
population is manufactured by how fast a requester that gave up comes back. `reask_hold` scales that
hold and the sweep spans sixteenfold. Pilot at sixteen assets: `busy_stale` reads 0.9525, 0.9525,
0.9571, 0.9611, 0.9608 at 0.5x, 1x, 2x, 4x, 8x. The ghost clock is not a product of the cadence and
the registered prediction for U3 is EQUIVALENCE across the whole range. FALSIFIER: it moves outside
the gate anywhere, which would make the result a property of one arbitrary re-ask rate.

### CR-66, REGISTERED. HOW UNLIKE EACH OTHER THE VEHICLES ARE DECIDES WHAT THE CONSOLE MUST KNOW

Four rungs, each costing strictly more than the one below, priced on one console at one span.
Sixteen seeds, sixteen assets, direct control, C0, buffer console.

```
    UNIFORM FLEET            served   answer   starved   share   busy_dead
    blind, unbounded         0.0022   0.0127    0.8125   ----     0.9862
    bounded k = 1            0.1456   0.3818    0.0000   ----     0.0000
    bounded k = 2            0.1476   0.4093    0.0000   ----     0.0098
    bounded k = 4            0.1032   0.2926    0.0039   ----     0.3146
    bounded k = 8            0.0079   0.0265    0.6992   ----     0.9425
    admitting                0.1489   0.4183    0.0000   ----     0.0000
    admitting + EDF          0.1489   0.4183    0.0000   ----     0.0000

    TWO-POINT FLEET          served   answer   starved   share   busy_dead
    blind, unbounded         0.0018   0.0083    0.8594  0.3125    0.9904
    bounded k = 1            0.0742   0.2070    0.0391  0.2901    0.4923
    bounded k = 2            0.0602   0.1948    0.3516  0.0552    0.5988
    bounded k = 4            0.0620   0.2104    0.4766  0.0046    0.5893
    admitting                0.1494   0.4668    0.3047  0.0244    0.0000
    admitting + EDF          0.1494   0.4344    0.0000  0.4432    0.0000
```

**On a uniform fleet the deadline field buys nothing.** A two-slot bound delivers 0.1476 against the
admitting console's 0.1489, and both exclude nobody. The field costs a message format, a clock
convention and a requirement on every vehicle in the fleet, and at sixteen assets on a uniform fleet
it is worth 0.0013 served per step, which is inside this project's rate floor of 0.0090.

**On a two-point fleet every rung buys something the one below cannot.** The bound gets less than
half the throughput (0.0602 against 0.1494) AND fails to fix exclusion (0.3516). The field recovers
the throughput and still leaves 0.3047 excluded. Only the field read TWICE, once to refuse and once
to order, gets both: 0.1494 served and 0.0000 excluded. That is the requirements ladder as a
measurement rather than an argument, and the variable that separates the rungs is not the span, not
the link and not the console speed. It is how unlike each other the vehicles are.

**Registered claim.** (a) On a uniform fleet, `boundTwo` is EQUIVALENT to `admits` on
`served_per_step` and on `starved_frac` at every span. (b) On a two-point fleet, `admits` gates UP
over `boundTwo` on `served_per_step` at every span, and `admitsEdf` gates DOWN over `admits` on
`starved_frac` at every span. (c) In the heterogeneity sweep there is a spread at which (a) turns
into (b), and it is reported as a threshold with the sweep's resolution stated, in the same form as
the dispersion threshold in CR-60. FALSIFIER for the headline: (a) fails, which would mean the free
bound never substitutes for the field and the ladder has no free rung; or (b) fails, which would
mean the field never earns its cost and the ladder has no paid rung. Either kills the framing, not
just a number.

**Second registered claim, V4, the sizing rule.** A bound is only a bound if it is TIGHT. Pilot on
the uniform fleet: 0.1456, 0.1476, 0.1032, 0.0079 served at capacities one, two, four and eight, so
the free rung has an INTERIOR optimum and a loose bound is not a weak repair but no repair.
Registered: `served_per_step` is non-monotone in `queue_capacity` with a maximum at or below four,
and the arm at capacity twelve is EQUIVALENT to the unbounded blind console. FALSIFIER: monotone in
either direction, which would make the sizing rule a corner solution and the recommendation "as
tight as possible" rather than a number a programme office can size.

**What is prior here and what is not.** Bounded buffers, drop-oldest and admission control are all
old. What is not prior is pricing them against each other AS INFORMATION REQUIREMENTS on one console
under one workload, and finding that the ordering of the rungs reverses with fleet heterogeneity.
That is the sponsor's question in its own terms: not "is a queue bound good" but "what does the
console have to be told, and what decides it".

Battery `hopper/batteries/ladder.sh`, four sections, 532 cells, smoke-tested at exit 0.

**CR-65 amendment three, and it turned the control into a result.** The cadence sweep was registered
as an equivalence to be put on the record. Run at eight assets as well as sixteen it is not one:

```
    reask_hold      n = 16                              n = 8
                req/step  served  busy_stale        req/step  served  busy_stale
        0.25      0.3173  0.0022    0.9544            0.1257  0.0331    0.2041
        1.00      0.3173  0.0022    0.9544            0.1267  0.0374    0.2025
        2.00      0.3261  0.0023    0.9575            0.1358  0.0837    0.0099
        4.00      0.3337  0.0024    0.9590            0.1145  0.0814    0.0089
        8.00      0.3330  0.0024    0.9596            0.0854  0.0619    0.0080
       16.00      0.3203  0.0024    0.9576            0.0643  0.0495    0.0083
```

At sixteen assets the ghost clock is flat across sixty-four-fold, which is the registered
equivalence and it holds. At eight it is not flat at all: asking back half as often takes the ghost
clock from 0.2025 to 0.0099 and MORE than doubles delivered throughput, 0.0374 to 0.0837, and the
curve then turns over so that sixteen-fold is worse than two-fold. That is a third free repair and
it is on the FLEET side rather than the console side: nobody has to know anything, a vehicle simply
waits longer before asking again, and it has an interior optimum like the buffer bound does.

U3 now carries both spans. Registered: `busy_stale` is EQUIVALENT across the whole cadence range at
sixteen assets, and gates DOWN at eight; `served_per_step` at eight is non-monotone with a maximum
at or below fourfold. FALSIFIER for the first half: the cadence moves the ghost clock at sixteen,
which would make CR-65's headline a property of one arbitrary re-ask rate after all. FALSIFIER for
the second: monotone in either direction at eight, which would make the fleet-side repair "back off
as far as possible" rather than a number that can be sized.

Reporting inertness measured only at the span where nothing moves would have been the flat-reference
defect wearing a control's clothes. Job 9459668 was cancelled two minutes in and resubmitted with
the amended section rather than scored as registered.

### ROUND 34 RULINGS, RECORDED

**Q2, the blind console.** Keep the zero, demote its use. Three jobs, separated: as the ladder's zero
it stays, because the absence of a discard rule is not a design choice but the absence of one and
the requirements text is addressed to whoever has not decided; as a mechanism exhibit it stays, 0.81
starved and 0.95 of the clock on ghosts; as a CONTRAST DENOMINATOR it is demoted, because any lever
quoted against blind at twelve assets and above is mostly quoting the bottom rung's collapse.

MANUSCRIPT ACTION, owed when `ladder` lands. Every increment is routed adjacent-rung and the
sixtyfold appears exactly once, as the price of omission. The exposed passages are the queue
section's `\qBlindClear` to `\qAdmitClear` success jump, the band section's `\MpBandSrvFull` served
per step over the blind console, and the discipline section's `\LvSrvFifo`. Each needs the bounded
console between them, and the numbers for that are in `ladder` V1 and V2. Nothing is edited until
they land.

**Q3, CR-64's blind-console section.** Ship the warning, hold the specifics. The warning sentence,
that the discipline recommendation is void on a console that cannot refuse, is true under both
branches of U2 and can ship now. The specific ordering claims (random recovering three times, EDF
worst of the four) have a live registered threat from U2, because the ghost-clock split already says
the rules differ in what they EAT: 0.6177 against 0.2670 at eight assets. Cost asymmetry decides it:
holding costs a week, retracting costs a table row and the churn around it. CR-64's numbers stay
valid for the works-the-ghost world either way; U2 threatens their interpretation, not their
validity. RULEBOOK: when a running battery can retract a claim but not its enclosing warning, ship
the warning and hold the specifics.

**Q4, attention cost.** The per-switch form is dead on measurement. The form that is not dead is
staleness-priced re-acquisition: a setup cost scaled by the time since the console last touched that
vehicle. It interacts with the discipline BY CONSTRUCTION, because contact-gap distributions are
exactly what the rules differ in, and it hands serve-least-served its real-world rationale, since a
fairness rule doubles as an attention-freshness rule. Instrument the per-discipline contact-gap
distributions first and build the cost only if the spread is there. Queued, not started.

**Q5, defect 17.** Row only, no prose. RULEBOOK: prose for defects that moved a PUBLISHED CLAIM
(defect 15's class), rows for defects that moved CODE (defect 17's class). The manuscript table
carries the claim-movers; the companion carries the taxonomy.

**Q4 SETTLED BY MEASUREMENT, AND THE ANSWER IS AGAIN "DO NOT BUILD IT".** Adversarial review's third
form of attention cost was staleness-priced re-acquisition: a setup cost scaled by the time since the
console last touched that vehicle, on the argument that contact-gap distributions are exactly what
the rules differ in and that serve-least-served minimises the worst gap. Instrumented before
building, sixteen assets, two-point fleet, eight seeds, gaps measured on the console clock and
cleared per mission:

```
    ADMITTING CONSOLE     n     mean     sd    p50     p90     p99     max
    fcfs               3107    40.65  10.76   40.0    53.3    60.0    66.7
    random             3303    38.61  19.23   40.0    60.0    93.3   125.0
    round_robin        3685    36.76  16.83   33.3    60.0    93.3   167.0
    edf                3646    35.93  12.81   33.3    53.3    66.7    70.7
    easy_first         3306    37.24  23.57   34.3    66.7   108.3   206.7
```

The means span thirteen percent, which is too little for a cost keyed on the mean gap to separate
the rules. The premise is also false as stated: serve-least-served does NOT minimise the worst gap
here, arrival order does, at a maximum of 66.7 against 167.0, because serve-least-served equalises
counts and not clocks and with sixteen assets arrival order is already nearly cyclic. And the one
direction the coupling does have runs the wrong way for a sceptic: deadline order has the SHORTEST
mean gap and arrival order the longest, so pricing re-acquisition would penalise the console this
paper argues against and strengthen the one it recommends. Declining to build a mechanism that would
strengthen our own result is the conservative choice, and it is recorded as one.

That is the defect-14 guard working upstream of authorship twice on the same question: two forms of
attention cost proposed, both killed on a dose-response measurement before a line of either was
written. The cost of the guard was two instrumentation scripts.

### `dispcol` LANDED (job 9459110). CR-60's OWED SECTION CONFIRMS THE READING, MOVES THE NUMBER, AND ADDS A CONDITION

Full tier, 32 blocks. The section was owed because `fleet_spread_mean`, the reading CR-60 ends up
recommending, was not among the metrics section L3 logged, so its threshold had only ever been
measured at one column length.

**K1, the column-length check.** Per-span admissible threshold intervals, four column lengths:

```
    reading                          n=4        n=8       n=12       n=16     held
    station_err_mean (scalar)   interleave interleave interleave interleave    0/4
    station_cross_mean            26 - 43  interleave interleave interleave    1/4
    fleet_spread_mean            128 - 309  122 - 250  160 - 405  199 - 560    4/4
    fleet_cross_mean               12 - 33    12 - 24    17 - 54    22 - 73    4/4
```

The recommendation SURVIVES: both readings that ignore the commanded formation hold at every column
length with zero inversions, and both station-referenced readings become unthresholdable as soon as
the column can change length. That is a stronger version of the L1 result rather than a repeat of it.

**And the number moves.** The threshold that serves every column length is the INTERSECTION of the
per-span intervals, not the average of their midpoints, because an average can sit outside an
interval it was averaged from. That intersection is 199 to 250 m. The published single-span figure of
189 m sits below it: at sixteen assets the widest formation that still succeeds reads 199 m, so a
line at 189 would forbid a formation that in fact arrives. The manuscript now specifies 225 m and
states the interval. Undecomposed dispersion is also preferred over fleet cross-track on a second
ground the owed section supplies: cross-track's joint window is 22 to 24 m, a specification that has
to be met to within two metres.

**K2 turned a robustness check into a condition, and this is the part worth having.** The reading is
properly blind to route bias, as required. The PENALTY is not.

```
    route_bias        0.0     0.25      0.5     0.75      1.0     spread range
    tight4          0.175    0.179    0.305    0.827    0.988      42 to   49
    wide4           0.102    0.112    0.209    0.760    0.986     284 to  349
    tight16         0.965    0.975    0.987    1.000    1.000     163 to  199
    wide16          0.550    0.512    0.659    0.434    0.000     883 to 1439
```

On the fully cover-biased route a 300 m commanded spread costs -0.0024 at four assets (equivalent,
t = -0.7, 12 of 32 blocks) and -1.0000 at sixteen (gates down, all 32 blocks, 100.0% to 0.0%). The
dispersed sixteen-vehicle column does BETTER on the exposed route (0.550) than on the covered one
(0.000), which the tight column of the same length does not, and that reversal is the mechanism
showing through: a covered route is a band of finite width and a formation wider than the band
cannot use it. So the requirement binds on long columns that intend to use terrain, and a short
column that plans to run the corridor has no dispersion requirement worth writing.

This also partially rescues the mechanism L2 failed to establish. L2 asked whether removing the
terrain removes most of the penalty and found it does not, at ONE column length and at the scenario's
default route. K2 shows the penalty's dependence on the route is real but conditional on column
length, which is why a single-span test could not see it. The manuscript still says the mechanism is
not isolated, because a conditional dependence is not an isolation.

Promoted to the manuscript, pre-flight PASS on all five checks including compile.

---

### CR-67, REGISTERED. THE FLEET-SIDE LEVER'S SIGN IS SET BY THE CONSOLE'S POLICY

Every lever in this paper so far has been on the console: which request to serve, whether to refuse
it, whether to recognise it as superseded. `reask_hold` is on the other side of the link. It changes
how long a vehicle that gave up stays on autonomy before asking again. It changes nothing whatever
on the console and needs no message, no field and no coordination, which makes it the cheapest
intervention anywhere in this system and the only one a fleet operator can make alone.

Pilot, twelve seeds, eight assets, buffer console, `reask_hold` 1.0 against 4.0:

```
    console                 link    served 1x   served 4x     answ 1x   answ 4x   aband 1x  aband 4x
    blind, unbounded         C0        0.0386      0.0807      0.2167    0.6406     0.0601    0.0323
    blind, unbounded         C2        0.0350      0.0680      0.2276    0.5996     0.0679    0.0171
    blind, unbounded         C3        0.0346      0.0375      0.2131    0.4535     0.0551    0.0127
    blind, bounded k = 2     C0        0.1275      0.1307      0.7481    0.8513     0.0387    0.0194
    blind, bounded k = 2     C2        0.0915      0.0660      0.5803    0.6585     0.0335    0.0114
    blind, bounded k = 2     C3        0.0532      0.0392      0.3618    0.4413     0.0429    0.0146
```
