```
    admitting                C0        0.1371      0.1371      0.7919    0.7919     0.0336    0.0336
    admitting                C2        0.0948      0.0858      0.6345    0.6634     0.0296    0.0168
    admitting                C3        0.0712      0.0522      0.4694    0.5035     0.0329    0.0163
```

**Registered claim one, the sign flip.** Slowing the fleet gates UP on `served_per_step` on the
console that cannot refuse and DOWN on both consoles that can, at C2 and C3. The two sides of the
system are therefore not separable: the same fleet-side change, with the console untouched, roughly
doubles delivered throughput on one console and costs a quarter of it on another. FALSIFIER: the
sign is the same on both console families anywhere in W1 or W2, which would make the fleet lever an
independent design choice and the whole framing wrong.

**Registered claim two, and it is the one a programme office can be caught by.** On a refusing
console the two metrics that agree everywhere else in this paper come apart: slowing the fleet gates
UP on `answer_rate_v` and DOWN on `served_per_step` in the same cell. A fleet that asks less often
gets a higher fraction of its questions answered while the force as a whole is answered less.
FALSIFIER: they agree in sign on every refusing arm.

**The two nulls, named before the run, per the defect-14 guard.** The admitting console at C0 is
expected INERT and the reason is structural: its refusal hold is `min(2*lat, patience)`, which is
zero at zero latency, so the knob has nothing to scale. Verified byte-identical in the pilot. The
deeply collapsed blind console at sixteen assets is also expected inert, and the pilot reads 0.0022
against 0.0024, inside the rate floor. Both are predictions rather than absences.

**W4 carries the opposite-signed fleet lever in the same battery on purpose.** Retrying is asking
again SOONER, and on the blind console at eight assets it takes `served_per_step` from 0.0386 down to
0.0138. Two fleet-side changes, no console change in either, opposite signs. Running them against
one another rather than each against its own baseline is what makes that a result instead of two
sensitivity analyses.

**W5 is the defect-14 guard.** A fleet-side lever acts by changing offered load, so where the console
is not load-limited it must do nothing on any console. Two to four assets on a fast console is that
place and every pair must be equivalent there.

**Genus note.** This is the seventh instance of same-information-opposite-rule and the strongest so
far, because the two rules are not even on the same machine. The information is identical (a vehicle
knows only that it gave up) and the correct rule inverts depending on a property of the console that
the vehicle cannot observe.

Battery `hopper/batteries/fleet.sh`, five sections, 543 cells, smoke-tested at exit 0.

### ROUND 35 RULINGS, RECORDED AND ACTED ON

**Q1, the headline.** The uniform free bound is not the news and was already known equivalent; the
news is the PRICING of heterogeneity. Ruled one-sentence form, adopted: what recovers span is
refusal, and what refusal has to be TOLD is set by how unlike each other the vehicles are, a uniform
fleet needing one integer with a closed form and a mixed fleet needing the deadline field read
twice. The mixed fleet is headlined because the sponsor flies one; the uniform fleet stays as the
free floor and as the honesty clause. On the two-point fleet the best bound reaches 49.7 percent of
the admitting console's throughput at k = 1, and the uniform-optimal k = 2 reaches 40.3 percent
while tripling exclusion, which is why the bound is not the recommendation there. CR-66 also unifies
three prices of one second moment: fleet dispersion costs throughput, costs fairness, and now costs
KNOWLEDGE, and the fleet-design corollary is that homogeneity is a subsidy to the console.

**Q2, the sizing cliff.** Ship it, because the cliff has a name and a formula. The optimum sits at
k* = floor(lambda + 1/2) - 1 and the cliff at k = 4 is the deadline boundary rather than new
brittleness: at k = 8 the arm is the blind console returning (busy_dead 0.9425 against 0.9862
unbounded). The asymmetry is measured: undersizing by one costs -0.0020, inside the rate floor, and
oversizing by two costs -0.0444 and gates. Recommendation in three clauses: take k from the closed
form, round DOWN when uncertain, and do not use a bound at all on a mixed fleet, because k* is
class-coupled and the two classes want different integers.

**Q3, the metric conflict, and it is a rule this project already wrote down.** `answer_rate_v` is
DISQUALIFIED as CR-67's primary reading. Its denominator is how often vehicles ask, and slowing the
fleet is a change to exactly that, so a metric whose denominator the treatment moves cannot audit
that treatment. Part of the rise is mechanical: asks fall from 0.1494 to 0.1293 per step while
answers fall 9.5 percent, so the ratio climbs 4.6 percent on arithmetic alone. `served_per_step` is
the primary intermediate, its denominator being the mission clock; `answer_rate_v` keeps one
legitimate job as a TRUST input, since reliability-when-called is what shapes whether a crew asks at
all, and it is reported as a consequence and never optimised. ACTED ON: job 9459788 was cancelled
fifteen minutes in and resubmitted as 9459816 with `blue_loss_frac` added to all five sections, so
every cell is gated on the mission as well as on the queue.

**Q4, the 4x3.** One section, one law: fleet-side demand damping SUBSTITUTES for console-side
refusal, helping consoles that lack it and running from redundant to harmful on consoles that have
it. The manuscript carries the law and the 2x2 sign-flip exhibit; the full 4x3 goes to the companion
as a procurement appendix. Not a second paper, because the substitution law is the queue section's
natural close and cutting it orphans CR-67.

**Two notes carried forward.** dispcol's superseded 189 m threshold is the argmax genus again, a
point published where an interval was owed, and the fix is the intersection rule now in the
manuscript. And the covered-route collapse from 100 percent to 0 is a feasibility wall rather than a
slope, so it is published as a WIDTH constraint with its terrain-class dependence named.

---

### CR-68, REGISTERED. THE GRAPH READING OF A FORMATION, AND WHAT IT COSTS TO USE ONE

CR-8 has sat in the todo file since the literature review as the most directly reusable idea there
for mission design: score a formation by network robustness rather than by connectivity alone. It is
now implemented as `src/sandtable/formation_metrics.py`, opt-in through an assumed peer-link range,
byte-identical when off, and it computes three things from the positions the mission already
produces: algebraic connectivity, EXACT vertex connectivity, and the f in the 2f+1 resilient-consensus
result. The fast path is a library call and the project's own textbook max-flow implementation is
kept as the independent check the tests run it against on two hundred random graphs.

**The disagreement CR-8 predicted is confirmed structurally rather than statistically.** A column in
single file has vertex connectivity 1 at every length, while its algebraic connectivity falls from
1.0000 at three vehicles to 0.0384 at sixteen. A formation tuned on the eigenvalue is not thereby
robust to one compromised vehicle, which is what the consensus literature says and what an earlier
idea in this project's own notes assumed away.

**Scope, stated in the module and not to be relaxed.** Those theorems are about CONSENSUS and these
vehicles run no consensus protocol; they pursue targets independently. What is scored is the
communication topology's resilience, not a proven property of the mission. Nothing licenses the
sentence "the formation tolerates f compromised agents".

**And a second thing fell out of building it, which is a constraint on the whole idea.** A disc
graph on a circle cannot realise a star above five spokes: rim neighbours are closer to one another
than to the hub, so a commanded star closes into a wheel and its vertex connectivity jumps from 1 to
3 whether the formation meant it or not. A topology requirement is not checkable without the
geometry that produces it. Locked in a test.

**Registered claim.** (a) In X2 the graph readings SATURATE outside a band of assumed peer-link
range: at 1000 m every arm is complete and at 150 m every arm is severed, so a saturated reading
separates nothing, and the band in which it discriminates is narrow. (b) Within that band the graph
readings carry no ordering information the undecomposed geometric dispersion does not: scored the way
CR-60 scored its four readings, over gated-apart pairs only, their inversion counts are no lower than
`fleet_spread_mean`'s. FALSIFIER for the headline: a graph reading orders a gated-apart pair
correctly that `fleet_spread_mean` inverts, at any link range in X1 or X3. That would mean the
topology carries information the geometry does not and the recommendation would have to add a graph
term, which is the outcome the literature predicts and this model may well not reproduce.

**Second registered claim, X4, the blindness the recommendation rests on.** Route bias moves the
whole force without changing its internal geometry. `fleet_spread_mean` is blind to it and that is
why it was recommended. Registered: the graph readings are NOT blind to it, because a route that
funnels the force through a corridor compresses it and raises its connectivity for reasons that have
nothing to do with the formation. FALSIFIER: they are as blind as the geometric reading, which would
remove the last argument against them.

**The parameter is the point.** The geometric reading needs nothing assumed. The graph reading needs
a peer-link range chosen before it can be computed at all, and X2 measures how much the answer moves
when that choice moves. A requirement that cannot be evaluated without first assuming a radio is a
worse requirement than one that can, even if it were otherwise equally predictive.

Battery `hopper/batteries/graph.sh`, four sections, 497 cells, smoke-tested at exit 0.

---

### CR-63 R1 RETRACTED ON ITS OWN FALSIFIER, AND R2 SUPPLIES THE FINDING ON A DIFFERENT AXIS

`verify` (job 9458832) is still running; R1 and R2 are complete and are scored here because the
retraction is owed as soon as the falsifier is met, not when the battery ends.

**R1's registered claim was that the best check rate is interior somewhere and rises with the
automation's error rate, and its registered falsifier was a tolerance band containing a corner in
every cell, which would make the finding binary. The falsifier is met.** Contrasts on mission
success against the never-check arm, which is the reference that moves (the always-check arm is
byte-identical across the sweep and the flat-reference guard says so out loud, which is the defect
this battery was written to fix):

```
    TIME channel, vs never-check, auto_error 0.1 / 0.3 / 0.5 / 0.7 / 0.9
      check 50%    -0.0562 down   +0.0088 equi   +0.0176 equi   +0.0771 up     +0.1050 up
      check 100%   -0.1167 down   -0.0376 inco   +0.0264 inco   +0.1133 up     +0.1577 up
    WEAK channel
      check 50%    -0.0674 down   -0.0444 inco   -0.0591 down   -0.0542 down   -0.0381 inco
      check 100%   -0.1318 down   -0.1152 down   -0.1084 down   -0.0947 down   -0.0835 down
    REFLECT channel
      check 50%    -0.0776 down   -0.0610 down   -0.0166 equi   -0.0156 equi   +0.0049 equi
      check 100%   -0.1235 down   -0.0991 down   -0.0640 down   -0.0391 inco   +0.0000 equi
```

In every cell a corner is either strictly best or inside the band. The optimum is never gated
interior on the error axis. The claim is retracted rather than reworded.

**What survives, and it is a mechanism rather than a curve.** The error axis does something in ONE
of the three harm channels and nothing in the other two, and the split is exactly the channel in
which a wrong answer generates REWORK. Under `time` the requester keeps its problem and comes back,
so a wrong answer costs the console a slot now and another later, and above an error rate somewhere
between 0.3 and 0.7 checking pays and keeps paying to the corner. Under `weak` and `reflect` the
wrong answer is delivered and accepted, so it costs the vehicle quality and the console nothing, and
checking is a pure loss at every error rate tested. The reflect channel is the one in which a wrong
answer puts a vehicle BELOW its own autonomy, which is the over-trust failure the literature is
about, and even there verification does not pay. **Verification pays when a wrong answer comes back,
not when it is harmful.** That is the sentence R1 actually supports.

**R2 has the crossover, and it is on the axis the adversarial reviewer registered rather than on
mine.** Sweeping `q_auto` under a fixed operator moves what a right answer is WORTH, and both
readings agree and gate:

```
    stakes g = q_op - q_auto        0.45     0.35     0.25     0.15     0.05
    success, check 50% vs never   +0.0620  +0.0088  -0.0166  -0.0493  -0.0591
                                     up      equi     equi     inco     down
    success, check 100% vs never  +0.0659  -0.0156  -0.0640  -0.1006  -0.1519
                                     up      equi     down     down     down
    losses,  check 100% vs never  -0.0491  +0.0199  +0.0700  +0.1067  +0.1461
                                    down     inco      up       up       up
```

The verification rate is decided by what a right answer is worth over autonomy, and on this console
the crossing sits near a stakes value of 0.35. Above it, check, and checking everything is as good as
checking half. Below it, do not, and checking everything is the worst arm on the board. Half-checking
is the arm that survives furthest into the low-stakes region, equivalent at 0.35 and 0.25 where full
checking has already gated down, which is what makes a partial rate the robust choice near a crossing
whose location a programme office will not know precisely.

So the interior optimum exists and I registered it against the wrong variable. The honest report is
one retraction and one finding, and the finding belongs to the reviewer's section.

R3 and R4 are still running. Nothing goes to the manuscript until they land, because R3 is the arm
that can kill the throughput explanation for the never-check result outright.

## CR-69 (audit, not a conjecture): is `success` readable at large span?

Raised by CR-66's V1. On the uniform fleet the blind unbounded console is fully collapsed at
twenty-four assets (`served_per_step` 0.0023, `busy_dead` 0.9871, `starved_frac` 0.87 above every
repaired arm) and mission `success` is still 0.9976. Every repair is therefore worth +0.0024 there
and reads "equivalent". Two readings, and they have opposite consequences for the paper:

- **A. The mission genuinely stops depending on the console at scale.** Fleet size substitutes for
  console quality. Sponsor-relevant, and it would mean the queue requirement has a span above which
  it is not worth writing.
- **B. `success` is at its ceiling and cannot resolve anything at that span.** Then every contrast
  this project reports at n_blue >= 16 on `success` is compressed by construction, which is an
  instrument defect of the familiar genus and would touch more than CR-66.

**Discriminator.** Run the published scenario across span with no treatment and read `success`
beside `blue_loss_frac`. If `success` is pinned near 1.0 while `blue_loss_frac` keeps moving, the
mission is being scored on the wrong axis at large span and reading B holds. If both flatten, the
mission really is over by then and reading A holds.

**Falsifier for the concern itself.** If baseline `success` at twenty-four assets is below 0.95,
there is no ceiling and the audit is withdrawn.

### CR-69 ruling: reading B holds, and it is worse than it looked

Baseline `sc_span_control`, no treatment, twelve reps per span:

| n_blue | success | blue_loss_frac | served/step | starved_frac |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 | 0.3333 | 0.7083 | 0.0788 | 0.0000 |
| 6 | 0.5833 | 0.5556 | 0.0928 | 0.0000 |
| 8 | 0.5000 | 0.4479 | 0.0218 | 0.5208 |
| 12 | 0.9167 | 0.2569 | 0.0024 | 0.7500 |
| 16 | **1.0000** | 0.1979 | 0.0024 | 0.8125 |
| 24 | **1.0000** | 0.1076 | 0.0023 | 0.8750 |
| 32 | **1.0000** | 0.0755 | 0.0022 | 0.9062 |

`success` is pinned at exactly 1.0 from sixteen assets up, so it has no resolution there and cannot
be moved by any treatment; `blue_loss_frac` keeps falling by a factor of 2.6 across the same range,
so the fleet is still paying visibly different prices. Reading B. The audit's own falsifier
(baseline success below 0.95 at twenty-four) is not met: it is 1.0000.

**Consequence, and it is a standing rule, not a one-off.** `success` is a censored instrument above
n_blue = 12 on this scenario. Any contrast reported on `success` at a larger span is compressed by
construction and an "equivalent" verdict there means "the instrument could not see", not "the
treatment did nothing". This is the same finding that disqualified `answer_rate_v` for CR-67 in
Round 35, arriving from the other direction: there the treatment moved the denominator, here the
metric has run out of range. `blue_loss_frac` is the readable mission axis at large span.

CR-66's V1 large-span cells are the first casualty and are read accordingly below.

### Instrument defect 18: `busy_*` is a fraction of the console's time on one console only

Found while running CR-69. The six `busy_*` metrics divide accumulated service time by
`total_steps` and the comment above them calls the result the console's utilization. On the BUFFER
console that is right: it refuses to begin a service while it is still busy, so its book never runs
past the clock. On the published RESERVE console it is wrong: that console commits a request to a
slot the moment the request arrives and books forward without bound, so at thirty-two assets it
holds a book 4.74 times longer than the mission and `busy_total` returns 4.7449.

The genus is the usual one. What makes this instance worth a guard is that 4.74 does not look like
an error to anyone who does not know which console produced it; it looks like a number.

**Blast radius: none.** All seven batteries that measure `busy_*` (acuity, bias, capped, fleet,
ghost, ladder, worked) set `queue_model: "buffer"`, and the only tool that reads one
(`make_acuity_numbers.py`) reads the acuity log. Nothing published moves. The trap was live for the
next battery that did not set it.

**Repair.** The six fractions are NaN off the buffer console. The ratio that IS defined on both is
emitted under its own name, `booked_per_step`: service time committed per mission step, equal to
`busy_total` on buffer and the offered-load ratio rho on reserve. Guarded by
`tests/test_busy_denominator.py`, which carries the negative control that the trap was real
(reserve books 1.29 per step at eight assets and 4.74 at thirty-two) and pins the buffer values
that must not have moved.

## CR-70: the console requirement does not stop mattering at scale; mission success stops seeing it

**Where it comes from.** CR-66's V1 reads the requirements ladder against span on `success`. The
free bound is worth +0.1743 at eight assets, +0.0981 at twelve, +0.0366 at sixteen and +0.0024 at
twenty-four, so the natural reading is that the console requirement has a span above which it is not
worth writing. CR-69 then showed that baseline success on this scenario is pinned at exactly 1.0
from sixteen assets up. The decay and the ceiling are the same three cells, and the ladder battery
carries no uncensored mission axis to tell them apart.

**Conjecture.** The ladder's mission value does not decay with span. It is `success` that runs out
of range. Measured on `blue_loss_frac`, which falls smoothly and monotonically across the whole
span axis with no treatment at all, the free bound keeps buying at twenty-four and thirty-two.

**Falsifier, declared before the run.** If `boundOne` against `blindUnbound` on `blue_loss_frac` at
twenty-four assets is `equivalent` or gates in the wrong direction, the conjecture is refuted and
the reading that the console stops mattering at scale stands as CR-66 wrote it.

**Sections.**

- **W1** the four rungs against span (8, 12, 16, 24, 32) on a uniform fleet, reading
  `blue_loss_frac` and `success` side by side so the decay and the ceiling are on one page. Also
  `blue_losses` and `blue_survivors`, because a loss FRACTION on a span sweep changes denominator
  with the sweep even though it does not change with the arm, and the counts say whether the
  fraction is moving because fewer die or because more are present.
- **W2** the same on the two-point fleet, where V2 says the rungs come apart.
- **W3** the discriminator. The ceiling is a property of the scenario's difficulty, not of the
  mission: at `n_red = 8` baseline success runs 0.125 at eight assets to 0.938 at thirty-two and is
  saturated nowhere. Run the identical ladder there. If the `success` gain that vanished at
  twenty-four in W1 REAPPEARS at `n_red = 8`, the vanishing was the instrument. **Falsifier for
  W3:** if the ladder's success gain at twenty-four is still equivalent once the ceiling is lifted,
  the diagnosis is wrong, the decay is real, and CR-70 is retracted whatever W1 says.
- **W4** the ladder against red strength at a fixed large span (twenty-four), which is the same
  question asked as a dose-response instead of at two points, and the section that would catch a
  W3 result that only works at one hand-picked value of `n_red`.

**Pilot disclosed** (12 to 16 reps, one seed, no gate). Baseline `success` / `blue_loss_frac` on the
blind console:

| n_red | n=8 | n=16 | n=24 | n=32 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 4 (published) | 0.500 / 0.469 | **1.000** / 0.227 | **1.000** / 0.109 | **1.000** / 0.068 |
| 8 | 0.125 / 0.891 | 0.562 / 0.508 | 0.938 / 0.305 | 0.938 / 0.234 |
| 12 | 0.000 / 1.000 | 0.000 / 0.910 | 0.125 / 0.750 | 0.500 / 0.541 |

Success is close to a step function of the blue-to-red ratio and has a narrow transition band;
`blue_loss_frac` is smooth and monotone over the whole grid. That is the reason for the conjecture
and it is also its own caution: at `n_red = 12` success is floored rather than ceilinged, and a
battery that ran there would make the opposite mistake.

### CR-66 V2 ruling: one field, two readings, opposite fixes

Two-point fleet, spans 8/12/16/24, full tier, adjacent-rung. This is the section the battery was
built for and it delivered more than the registration asked.

`starved_frac` (the fraction of vehicles that never get an answer at all):

| rung | n=8 | n=12 | n=16 | n=24 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| blind unbounded | 0.5567 | 0.7850 | 0.8448 | 0.8953 |
| bound at one | 0.0037 | 0.0218 | 0.0495 | 0.0956 |
| bound at two | 0.0784 | 0.2439 | 0.3039 | 0.3047 |
| admitting | 0.0031 | 0.1289 | 0.3559 | **0.4452** |
| admitting + EDF | 0.0001 | 0.0009 | 0.0014 | **0.0022** |

Adjacent-rung, gated at every cell shown:

| increment | throughput (served/step) | exclusion (starved_frac) |
|---|---|---|
| bound at one | **+0.0720** gates up | **-0.7997** gates down |
| second slot | **-0.0121** gates down | **+0.2091** gates up |
| the deadline field | **+0.0882** gates up | **+0.1405** gates up |
| the same field, read to order | +0.0002 equivalent | **-0.4430** gates down |

**The law.** On a heterogeneous fleet the deadline field read ONCE is a throughput instrument that
makes exclusion worse; the SAME field read a second time, to order rather than to refuse, is an
exclusion instrument that adds no throughput. Admitting excludes 0.4452 at twenty-four assets
against ordering's 0.0022, a factor of 201, and against the free bound's 0.0956: the informed
console is four and a half times worse on exclusion than the console that knows nothing. A
requirement that says "the console shall use request deadlines" buys whichever of the two the
vendor happens to implement, and the two are the same field at the same cost to field.

This is the **eighth instance of the same-information-opposite-rule genus** and the sharpest one so
far, because the earlier seven contrast two policies. Here there is one policy input, one field, one
integration cost, and the sign of the outcome turns on which line of code reads it.

**Second result, unregistered and worth its own sentence.** The second slot is worse than the first
on every metric at every span: throughput -0.0121 gates down, exclusion +0.2091 gates up, mean
answer rate -0.0133 gates down. Round 35's sizing rule said "round DOWN when uncertain" on
theoretical grounds. It now has direct backing on the fleet where the bound matters.

**Third, and it is the cost side of the law.** Ordering buys exclusion with mean answer rate:
-0.0342 at sixteen and -0.0479 at twenty-four, both gating down. Consistent with the fairness
results already in the paper (`FrEdf*`), reached independently on a different fleet.

**Why the uniform fleet showed nothing.** V1's EDF rung is byte-identical to the admitting rung on
24 of 24 measured cells. With equal deadlines there is nothing to order, so the second reading is
free and useless. Heterogeneity is what switches the second reading on, which is the registered
claim of the battery and is now measured rather than piloted.

## Round 36 rulings, and what acting on them found

**Q2 accepted, and it is a project rule rather than a guard.** An axis is admissible on a cell only
where the REFERENCE arm sits off its bounds by more than the rate floor. The diagnosis is computed
from the reference arm alone and never from the contrast, so it cannot be steered by the result.
The fallback order is fixed once, project-wide, in the sponsor's priority order: `success`, then
`blue_loss_frac`, then time. Both axes are always reported and only primacy switches. The rule is
symmetric: it mutes the `n_red = 12` cells where success is floored at 0.000 exactly as it mutes the
`n_red = 4` cells where it is ceilinged at 1.000. The positive defence is that a saturated axis
answers the sponsor's question identically for every arm, so switching there declines a tautology
rather than picking a winner. Fourth appearance of range validity in this project and the first on a
mission-referenced axis.

**Q3 accepted as (c), and the ungated sentence is cut.** The evidence is weaker than I presented it:
15/32, 19/32 and 16/32 agreeing blocks are coin flips, and the negative mean at twenty-four rides a
3-block tail against 29 dissenting blocks. But the sign is mechanism-predicted, so the consequence
is registered rather than the observation:

> **Waiting exposure.** An admitting console serves more requests, so more vehicles are alive and
> waiting on it; a bounded console drops the overflow to autonomy at once. If that mechanism is
> real, `blue_loss_frac` for `admits` exceeds `boundOne` at n >= 16, gated.

CR-70's W1 adjudicates this for free on runs already submitted. If it gates, the genus row writes
itself with a mechanism attached. If it does not, the inversion is noise and no version of the
sentence ever ships. Nothing ungated touches the manuscript in the meantime.

**Q4 accepted, implemented, and it immediately corrected a paragraph I had already written.**
`blue_exposure` (vehicle-steps alive) accumulates in the mission loop and `loss_intensity`
(losses per vehicle-step at risk) is emitted beside it. The decomposition Fable asked for,
`d(frac) = d(intensity) * exposure + intensity * d(exposure)`, applied to CR-69's own supporting
number, says this:

| n_blue | blue_loss_frac | absolute losses | exposure (veh-steps) | intensity |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 8 | 0.4479 | 3.58 | 8 116 | 0.000486 |
| 16 | 0.1979 | 3.17 | 17 960 | 0.000184 |
| 24 | 0.1076 | 2.58 | 29 112 | 0.000091 |
| 32 | 0.0755 | 2.42 | 40 717 | 0.000060 |

Absolute losses are nearly flat. The fraction falls by 2.6 across the saturated range and the
absolute count by only 1.31, so **most of what the loss fraction reports across a span sweep is a
larger denominator rather than fewer deaths.** CR-69's claim that "the fleet is still paying
visibly different prices" was true but overstated by roughly a factor of two, and the manuscript
paragraph has been corrected to say so.

The distinction that keeps this from voiding CR-70: the caution is about reading the SWEEP, where
the denominator is the sweep variable, and not about reading a CONTRAST, where every arm in a cell
shares one force size. CR-70 compares arms within a cell and is unaffected. The two are printed in
the same column, which is why they get confused, and that is worth one sentence in the paper.

**Q5 accepted in full, including the criticism.** The instrument catalogue freezes at 19 rows except
where a defect blocks a published number. The bridge is the priority.

### CR-66 V3 and V4: the threshold located, and the escape closed

**V3, heterogeneity as the sweep at sixteen assets.** `served_per_step` against patience spread:

| arm | 0 | 3 | 6 | 9 | 12 | 18 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| blind unbounded | 0.0023 | 0.0019 | 0.0020 | 0.0020 | 0.0020 | 0.0020 |
| bound at two | 0.1476 | 0.1427 | 0.1260 | 0.0952 | 0.0623 | *0.0540* |
| admitting | 0.1490 | 0.1490 | 0.1489 | 0.1496 | 0.1491 | *0.1485* |
| admitting + EDF | 0.1490 | 0.1494 | 0.1494 | 0.1500 | 0.1497 | *0.1486* |

The free bound loses 58% of its throughput across the readable range. The deadline field loses
nothing, flat to three decimals. **The value of buying the field is exactly the heterogeneity of the
fleet**, which is the battery's registered claim, now a dose-response rather than two points. The
mechanism is one sentence: a bound refuses by arrival order, and arrival order is uninformative
about a deadline exactly when deadlines vary.

**A censored level, caught before it was written up.** The two-point layout puts the impatient half
at `patience - spread`. At spread 18 that is 2 steps against a mean service of 6.67, so half the
fleet cannot be served by any console whatever it knows, and `boundTwo`, `admits` and `admitsEdf`
all read `starved_frac` exactly 0.5000 there. That is a floor set by the fleet. Reported as "EDF's
advantage disappears at the widest spread" it would have been a false finding; the level is now
flagged by `LdHCensorSpread` and the prose stops at 12. Same family as CR-69 and the second time
today that a range check changed a conclusion.

**V4, how tight the bound has to be, and it closes the obvious escape.** A program office will ask
whether a better-sized bound would do, since sizing is free and a semantic field is not.
`served_per_step` against capacity 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12:

| fleet | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 | 6 | 8 | 12 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| uniform | 0.1461 | **0.1476** | 0.1299 | 0.0993 | 0.0314 | 0.0052 | 0.0029 |
| two-point | **0.0754** | 0.0623 | 0.0609 | 0.0619 | 0.0596 | 0.0369 | 0.0036 |

The optimum tightens from k = 2 on the uniform fleet to k = 1 on the mixed one, which is direct
evidence for rounding k* DOWN. And the best achievable free bound on the mixed fleet delivers
0.0754 against the field's 0.1491: **at its own optimum the bound gets 51% of what the field gets,**
so it cannot be sized out of its deficit. That is the number that decides whether the field is worth
specifying.

All of V1 through V4 is now in the manuscript as two paragraphs in the requirements passage, keyed
adjacent-rung with the whole-ladder gap quoted once, per Round 35.

### CR-68 ruling: the topology carries something the geometry does not, for one assumed number

**X1, the design grid.** Five arms (star and chain topologies at three commanded spreads) crossed
with four spans, 20 cells. Round 36's axis rule removes the 4 cells where mission success is against
a bound, leaving 16. Rank correlation of each reading against mission success:

| reading | rho | kind |
|---|---|---|
| `fleet_faults_mean` | **+0.719** | graph |
| `station_cross_mean` | -0.621 | geometric, **referenced to the commanded station** |
| `station_err_mean` | -0.591 | geometric, **referenced to the commanded station** |
| `fleet_kappa_mean` | +0.579 | graph |
| `fleet_fiedler_mean` | +0.556 | graph |
| `fleet_disconnected_frac` | -0.528 | graph |
| `fleet_cross_mean` | -0.406 | geometric |
| `fleet_spread_mean` | **+/-0.347** | geometric, **CR-60's recommendation** |

Every reading carries its predicted sign. The two station-referenced readings are excluded from the
comparison rather than beaten by it: the treatment on this grid IS the commanded geometry, so a
station-referenced reading looks good here by construction. That is the reference-set-by-treatment
failure arriving in the one place where it would have looked like a result.

Among the readings not referenced to what the treatment sets, **the graph readings beat the
geometric ones**, and the tolerated-fault count beats CR-60's recommended RMS spread by a factor of
1.8 in rank correlation. This qualifies a recommendation the paper already makes.

**The monotone objection, answered.** `faults` is floor((kappa-1)/2), which is monotone in kappa, so
a reader will say it cannot know more. It does, because the floor is applied PER SAMPLED STEP and
then averaged: the mean of the floor is not the floor of the mean, and the two rank this grid at
rho = 0.825 rather than at 1.000. The count answers how often the formation could survive a fault;
mean connectivity answers how connected it was on average.

**The assumed-parameter objection, answered on the same runs.** A graph reading needs a peer-link
range assumed and a distance needs nothing. Sweeping that range from 150 to 1000 m moves the
readings by an order of magnitude and moves what they RANK by very little: the fault count
reproduces its own ordering at rho between +0.700 and +0.900 across the whole sweep. The control
that makes this readable is that mission success is identical to 0.0000 across the same sweep,
because `graph_range` is an observer's parameter that touches nothing in the simulation. **The price
of the better reading is a number that has to be declared and does not have to be right.**

**Scoring caveat, stated in the macro names and in the prose.** This is an ORDERING claim scored by
rank correlation over a design grid, not a paired gate. The project's gate answers "did this arm
move the metric" and no amount of it answers "does this reading rank the design space the way the
mission does". Both are in the paper and they are not interchangeable.

### CR-67 ruling: held, and sharper on the axis Round 35 required

Primary reading `blue_loss_frac` throughout, per Round 35's disqualification of `answer_rate_v`
(the treatment moves its denominator). An eightfold re-ask hold, against the base cadence, within
each console, at spans 6/8/12/16 on a clear link:

| console | n=6 | n=8 | n=12 | n=16 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| blind (cannot refuse) | +0.0145 equiv | **-0.0510 gates down** | +0.0034 equiv | -0.0086 inconc |
| bounded at k* | **+0.0287 up** | **+0.0328 up** | **+0.0251 up** | **+0.0332 up** |
| admitting | +0.0000 | +0.0000 | +0.0000 | +0.0000 (0/32 blocks, all four) |

Sharper than the pilot in a way that matters. The benefit on the blind console is at ONE span, the
collapse edge; the harm on the bounded console is at ALL FOUR. Since the bound is this paper's
headline recommendation, the honest statement is not "positive here, negative there" but **once you
take the cheap console fix, the fleet-side lever becomes a liability.**

The admitting console's zero is structural, not measured-small: that console overrides the vehicle's
hold with `min(2*lat, patience)`, so `reask_hold` never reaches the code that would apply it.

**W3, the dose-response, gives the recommendation a number.** Sweeping the hold 1, 2, 4, 8, 16 at
eight assets on C2, the loss-minimising hold is **2 on the blind console, 1 on the bounded one and 1
on the admitting one.** Back off twice on a console that is collapsing; do not back off at all on a
console that has been fixed.

**W5 isolates the mechanism with a control instead of an argument.** At `service_rate = 1.2`, where
the console is not load-limited, blind and admitting are **byte-identical on every cell** (a console
with no queue never refuses), and the lever is pure cost: +0.0989 blue loss at four assets, gates
up. So the fleet-side change buys congestion relief and nothing else, and where there is no
congestion there is only its cost.

**Seventh instance of the same-information-opposite-rule genus**, and the one with the clearest
procurement consequence: damping demand at the vehicle and refusing it at the console are
substitutes, doing both is worse than doing either, and the vehicle cannot see which console it is
talking to. The cheapest item in the paper is the only one whose sign is somebody else's decision.

### CR-64 ruling: the exclusion fix is a counter, not a field, and it is void without refusal

**T1, admitting console, two-point fleet, spans 4 to 24, reference = arrival order.** `round_robin`
serves whoever has been served least and reads NOTHING about the request: no deadline, no timestamp,
no difficulty, only a counter the console already keeps about itself.

| rule | exclusion at n=24 | vs arrival order | throughput vs arrival order |
|---|---|---|---|
| arrival order (fcfs) | 0.4242 | | |
| round robin (free) | 0.0001 | **-0.4240 gates down** | +0.0233 gates up |
| earliest deadline (a field) | 0.0002 | **-0.4240 gates down** | +0.0471 gates up |
| random | 0.0557 | -0.3684 gates down | -0.0082 equivalent |

Compared directly, `round_robin` against `edf`: the largest exclusion gap at any span is 0.0000 to
four decimals. **The free counter matches the deadline field on exclusion.** The gate cannot certify
that and the prose says so: `edf` reads exactly zero at four of six spans, so a relative margin
against it is undefined and the zero-base guard fires. The supported statement is the weaker and
sufficient one, that the two leave the same number of vehicles unanswered.

Where they part is throughput: the counter gives up -0.0092 at sixteen and -0.0239 at twenty-four,
both gating down. **So the requirement splits along the two currencies. Exclusion is fixed by not
privileging arrival order, and the cheapest way to stop is a counter needing no message change.
Throughput is bought by the deadline field, and only on a fleet whose deadlines differ.**

This refines CR-66's V2 in the direction that helps a program office. V2 priced the exclusion fix as
a second reading of the deadline field. It does not have to be bought that way at all.

**T2, the precondition, and it is the amendment that inverted the original conjecture.** On a
console that cannot refuse: arrival order leaves 0.8897 unanswered, the free counter leaves 0.8897,
the same number, the deadline field leaves 0.9659, which is worse than doing nothing, and a coin
flip at 0.7105 beats all three. Ordering is not a lever on a console with no capacity to allocate;
it is a way of choosing who is denied.

**T6, the control**, where there is no scheduling problem: every rule reads at most 0.0026 and none
can win. Fires correctly.

## CR-65 SCORED. THE REPAIR REMOVES ALL OF THE WASTE AND RECLAIMS NONE OF THE LOSS

Job 9459739, 773 rows, 32 blocks at 64 replications, scored against the registration and its three
amendments. Every registered prediction holds. The battery is kept. What follows is the reading, and
then the instrument defect it exposed, which is the more important half.

**U1 as registered.** Dropping the ghost is free in information and it removes the entire stale
clock. It also buys almost nothing, because the dead clock is conserved: `busy_late` rises by
exactly what `busy_stale` loses, +0.9670 at twenty-four assets against -0.9671. The console is still
busy 98.7 percent of the time producing no answer. It has stopped producing no answer on superseded
requests and started producing no answer on fresh ones.

The number that says it plainly, as a share of what refusing the work buys:

```
    span   works    drops   boundTwo  admits  | drop gains  refuse gains  drop/refuse
      6   0.0956   0.0960    0.1167   0.1172  |   +0.0004      +0.0216        1.7%
      8   0.0411   0.0477    0.1316   0.1338  |   +0.0067      +0.0927        7.2%
     12   0.0028   0.0149    0.1442   0.1470  |   +0.0121      +0.1442        8.4%
     16   0.0023   0.0039    0.1476   0.1490  |   +0.0015      +0.1467        1.0%
     24   0.0023   0.0023    0.1491   0.1493  |   +0.0000      +0.1470        0.0%
```

The free repair has an interior window. It is worth nothing at six assets because there are no
ghosts to drop, rises to 8.4 percent of refusal's value at twelve, and decays to exactly zero at
twenty-four. At twenty-four it removes 0.9671 of the console's clock from the stale bucket and
reclaims 0.0001 of the dead clock, which is one part in ten thousand of the loss the waste was
named for.

**So the operational statement is a warning about a class of intervention rather than a
recommendation.** An efficiency repair is measured by the waste it removes; that measurement cannot
fail, because the repair is defined by removing it. What decides whether it is worth fielding is the
loss it reclaims, and above saturation that is zero for any efficiency repair whatsoever. At
twenty-four assets the console is offered 0.33 requests per step against a service capacity near
0.15. At sixty-five-fold over-commitment nothing that makes the operator more efficient can matter,
because efficiency is not the binding constraint. Only refusal is. Efficiency repairs and admission
repairs are not substitutes and they do not trade off: below saturation the first works, above it
only the second does.

**U2, and it answers the question CR-64 left open.** On a blind console that cannot refuse, dropping
the ghost INVERTS the discipline ordering rather than shifting it. Against `fcfsWorks` on
`answer_rate_v` at eight assets, the works arms rank round robin +0.0290, deadline order +0.0127,
arrival order 0; the drops arms rank arrival order +0.3226, round robin +0.2067, deadline order
+0.1172. First come first served goes from worst rule to best rule when the ghosts are dropped. So
CR-64's inversion is a property of what a blind buffer CONTAINS, not of the rules that order it.

**U3, both halves, as registered, with one correction to the sweep's own advertised range.** At
sixteen assets `busy_stale` spans 0.9546 to 0.9577, a relative span of 0.3 percent: the registered
equivalence holds and its falsifier does not fire. The range over which it holds is THIRTY-TWO fold
and not the sixty-four the sweep was written to cover. Review caught it and the log confirms it:
`reask_hold` 0.25 and 0.5 are byte-identical in all twenty-eight arm-by-metric cells at both spans,
because the hold quantizes to the same integer number of steps. The sweep has six distinct doses,
not seven, and one of its advertised octaves does not exist. `_flat_reference` cannot see this: it
demands the WHOLE row be constant, and here only an adjacent pair is. A control that flatters its
own flatness with a duplicated dose is a control that has not been run at the range it claims. At eight assets it gates down from 0.2065 to 0.0066, and
`served_per_step` is non-monotone with its maximum at a hold of 2.0, rising from 0.0395 and falling
back to 0.0498 at sixteen. The registered "maximum at or below fourfold" holds. The fleet-side
repair is real, it is free, and it has a size: backing off as far as possible is wrong.

**U4 and U5 and U6 fire correctly as controls.** Where there are no ghosts every arm is equivalent
on every reading. The comms ladder shrinks the problem as predicted, because a degraded link is
accidental admission control.

### DEFECT 19, AND IT IS NOT THE SAME GENUS

`busy_stale` gave three interventions that differ by two orders of magnitude in value an IDENTICAL
reading. Not similar: the per-block vectors of `dropsGhost`, `boundTwo` and `admits` are
byte-identical in all thirty-two blocks at all five spans, every value exactly zero, so the three
arms carry the same effect to four decimals and the same t to the last digit, t = -10436 at
twenty-four assets with 32/32 sign consistency. On `busy_dead` the same three arms separate
completely, one equivalent and two gating down by 0.98.

It did this three times in one battery: it also collapsed the discipline ordering in U2, where the
three drop arms are likewise byte-identical, and it would have reported the U3 cadence sweep as
inert on a reading where `busy_dead` moves seventy-fold.

The mechanism is that `stale_check = "before"` is DEFINED as never working a stale request, and
`busy_stale` counts clock spent working stale requests. The metric is the negation of the arm's own
definition. Its value is entailed rather than measured, and the other two arms reach the same zero
by a second route, since nothing survives in a bounded or admitting buffer long enough to go stale.

**None of the three guards fires and the Round 36 rule declares the axis admissible.**
`_censored_cells` wants a NaN. `_flat_reference` and `_zero_base` both audit the REFERENCE arm, and
the reference sits at 0.94 to 0.97, nowhere near a bound. Round 36 computes admissibility from the
reference arm alone, deliberately, because the Round 18 rule forbids letting a treatment set its own
audit. So Round 36 covers the case where the reference is pinned and the treatment had no room to
move, and is blind to the case where several treatments are pinned at the opposite bound and cannot
be ranked against each other. Those are different questions and the manuscript currently conflates
them.

This is why it may not belong to the instrument genus at all. The eighteen instances so far are each
a convention correctly derived for a first use case and carried into a second where it does not
hold, and each could in principle be revealed by running somewhere else: defect 18's success ceiling
is a fact about one scenario's physics and a different scenario relaxes it. This one is entailed by a
definition, so no scenario relaxes it and no amount of replication reveals it. More replication makes
it look BETTER, because the t statistic grows without bound while the information stays at zero.

Sent to review as Round 37 Q1 before any repair is written, because the obvious fix, auditing the
treatment arms for pinning, appears to reintroduce the Round 18 defect, and because the better fix
may be a registration-time check on the arm's DEFINITION rather than any statistic at all.

### ROUND 37 RULINGS. THE DEFECT IS REAL, MY DIAGNOSIS OF IT WAS NOT

Review verified the mechanism at the code level and then corrected three things in the account I
wrote above. The corrections matter because each one points at a different repair.

**It failed once, not three times.** U1's `dropsGhost`, U2's three drop arms and U3's drop arms are
one entailment with three exposures, not three failures. `stale_check = "before"` forecloses the
`t_stale` accumulator, and every reading downstream of that is the same fact seen again. Counting
exposures as failures makes the axis look statistically treacherous when it is definitionally
degenerate, and that misdiagnosis points at a statistical repair when the right one is a registry.

**The three zeros are not epistemically alike.** Only `dropsGhost` reaches zero by entailment.
`boundTwo` and `admits` reach it empirically and could have been otherwise: that the admitting
console holds `busy_stale` at zero WHILE serving 0.149 per step is a real and publishable fact. Any
repair that treats "pinned" uniformly throws two informative zeros away with the tautological one.
That kills the set-wide mute I was drifting toward.

**Every contrast that was actually computed is true, and correctly gated.** "Dropping the ghost
takes `busy_stale` from 0.94 to zero" is registered claim (a) of CR-65 and it is fine as a
manipulation check. What failed is a claim NOBODY EVER COMPUTED: the ranking among the three
treatments was read off three separate contrasts against a common reference, and the ranking's own
contrast was never formed. No guard fired because no guard was ever pointed at the comparison being
consumed. This is the sharpest correction of the three and it dissolves most of my worry about the
tooling: run that comparison through the existing machinery and `paired()` returns t = 0 on
byte-identical arms by explicit design, the TOST margin is zero, and both `_zero_base` and
`_flat_reference` fire. **The tools already refuse this claim, loudly and twice. What was missing
was the obligation to ask them.**

So the repair is not a new statistic. It is two sentences:

1. **Admissibility attaches to a CONTRAST**, an ordered pair of the claim's own reference and the
   claim's arm, computed from that reference alone. Round 36 is the special case where the claim's
   reference is the battery's designated reference. Every gated claim now in the manuscript is of
   that form, so the restatement moves no landed verdict; the rewrite I feared is small.
2. **Any cross-arm reading, an ordering, an equivalence, a "these two differ by X" among
   treatments, must be COMPUTED as a contrast among those arms.** Once formed, rule 1 applies to it
   automatically and in the pinned case it refuses.

And the observation that belongs in the paper rather than only in this log: Round 36's scope clause,
"computed from the reference arm alone", is itself a convention correctly derived for its first use
case, designated-reference batteries, and carried silently into a second, cross-arm readings, where
"the reference arm" has no referent. **The rule against the instrument genus contains an instance of
the instrument genus.**

**My "property of a set" defence does not survive, and the correct defence is a different one.**
Round 18 does not forbid "one arm wrote the audit". It forbids any causal path from outcomes under
audit into the audit, because every such path is a selection channel. Set membership is chosen by
the registrant, and pinning is easy to engineer, since any prohibition-defined arm pins its own
metric; so a registrant could demote an unfavourable axis by adding a decoy arm built to pin.
Multiplicity is more surface, not a firewall. What survives is narrower and rests on vacuity rather
than on symmetry: the check is PAIRWISE rather than set wide, it is REFUSAL ONLY so it can withhold
a cross-arm verdict but never grant or reverse one and never touches an arm-versus-reference
contrast, and the fallback axis order stays fixed in advance so a refusal cannot steer the reading.
Under those three restrictions the channel carries nothing, because refusing to rank two
byte-identical vectors suppresses no true statement. There is no payoff to steer toward.

**It is a genus of its own, and the ledger's own test decides it.** The manuscript defines the
instrument genus by the question that exposes every instance: ask what the denominator counts, then
ask whether the arm changed it. Ask that here and the answer is nothing, because the denominator,
`total_steps`, is innocent. The exposing question for this defect is a different one: can this
metric's source event occur at all under this arm's definition? That is a reachability question,
answerable at registration with no data, and it lands in a different phase with a different guard
class. The crisp difference, and it is the sentence for the paper: **every one of the eighteen
instrument instances produced a WRONG number. The entailed cell produces only TRUE ones.**
`busy_stale` really is zero, the contrast really is -0.9671, the t really is -10437. Nothing is
miscomputed, which is exactly why no amount of replication, range checking or benching can touch it:
the reading is a theorem, and theorems replicate perfectly. Defect 18's 4.74 was CONTINGENTLY wrong
and another console makes it right. This cell is NECESSARILY uninformative and no scenario, seed or
span changes it.

**And the genus does not arrive as a singleton.** Review predicted a dual entailment in the same
log and it is there: `stale_dropped_frac` is exactly zero on the works arms, because its counter's
only increment site sits inside the `before` branch. CR-67's W5 is a third instance, caught by hand
at the time and correctly reported as structural. A mechanical sweep of all seventy landed logs then
put a number on it: an arm is pinned at a structural value in **66 of 623 section-by-metric
readings, one in ten**, and in **42 of them two or more arms are pinned at the SAME value**, so
those pairs cannot be ordered on that axis at all. That is not exotic. It is the shadow of the
project's best architectural decision, since every opt-in mechanism behind a parameter gate entails
zeros on its own metrics when it is off.

**Three guards added, all announcements rather than verdict changes, because the scoring surface is
frozen and a margin changed after the fact would move landed verdicts silently.** `_pinned_arm`
announces any arm byte-identical in every block at every sweep point. `_duplicate_dose` announces
adjacent sweep points that are one dose wearing two labels, which is how the sixty-four-fold claim
above got corrected to thirty-two. `_thin_base` announces a reference that is nonzero but under a
hundredth, where the relative margin has collapsed toward nothing: at six assets `worksGhost` reads
0.000541 on `busy_stale`, so the margin is 0.0000325 and a repair worth half a thousandth of a
percent of operator time would gate as material with a large t. `_zero_base` demands exactly zero
and cannot see it.

## CR-72, REGISTERED. AN EXPLORATION CONSTANT IS DENOMINATED IN THE METRIC'S NOISE

**Verdict:** M1 refuted, M2 refuted, M3 not scorable (no gradeable success problem)

Registered before the search benchmark's run stage, with the pilot disclosed, because the pilot is
what prompted it and hiding that would be dishonest.

Textbook UCB1 adds `c * sqrt(2 log t / n)` to each arm's mean, and that constant is derived for
rewards bounded in the unit interval. Mission metrics are not bounded there and, more to the point,
what the bonus has to be commensurate with is not the metric's LEVEL but the GAP between the designs
being told apart. On this simulator those gaps are small in absolute terms:

```
    metric              gap      per-mission sd    UCB1 bonus at 8 pulls   bonus / gap
    served_per_step   0.00641        0.0193               1.020              159x
    success           0.03333        0.4325               1.020               31x
    blue_loss_frac    0.01917        0.2468               1.020               53x
```

An allocator whose bonus exceeds its signal by two orders of magnitude always selects the
least-pulled arm. So unscaled UCB on this benchmark is not exploring too eagerly. It has stopped
being UCB and become uniform allocation with an argmax stapled to the end, which is the grid
baseline under another name. Had the benchmark shipped that way it would have reported "the bandit
ties the grid", and the tie would have been a fact about units.

**The pilot, disclosed.** A surrogate with the measured scale, means from the landed ladder table
and a per-mission sd of 0.019, budget 400, counting missions per design:

```
    method      allocation                        contenders   hopeless   ratio
    ucb_raw     [64, 64, 64, 56, 56, 48, 48]         128          96      1.33
    ucb         [24, 320, 16, 16, 8, 8, 8]           344          16     21.50
    thompson    [144, 200, 16, 16, 8, 8, 8]          344          16     21.50
```

`ucb_raw` spends forty-eight missions on a design worth 0.0029 while the best is worth 0.1476. All
three return the right design on a problem this easy, so the difference is invisible in the headline
and decisive in the budget.

The repair carries the bonus in units of the estimate's own standard error, which is the treatment
`thompson` already gave its posterior and which makes the two comparable for the first time. The
unscaled version is KEPT as `ucb_raw`, a control rather than a recommendation, because the effect is
worth showing rather than asserting.

**Registered predictions.**

**M1.** `ucb_raw` is equivalent to `grid` on both regret and found-rate at every budget on every
problem whose answer key resolves. FALSIFIER: it gates away from `grid` on either, which would mean
the unscaled bonus is doing something after all and the degeneracy argument is wrong.

**M2.** `ucb` gates up against `ucb_raw` on regret at the middle of each problem's budget ladder.
FALSIFIER: equivalent or worse. Scaling the bonus is then not the repair, and the honest conclusion
is that this design space is too small for any allocator to beat enumeration, which is a real result
about mission design and not a failure of the benchmark.

**M3, and it is the one this conjecture exists for.** The advantage of `ucb` over `ucb_raw` is
LARGER on the axes whose gap-to-spread ratio is worse. Ranked by `bonus / gap` above, the ordering
is served_per_step, then blue_loss_frac, then success. FALSIFIER: no ordering, or the reverse one.
The claim would then be that scaling helps, without the stated reason, and it would ship as the
weaker claim.

**The general form, and why it belongs in the second paper rather than in a footnote.** This is the
paper's own thesis arriving INSIDE a method rather than between two of them. If an exploration
constant is denominated in the metric's noise, then a bandit tuned on one mission metric is
mistuned on another by whatever ratio separates their spreads, and no amount of care in choosing the
optimizer substitutes for knowing the axis it will be run on. The same argument applies to any
method carrying a scale-bearing hyperparameter, which includes the length scale of the Gaussian
process, and that is checked as an ablation rather than assumed.

### CR-72 AMENDED BEFORE ITS RUN, AND THE AMENDMENT WEAKENS IT

The GP ablation CR-72 promised was run, and then a surrogate pilot across all three axes was run,
and the pilot disagrees with two of the three registered predictions. It is recorded here, before
the benchmark lands, because a prediction quietly softened after the fact is not a prediction.

**The ablation first, because it confirms what it was asked to.** Expected improvement carries two
scale-bearing hyperparameters and both are wrong in the same way UCB's constant was. A unit
amplitude RBF prior asserts the response varies with standard deviation one; the true design values
on delivered service have a spread of 0.056, so the prior claims eighteen times more signal than
exists. And the fixed observation noise of 0.01 sits against a per-chunk noise of 0.153 on mission
success, so the surrogate is told the data are fifteen times cleaner than they are and interpolates
noise as signal. The two errors push the acquisition in opposite directions and neither cancels.
Standardizing the response puts both in units the data has, which is what a Bayesian optimization
package does in its defaults. **That this implementation is hand rolled is what made the defect
visible.** A package would have standardized silently, and the benchmark would have reported a
number about the package's defaults while appearing to report one about the method. `gp_raw` keeps
the unstandardized version as a control, exactly as `ucb_raw` does.

**The pilot, disclosed, and it is not what CR-72 predicted.** True values from the landed ladder
table, rescaled to each axis's measured spread and given that axis's measured per-mission noise, so
the design ranking is identical in all three blocks by construction and the only thing varying is
the scale. Budget 400 missions, 60 replicates, seven designs. Fraction returning the true best:

```
                       served            success             loss
                   found  regret     found  regret     found  regret
    grid            0.65  0.0005      0.33  0.0101      0.48  0.0077
    random          0.62  0.0011      0.30  0.0168      0.42  0.0094
    ucb_raw         0.70  0.0005      0.27  0.0139      0.65  0.0050
    ucb             0.73  0.0004      0.43  0.0116      0.45  0.0065
    thompson        0.80  0.0003      0.45  0.0110      0.60  0.0044
    gp_raw          0.73  0.0004      0.47  0.0103      0.55  0.0053
    gp_ei           0.75  0.0004      0.33  0.0139      0.42  0.0072
```

**M2 fails on the loss axis and M3 fails outright.** Scaling helps UCB on delivered service, 0.73
against 0.70, and helps it a great deal on success, 0.43 against 0.27. On loss it HURTS, 0.45
against 0.65, and the unscaled version has the lower regret there too. M3 predicted the advantage
would be ordered by the bonus-to-gap ratio, which ranks the axes served, loss, success. The observed
ordering is success, served, loss, with the sign flipped on the last. Standardizing the GP shows the
same pattern: it helps on delivered service and hurts on both noisier axes.

**The mechanism I now believe, stated so it can be killed too.** The loss axis has a true design
spread of 0.160 against a per-mission noise of 0.247, so noise exceeds signal. When noise dominates,
an adaptive allocator's early commitment is a liability rather than an advantage: it concentrates on
whichever arm drew well first and cannot recover, while uniform allocation keeps every arm's
estimate improving and is close to minimax optimal in that regime. So scaling the bonus is not
uniformly good. It makes an allocator adaptive, and adaptivity itself is what fails when the axis
cannot support it.

If that survives the real benchmark, the finding is sharper than the one registered and it is the
same thesis one level down: the metric does not merely set the price of a search, it decides whether
adaptive search is worth doing at all. CR-72's headline becomes conditional, and the conditional is
the noise-to-signal ratio of the axis rather than anything about the optimizer.

**Two cautions on the pilot, and they are the reason it is a pilot.** The surrogate draws Gaussian
noise; mission success is nearly Bernoulli and loss fraction is a bounded ratio, and neither is
Gaussian in the tail that decides an early commitment. And sixty replicates put the standard error
on these proportions near 0.06, so the 0.03 differences in the served column are not readable and
only the 0.16 and 0.20 swings are. The benchmark settles it. This is registered so that whichever
way it goes, it was written down first.

## CR-70 SCORED. THE REQUIREMENT DOES NOT STOP MATTERING; THE INSTRUMENT STOPS SEEING IT

Job 9460876, 467 rows, 32 blocks at 64 replications. Two falsifiers were registered, one for the
conjecture and one for the diagnosis, and neither fires. The battery is kept.

**The conjecture.** CR-66's V1 read the requirements ladder against span on mission success and
watched the free bound's value decay from +0.1743 at eight assets to +0.0024 at twenty-four. CR-69
then found baseline success pinned at exactly 1.0 from sixteen assets up on this scenario. The decay
and the ceiling are the same three cells, and the ladder battery had no uncensored mission axis to
tell them apart.

On the uncensored axis the bound is still there at every span:

```
    n_blue    success (boundOne)          blue_loss_frac (boundOne)
      8       +0.1743  gates up             -0.1194  gates down
     12       +0.0981  gates up             -0.0737  gates down
     16       +0.0366  equivalent           -0.0497  gates down
     24       +0.0024  equivalent           -0.0103  gates down
     32       +0.0000  equivalent, 0/32     -0.0064  gates down
```

At twenty-four assets mission success reports the free bound as making no difference, +0.0024 with
four of thirty-two blocks agreeing, while blue loss reports it gating down at t = -4.06. At
thirty-two success reads exactly +0.0000 with ZERO blocks agreeing, which is not a null but an
instrument with nothing left to move, and loss still gates. The registered falsifier was
`boundOne` against `blindUnbound` on `blue_loss_frac` at twenty-four reading equivalent or gating
the wrong way. It gates down. **The conjecture holds.**

**The diagnosis, and this is the half that makes it more than a metric preference.** A reader can
always answer that the loss axis is simply more sensitive and the effect really did decay. W3
settles it by lifting the ceiling rather than changing the axis: the same ladder at `n_red = 8`,
where baseline success is no longer pinned. Success then SEES the bound again at the span where it
had gone quiet, +0.0889 at twenty-four with t = +6.93 and 29 of 32 blocks, against +0.0024 and 4 of
32 at the same span with the ceiling in place. The registered falsifier for the diagnosis was that
success remain equivalent at twenty-four once the ceiling was lifted, in which case the decay was
real and CR-70 was to be retracted whatever W1 said. It does not remain equivalent. **The decay was
the ceiling.**

**What this is worth to a program office, which is the reason it was run.** A test program that
reports a capability as making no difference at the top of its intended range is making two claims
and usually defending one. The first is that the capability did not help. The second, unstated, is
that the test could have seen it if it had. Here the second is false at twenty-four assets and
provably false at thirty-two, where the metric has no range left at all, and the same experiment run
one notch harder recovers the effect at full strength. The check is cheap: run the baseline across
the range with no treatment and see whether the metric still moves.

**One guard fired and it was right to.** `admits` and `admitsEdf` are byte-identical in all thirty
cells of W1 and all twenty of W3, and separate in twenty-three of twenty-five cells of W2. The cause
is physics rather than a dead knob: W1 and W3 run a UNIFORM fleet, where every vehicle carries the
same deadline, so earliest-deadline order and arrival order are the same permutation. W2's two-point
fleet is where they come apart. CR-70 ranks neither arm against the other, so nothing here rests on
it, but the pattern is the pairwise case `_pinned_arm` cannot see, and `_identical_arms` is added to
announce it: two arms that both move and move together exactly mean whatever separates them never
reached the code, and the reason has to be stated rather than assumed.

## CR-73: what a benchmark's answer key costs, and whether argmax is the right thing to grade

**Verdict:** A measured, B retracted, C holds narrowly (2 of 9 methods separate, in opposite directions)

**Registered before the head-to-head runs, from the truth tables alone.** The tables are the answer
key for paper two's search benchmark: thirty problems, each an operating point crossed with a
metric, each design estimated on common random numbers at a per-metric replication count (six
hundred on delivered service, two thousand on mission success, eight thousand on blue loss).

**A, and this part is measured, not conjectured.** Establishing an answer key by argmax is a
property of the (metric, operating point) pair, and at those counts most pairs do not have one.
Twelve of thirty problems resolve their own top two at two standard errors. On delivered service
nine of twelve resolve; on blue loss three of ten; on mission success **zero of eight**. Extrapolating
from the achieved standard error, the median unresolved problem would need about ten thousand
missions per design and the worst of the success problems three hundred forty thousand. One loss
problem, `disc_s8_loss`, separates its top two by nine parts in one hundred thousand and would need
fifty-two million.

**The genus-two check, run first, because non-resolution is exactly the shape that is sometimes
entailed.** If success were pinned at its ceiling the tie would be a theorem and not a cost. It is
not pinned. `qcap_s8_succ` spans 0.156 from best design to worst with the best at 0.8075. The
exception is `disc_s16_succ`, best 0.9895 and spanning 0.0025, which is ceiling-adjacent and is
reported as such rather than as a replication cost.

> **Corrected 2026-08-26.** This paragraph originally added that "six of the other seven success
> problems have comparable room", which is false. The eight spans run 0.156, 0.134, 0.087, 0.041,
> 0.027, 0.024, 0.021 and 0.003; only one is comparable to the widest. The claim was written from
> one remembered number and nothing generated it, so nothing could find it stale. The conclusion
> survives on a test that is generated: on 7 of the 8 success problems the design axis moves the
> metric further than that problem's own key can resolve, by at least a factor of 1.3, so the tie
> between the top two designs is not entailed by the ceiling.

**B, the consequence, and the reason this is worth a section rather than a footnote.** The spread
across the design set and the gap between the top two are different quantities, and here they differ
by a lot: the median problem moves its metric 8.9 times further from best design to worst than the
best two designs differ from each other, and the ratio reaches 283. Choosing a design well is worth
a great deal and is cheap to do; proving which single design is best is worth little and is often
unaffordable. So argmax recovery is the wrong grading rule and normalized regret, the fraction of
the achievable spread a method captured, is the right one.

**Falsifier for B.** If the ranking of methods under regret agrees with the ranking under argmax
recovery on the twelve resolvable problems, at Spearman above 0.9 or the same winner in ten of
twelve, then the distinction changes no conclusion and B is retracted as academic.

**C, which is what makes B pay.** Regret does not need the top two separated. It needs the found
design to be distinguishable from the best one, and since the spread is about nine times the gap,
that is roughly eighty times cheaper. So the eighteen problems dropped for having no gradeable
argmax should be gradeable under regret, and the benchmark should run on all thirty.

**Falsifier for C.** If on those eighteen no method separates from `random` under regret at the full
tier, |t| above 1.70 with sixty percent sign consistency paired by problem, then they carry no
information under regret either and the drop stands.

Registered so that whichever way each of the three goes, it was written down first.

## CR-74: the ceiling account, made quantitative on data already in hand

**Verdict:** post hoc account, fitted with an out-of-sample check, not a prediction

**Status, stated first because it governs how the rest reads.** This is POST HOC. CR-70 registered a
qualitative discriminator and it held. What follows was not registered in advance: it is a model
fitted to batteries that had already run, and it is reported as a fitted account with an
out-of-sample check rather than as a prediction that survived a falsifier. The distinction is the
whole reason this project keeps a registration log, so it is not blurred here.

**What was sitting unscored.** The CR-70 battery ran four sections and only three were read. W4 is
the ladder against opposing strength at a fixed large span, the section registered precisely as the
guard against a W3 result that only works at one hand-picked value of `n_red`. It ran, it is in the
log, and it was never surveyed. Scored now, it is monotone on both axes: the backlog bound's effect
on mission success reads +0.0024 equivalent at four opposing units, +0.0161 equivalent at six, then
gates at +0.0889, +0.1411 and +0.1372 at eight, ten and twelve, while blue loss gates down at every
dose from -0.0103 to -0.0820. The recovery is therefore not an artefact of one chosen operating
point. It is a dose-response, and the guard the registration asked for did its job.

**The model.** Treat mission success as a threshold reading of a latent margin and let a capability
add a constant to that margin. Then the effect a test reports at baseline `b` is
`Phi(Phi^-1(b) + delta) - b`: one parameter per capability, and no freedom at all in the shape.

**The fit.** One `delta` across twenty cells spanning four sections, two different knobs (span and
opposing strength), two scenarios, and baselines from 0.079 to 1.000 gives `delta = 0.454` for the
backlog bound, R-squared 0.935, RMS residual 0.017 against effects running to 0.188. Section mean
residuals are +0.0073, -0.0050, +0.0089 and -0.0153, so which knob moved the baseline adds nothing.

**The out-of-sample check, which is what makes a post-hoc fit worth reporting.** Fitting `delta` on
three sections and predicting the fourth gives pooled R-squared 0.888 and RMS 0.023, with `delta`
between 0.420 and 0.494 across the four held-out folds. The admission rule, a different capability
on the same axis, gives `delta = 0.394`, pooled out-of-sample R-squared 0.768, folds between 0.364
and 0.448. Two capabilities, different constants, the same curve: the compression belongs to the
metric and the constant belongs to the capability.

**Why it is worth a section rather than a footnote.** The model predicts the NULLS. It says a test
run where the baseline sits at 0.998 will report +0.002 for a capability worth 0.45 on the latent
scale, and that the reading carries no information about the capability at all. So a program office
can measure a capability once where the metric has room and then compute what a test will report
anywhere else, including computing in advance which planned tests cannot answer the question they
are being run to answer. That is a cheaper and more useful thing than another battery.

**What it does not establish, kept explicit.** A one-parameter probit shift is the simplest account
that fits, not the only one that could; the twenty cells are four sections of one simulator on one
scenario family; and the fit and its cross-validation share those cells, so the honest next step is
a section registered in advance whose baseline is chosen to make the model's prediction sharp, with
the predicted effect written down before it runs.

## CR-75: the ceiling model as a registered prediction with no free parameters

**Verdict:** RETRACTED on falsifier two (shape); falsifier one (level) passes

CR-74 is a fit. This is the test, registered before it runs, and the point of it is that nothing is
estimated from the new data at all. `delta` is fixed at 0.454 for the backlog bound and 0.394 for
the admission rule, both taken from CR-74's twenty cells and not refitted. The predicted effect at a
cell whose control arm reads baseline `b` is `Phi(Phi^-1(b) + delta) - b`, so the prediction is
conditional on a quantity the same run measures, which is what makes it checkable at operating
points nobody has visited yet.

**Falsifier one, the level.** RMS deviation of observed from predicted effects, over every gradeable
cell of the battery, above 0.045. That is twice CR-74's out-of-sample RMS, so a model that holds
only as well as it already did passes and one that degrades twofold fails.

**Falsifier two, the shape.** The success gain's maximum falling outside the baseline band 0.35 to
0.65. The curve puts the maximum at exactly 0.5 with no freedom; W4 put it at 0.484 on cells the
model was fitted to, and this asks for it on cells it was not.

**Three sections, chosen so that none of their cells appear in the fit.** X1 sweeps opposing
strength to twenty, past where W4 stopped, so the descending limb of the interior maximum rests on
more than the single point W4 could reach. X2 interpolates at strengths W4 skipped, where the curve
is steepest and a wrong shape costs the most. X3 moves the baseline with span instead of strength at
a strength W4 never used, which is the section that decides whether the model is about the metric or
merely about opposing strength. Blue loss rides along on every section, because the whole account
requires that where success stops reporting the capability, the uncensored axis still does.

## CR-76: the gate has a resolution, it is set by block size, and more blocks do not move it

**Verdict:** holds, 262 gated success contrasts audited and none below the floor

**The arithmetic came first and needs no data.** The gate requires that sixty percent of blocks
agree in sign. Mission success is zero or one per mission, so a block mean over `reps` missions moves
in steps of one over `reps`, and a block registers any difference at all only if at least one of its
missions flips. The fraction of blocks that can agree is therefore capped at
`1 - (1 - p)^reps` where `p` is the per-mission flip rate, and the sign requirement becomes

    p >= 1 - 0.40 ** (1 / reps)

which at sixty-four missions per block is 0.0142. Since the reported effect is bounded above by the
flip rate, an effect below that value can only clear the gate if the treatment also moves a
comparable number of missions the other way, which is the regime where the difference is noise. The
consequence runs against instinct: **running more blocks does not lower this floor. Only more
missions per block does.** Sixteen missions per block puts it at 0.0557, two hundred fifty-six at
0.0036, one thousand twenty-four at 0.0009.

**The audit, which the arithmetic did not need but which prices it.** Sweeping every contrast that
feeds a macro: 2150 published contrasts, 596 of them on mission success, the one metric whose block
means actually step in one over `reps`. Which metrics those are was established by asking the logs
rather than by reading names, because getting that wrong would have been this project's first genus
committed inside the check for its third: success steps in one over `reps` in 98.8 percent of
16,688 readings, `blue_loss_frac` in 12.6 percent since its denominator counts vehicles, and
`served_per_step` in 1.4 percent.

Of those 596, **262 gated and not one had an effect below the floor.** The smallest effect that ever
gated is 0.0405. The largest effect that read `equivalent` while below the floor is 0.01416, which
sits 0.00005 under the predicted 0.01422. A single counterexample would have refuted the whole
account and there are none in seventy battery logs.

**So 151 published `equivalent` verdicts were returned by arithmetic rather than by evidence.** They
are not wrong. Every one of them reports an effect that is genuinely small. What they cannot do is
distinguish an effect of 0.000 from one of 0.014, and no quantity of replication at that block size
ever could.

**This is a third genus, and its exposing question is again different.** The first asks what the
denominator counts and whether the arm changed it, and every instance produced a wrong number. The
second asks whether the event a metric counts can occur at all under an arm's definition, and every
instance produced a true number carrying nothing. The third asks **what the smallest effect this
decision rule could have separated from zero is, and whether the observed effect clears it.** Like
the second it is answerable at registration with no data at all, needing only the block size and the
sign threshold. Unlike the second, the remedy is not a different scenario or a different axis: it is
a larger block, and it is the one repair that a study which reaches for more replication will not
find, because more replication is exactly what does not work.

**What this does not say.** The floor is derived for a metric whose per-mission reading is zero or
one. It says nothing about continuous axes, where blocks essentially always differ and the sign
criterion is not the binding constraint. And it is a property of a sign-consistency rule rather than
of the world: a gate built only on a t statistic has no such floor, and would instead accept
differences this one refuses. Which is the better rule is not settled here. What is settled is that
this one has a resolution, that the resolution is computable before a run, and that it was not
being computed.
