## CR-77: a study's cost at a new operating point is predictable from a study already run at another

**Verdict:** A holds against both controls at 3 of 4 arms, B retracted

**Registered before any of the numbers below were read.** The quantities this conjecture is scored
on sit in the truth caches and in the `difficulty()` cache, both written for other purposes (the
answer key, and sizing the search ladder). Neither was inspected for this question before this
paragraph was written. The one thing already known and used is `delta = 0.454`, CR-74's fitted
latent offset for the backlog bound, which was fitted to *effect sizes in gated batteries* and is
being asked here to predict *a different quantity on a different data set*.

**The structure being asserted.** CR-74 modelled a capability as a constant added to a latent
margin, so a test at baseline `b` reports `Phi(Phi^-1(b) + delta) - b`. If that is the right shape
then it is not only a statement about one capability against one baseline. It says the whole design
set is a fixed vector of latent offsets `{delta_j}`, one per design, and an operating point is a
single number `b` that slides all of them along the same probit curve. The offsets belong to the
designs; the baseline belongs to the operating point; nothing else is free.

The benchmark suite is arranged to test exactly that, though it was not built to. It is two knobs
(the backlog bound `qcap`, the queue discipline `disc`) crossed with four spans
(`n_blue` in 8, 10, 12, 16) crossed with three metrics. Fixing a knob and a metric leaves the same
seven designs measured at four operating points.

**A, the prediction.** For `success`, fit the seven latent offsets at the *lowest* span alone
(`n_blue = 8`), anchoring one design at `delta = 0`. Then predict all remaining (design, span) cells
at spans 10, 12 and 16 with no further fitting, taking each held-out span's baseline from the anchor
design at that span and nothing else. That is six free offsets plus three baseline readings, and it
predicts eighteen scorable cells per knob.

The anchor design's own cells are fitted by construction and are therefore excluded from scoring.
That is the genus-two check run at registration rather than after: a metric whose value is entailed
by the fitting rule carries no evidence, and four of the twenty-eight cells per knob are entailed.

**Falsifier for A.** Out-of-sample RMS error above 0.045 in success units on the eighteen scored
cells, or out-of-sample R-squared below 0.5, at *either* knob. The threshold matches the one CR-75
registered so that the two are read on the same scale.

**B, the consequence, which is the part a program office would use.** The offsets fix the gap
between any two designs at any operating point, and the same probit reading fixes the per-mission
standard deviation. So the replication needed to separate two designs is determined at a new
operating point by a fit made at an old one, and a study can be priced before it is run. Scored
against the `budget_to_choose` the truth tables actually achieved.

**Falsifier for B.** If predicted and measured cost disagree in rank across the four spans, Spearman
below 0.5, at both knobs, then the model does not price studies and B is retracted.

**Pre-declared branch, so it is not decided after the fact.** The measured gaps between top designs
may themselves be noise: at two thousand missions the standard error of a success reading is about
0.011, and if more than half the cells have a measured top-two gap smaller than the standard error
of that gap, then the measured costs are not a usable comparison target. In that case B is reported
as a prediction with no valid test available, *not* as a validated one, and the fact that direct
measurement is the noisier of the two estimates is reported as the finding.

**Secondary and exploratory, declared as such.** `blue_loss_frac` is also a bounded fraction and the
same shape may apply. It is scored and reported, but it is exploratory: no threshold is registered
for it and it cannot support the claim on its own. `served_per_step` is a rate with no upper bound,
the threshold story does not apply, and it is not scored.

## CR-77, scored

**Verdict:** A holds against both controls at 3 of 4 arms, B retracted on its falsifier

**A holds, and the registered threshold turned out to be the weakest part of the registration.**
Fitting six latent offsets at `n_blue = 8` and reading one baseline per held-out span predicts
eighteen out-of-sample cells per knob at RMS 0.0122 and R-squared 0.967 on the backlog bound, and
RMS 0.0134 and R-squared 0.896 on the queue discipline. Both clear the registered 0.045 and 0.50.

**The controls were added after A had already passed, and they change how it reads.** The
registration named a threshold on RMS and R-squared and never asked whether a predictor with no
shape in it could clear the same bar. That is the entailed-zero question asked about a decision
rule instead of a metric, and not asking it is the same mistake the first paper's second genus
names. Two controls were added: a linear one, where a design's offset is a constant shift in the
probability itself, and a null one, where every design is predicted to equal the anchor.

| knob | metric | probit RMS | linear RMS | null RMS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `qcap` | success | 0.0122 | 0.0273 | 0.0609 |
| `qcap` | blue loss | 0.0174 | 0.0324 | 0.0468 |
| `disc` | success | 0.0134 | 0.0231 | 0.0084 |
| `disc` | blue loss | 0.0056 | 0.0097 | 0.0110 |

The probit shape beats the linear one at all four arms, by between 1.7 and 2.2 times on held-out
RMS. That is the CR-74 shape claim tested out of sample on a quantity CR-74 never touched, and it
is the part worth keeping.

The probit shape beats the null at three arms and **loses to it at the fourth**, queue discipline
on mission success, 0.0134 against 0.0084. The reason is visible once asked: across the seven
disciplines mission success spans 4.6 standard errors at eight assets and **1.0 standard error at
sixteen**. There is almost nothing there to predict, so the baseline alone predicts it, and the
fitted offsets carry forward the noise they were fitted on. That arm cannot test a shape and is
reported as uninformative rather than as a second confirmation. A registration that had asked
the entailed-zero question of its own threshold would have excluded it in advance.

**B is retracted on its registered falsifier.** Spearman between predicted and measured
replication cost across the four spans is -0.200 on the backlog bound and +0.400 on the queue
discipline; the registration required 0.50 at either. The pre-declared branch that would have
excused the test did not fire: it needed more than half the measured gaps to sit inside their own
standard error and exactly half of them do, four of eight. B is retracted, not excused.

**What the retraction shows, recorded separately because it is not a rescue of B.** The measured
cost is a ratio whose denominator is the measured gap, and on the queue discipline three of four
gaps sit inside one standard error of zero: 0.0075 against 0.0124, 0.0030 against 0.0097, and
0.0005 against 0.0033. Squaring that denominator gives numbers like a measured cost of 170,235
missions per design at sixteen assets against 2,435 at twelve. Those are not measurements of
anything. Whether a fitted estimate of the same quantity is steadier is a different claim from B,
needs its own registration, and does not un-retract B.

**A post hoc observation from the same tables, flagged as post hoc, and registered forward as
CR-78 C rather than claimed here.** Across both knobs and all four spans, the design set's grip
on the two bounded metrics weakens as span widens while its grip on the unbounded one
strengthens. In spread over standard error, the backlog bound runs 15.6, 15.6, 12.4, 7.5 on
success and 38.0, 39.7, 31.5, 19.8 on loss, while on delivered service it runs 113, 158, 213,
478. The queue discipline does the same thing: 4.6, 3.5, 3.9, 1.0 and 9.8, 9.0, 8.5, 6.4 against
22, 38, 65, 160. A threshold account predicts exactly that asymmetry, because compression against
a bound is what a bounded reading does and a rate has no bound to compress against. Delivered
service is the control the ceiling account never had.

## CR-78: the same fit, asked to predict an operating point nobody has simulated

**Verdict:** RETRACTED, all three parts

**Registered before the tables at `n_blue = 24` were built.** Nothing at that span exists on disk
for these metrics when this is written. The offsets below were fitted at `n_blue = 8` alone and
are frozen here as numbers so that scoring is transcription rather than judgement.

**D, declared first because it removes an arm before it can be misread.** Extrapolating the
anchor's probit trajectory puts mission success at `n_blue = 24` between 0.9991 and 0.9999 for
all seven designs at both knobs: a spread of about 0.0007 against a standard error of 0.011, or
six hundredths of one standard error. Success at this span cannot rank the design set, so it is
**not scored as a test of the shape**. It is still run, for one reason: if success at twenty-four
is not at ceiling then the baseline extrapolation is wrong and B fails. Declared now so that a
ceiling reading cannot later be reported as a confirmation of anything.

**A, the operationally useful form.** Measure only the anchor design at the new operating point
and predict the other six from the frozen offsets. Scored on blue loss at both knobs, twelve
predictions.

**Falsifier for A.** At either knob, probit RMS at or above the smaller of the null and linear
control RMS on the same cells, or probit RMS at or above half the measured spread across designs
at `n_blue = 24`. The control clause is the lesson CR-77 paid for.

**B, the bold form: no data at the new operating point at all.** The anchor's own baseline is
extrapolated from its probit trajectory across the four fitted spans, which is a straight line in
probit with slope -0.1009 per asset on the backlog bound and -0.0822 on the queue discipline for
blue loss. The point predictions follow.

`qcap` blue loss at `n_blue = 24`, anchor `{"queue_capacity": 12}`:

| design | offset | predicted |
|---|---|---|
| `{"queue_capacity": 12}` | +0.0000 | 0.0437 |
| `{"queue_capacity": 1}` | -0.2611 | 0.0244 |
| `{"queue_capacity": 2}` | -0.2687 | 0.0240 |
| `{"queue_capacity": 3}` | -0.1501 | 0.0315 |
| `{"queue_capacity": 4}` | -0.0659 | 0.0380 |
| `{"queue_capacity": 6}` | -0.0353 | 0.0406 |
| `{"queue_capacity": 8}` | -0.0183 | 0.0421 |

`disc` blue loss at `n_blue = 24`, anchor `{"queue_discipline": "patient_first"}`:

| design | offset | predicted |
|---|---|---|
| `{"queue_discipline": "easy_first"}` | -0.0469 | 0.0419 |
| `{"queue_discipline": "edf"}` | -0.0711 | 0.0398 |
| `{"queue_discipline": "fcfs"}` | -0.0254 | 0.0439 |
| `{"queue_discipline": "hard_first"}` | -0.0184 | 0.0446 |
| `{"queue_discipline": "patient_first"}` | +0.0000 | 0.0463 |
| `{"queue_discipline": "random"}` | -0.0471 | 0.0419 |
| `{"queue_discipline": "round_robin"}` | -0.0709 | 0.0398 |

**Falsifier for B.** The anchor design's own reading at `n_blue = 24` differing from its
predicted value by more than 0.010 in loss units, at either knob. That isolates the one thing B
adds over A, which is the extrapolated baseline.

**C, the discriminator, and the reason this run is worth an hour of a cluster.** If the
compression is a property of bounded readings rather than of the simulator getting duller, then
at `n_blue = 24` against `n_blue = 16` the design set's spread in units of its own standard error
must FALL on blue loss and on success, and must RISE on delivered service, at both knobs. Six
directions, registered.

**Falsifier for C.** Any one of the six going the other way.



## Scored together: the two span-moving tests, and what they jointly kill

**Verdict:** both retracted; the latent account survives only where opposing strength moves the baseline

**CR-75, retracted on falsifier two.** Level passes: RMS deviation of observed from predicted
effects is 0.0229 over 26 gradeable success cells against a registered 0.045, and against CR-74's
own out-of-sample 0.0225. Nothing was refitted; `delta` stayed at 0.454 and 0.394. Shape fails: the
success gain peaks at baseline 0.2319 and the registered band was 0.35 to 0.65. The curve puts its
maximum at exactly 0.500 with no freedom, so a peak at 0.23 is not a near miss, it is the wrong
shape.

**The diagnostic is the section split, and it is the one X3 was registered to provide.** X1 and X2
move the baseline by opposing strength and give RMS 0.0115 and 0.0193. X3 moves it by span and
gives 0.0310, with a worst cell of +0.0654. The registration said X3 decides whether the model is
about the metric or merely about opposing strength. It decided.

**CR-78, retracted in all three parts, and it moves the baseline by span too.** A fails: at
`n_blue = 24` a null predictor beats the fitted offsets, 0.0066 against 0.0201 on the backlog bound
and 0.0038 against 0.0048 on the queue discipline, so offsets fitted at `n_blue = 8` actively hurt
at 24. B fails: the extrapolated anchor baseline predicted blue loss 0.0437 and measured 0.1046, an
error of 0.061 against a registered tolerance of 0.010, with the sign wrong, since loss RISES with
span past sixteen rather than continuing to fall. C fails: two of six registered directions reverse,
both on the queue discipline.

**Part D was declared untestable in advance and the declaration was wrong in its reasoning.** It
predicted success at `n_blue = 24` would sit within six hundredths of a standard error of ceiling,
computed from an ASSUMED standard error of 0.011. The measured standard error is 0.00049, twenty
times smaller, because Bernoulli variance collapses as the mean approaches one. The arm is therefore
gradeable at 3.05 and 2.59 standard errors even though every design reads 1.0000. The conclusion,
that this arm cannot support the shape claim, survives; the number used to justify it did not.
Assuming a standard error rather than computing one is the same defect this project catalogues
elsewhere, committed inside a registration whose purpose was to prevent exactly that.

**Joint reading.** Two registered tests, run on different data, both fail specifically where the
baseline is moved by span rather than by opposing strength, and the one that moves it furthest by
span fails hardest. The latent-shift account is retained as a description of what opposing strength
does to a bounded metric over the range CR-74 fitted, and retracted as a general threshold reading
of the metric. It goes into neither manuscript as an account. CR-74 stands as what it always said it
was: a post hoc fit with an out-of-sample check.

## CR-72, CR-73 and the estimator controls, scored at three hundred replicates

**Verdict:** M1 refuted, M2 refuted, M3 unscoreable; CR-73 B retracted, C holds; one of four
estimator controls gates, against the method it was meant to help.

Job `st-search-9471782` on hopper, `normal` with `--constraint=intel`, 540,000 rows in 1,352 s,
`$ROOT/search/bench-r300.jsonl`. Ten methods, thirty problems, six rungs, three hundred replicates,
all 54,000 cells reached by every method. This supersedes the twenty-replicate sweep for every
number in paper two.

**CR-72 M1 is refuted, and scoring it as registered is what refutes it.** M1 predicted
EQUIVALENCE to `grid` at every budget on both grading rules, restricted to problems whose key
resolves. Four of the six rung and grading-rule combinations gate away. The worst is the low rung on
argmax recovery, where `ucb_raw` is worse than uniform allocation by 0.3990 at t=-42.49. The top rung
is the one place the two ARE equivalent, -0.0006 at t=-0.89, so scoring M1 at the top rung alone,
which is where every other contrast in this paper is scored, would have called it held. The
registered wording said every budget and the scorer now honours that. M2 is refuted at the middle
rung as registered, +0.0015, t=1.44, 46% sign consistency.

An earlier note in this session recorded M1 as "refuted with the sign reversed, predicted +0.0772
and measured -0.1854". That was wrong about what M1 predicted and is superseded by the generated
numbers above. Nothing generated it, which is why it stood. M3 cannot be scored: the arm it needs was never run, because the sweep driver
carried a hardcoded six-name method list that shadowed the ten-name registry, so both controls and
both discriminators produced no rows. The list sat two hundred lines above a comment warning that
methods must come from the registry.

**CR-73 B is retracted.** Ranking by the answer key and ranking by the best value any method found
agree at Spearman 0.988 with the same winner on 11 of 13 problems, against a registered threshold
that required them to disagree. C holds: 8 of 9 methods separate from random allocation.

**The headline changed for the third time and the reason is worth more than the headline.** The
first sweep found uniform allocation unbeatable at every budget, which was an artefact of a ladder
whose top rung sat below the separating budget on 25 of 30 problems. The corrected ladder reaches it
on 15 of 30, and at twenty replicates produced a crossing: `ucb_pooled` ahead of grid at the top rung
by -0.0183, t=-3.74, which cleared the tier. At three hundred replicates the effect halves to
-0.0097 and lands under the minimum practical effect of 0.0157, while |t| more than doubles to 8.09.
More data made the difference more certain and less worth acting on at the same time. `RsBeatGrid`
is zero: no method separates from uniform allocation at any budget on this suite.

That is the strongest argument in either paper for a gate with a minimum-practical-effect clause.
Three of the four estimator controls are equivalent at |t| of 22.06, 15.26 and 11.79. A tier written
in significance alone would have called all three real, and would have read the extra replicates on
the crossing as confirmation of a result they in fact removed.

**The one control that gates runs backwards.** Fitting expected improvement to the best observed
value rather than to the raw surrogate is WORSE by +0.0357, t=40.46, 69% sign. An incumbent read off
the noisiest arm seen so far is an optimistic target and chasing it costs more than the acquisition
rule recovers.

## Two more instrument defects, found while promoting the sweep into paper two

**Verdict:** both are genus one, both are now guarded by tests.

**The manuscript cited the wrong denominator for the ladder artefact.** It reported the count of
problems whose top rung fell short of the separating budget as `\BnSpreadBounded`, which is the
count of answer keys that cannot resolve their own top two. Those are different quantities, 18
against 25, and the second was never measured until now. The macro was correct for the sentence it
was derived for and wrong for the sentence it was carried into, which is the genus exactly. Both
ladder counts are now computed from the sweep files themselves by `short_of_separation`, and the
superseded twenty-replicate effect is rescored through the same gate by
`make_results_numbers.py --prev` rather than remembered in the prose.

**The benchmark's published inventory came from a directory listing.** `make_bench_numbers.py`
globbed `experiments/results/search_truth/*.json` instead of iterating the problem registry, so the
six span-24 tables written for CR-78 had silently joined the benchmark: thirty problems would have
been reported as thirty-two because six files were saved next to the others. `PREDICT` was kept out
of `PROBLEMS` precisely so a queued sweep could not be invalidated, and the generator went around
that decision by reading the filesystem. It surfaced only because two of the added tables sit at the
success ceiling with a top-two gap and a standard error of exactly zero, so `bounded_ratio` raised
rather than dividing by an unbounded denominator. The guard added after the 52-million extrapolation
caught a defect it was not written for.

**A third error in the same paragraph was not a genus instance.** The bound-effect check claimed the
success problems' spans were comparable to the widest at 0.156; they run 0.134, 0.087, 0.041, 0.027,
0.024, 0.021 and 0.003. That was prose written from one remembered number. The paragraph now makes
the claim it actually needs, which is reachability: on 7 of 8 success problems the design axis moves
the metric further than that problem's own key can resolve, by at least a factor of 1.3, so the tie
between the top two is not entailed by the ceiling. `disc_s16_succ` spans 0.0025 against a detectable
gap of 0.0065 and is reported as ceiling-adjacent. A test now fails the build if any decimal appears
in paper two's prose rather than coming from a macro.

## CR-73 C, rescored at three hundred replicates, and it is weaker than first reported

**Verdict:** holds on its registered falsifier, but 2 of 9 rather than 7 of 9, and the two point in
opposite directions.

The falsifier said the unresolved problems should be dropped if NO method separates from `random`
under regret at |t| above 1.70 with sixty percent sign consistency, paired by problem. Scored over
the whole ladder on the 17 unresolved problems, `ucb_pooled` separates in the favourable direction
at -0.0400, t=-25.61, 61% sign, and `gp_noisy` separates in the unfavourable one at +0.0602,
t=+30.51, 62% sign. The other seven miss, almost all of them on sign consistency: `grid` at 59%,
`thompson` at 59%, `ucb_raw` at 58%, `ucb` at 54%, `gp_raw` at 53%, `halving` at 53%. The effects are
not small, `thompson` reaches -0.0358 and `grid` -0.0303, but they do not hold their sign from
problem to problem.

**Why the earlier count was wrong.** The 7 of 9 came from `RsBeatRandom`, which counts methods
separating from random at the TOP rung across all thirty problems, and was carried into a sentence
about the unresolved seventeen across all rungs. Two different populations and two different budget
selections. That is the same genus as the ladder citation corrected in the section above, met twice
in one afternoon in the same manuscript.

**What the paper now says.** That these problems carry information about method quality, which is
what justified keeping them, but that what they mainly separate is a bad method from an arbitrary
one, so a benchmark built only from them would be a weak instrument. The count and both names are
generated by `make_results_numbers.py` and cannot drift from the sweep again.

## A fourth genus, named today: a count that only counts the favourable direction

**Verdict:** named and guarded; three instances found in one manuscript in one afternoon.

The first three genera are about denominators, reachability and decision rules. This one is about
the SIGN of a separation. A benchmark asks "how many methods separate from the baseline", computes
it as "how many gate in the direction I hoped for", and reports the answer as though it were the
question. Three instances, all in paper two, all found within an hour of each other:

1. `RsBeatGrid` counts methods with LOWER regret than uniform allocation and returns zero, and the
   manuscript said "no method separates from uniform allocation at the top of the ladder". Three do:
   `gp_ei` by +0.0858 of the spread, `gp_noisy` by +0.1064, `random` by +0.0162, all worse.
2. CR-73 C was scored as "methods better than `random` on the unresolved problems", giving one.
   Counting both directions gives two, and the second, `gp_noisy` at +0.0602, is the more
   informative of the pair: it shows those problems can tell a bad method from an arbitrary one.
3. CR-72 M1's worst-case direction was reported from the sign of the effect alone, which is correct
   for normalized regret and backwards for argmax recovery. `ucb_raw` at -0.3990 on argmax recovery
   is 39.9 points WORSE than uniform allocation, and the first version of the generator printed
   "better than".

The exposing question, in the form the other three take: **ask what direction the count is in, and
then ask what the other direction contains.** A method that is decisively worse than the baseline
has separated from it just as surely as one that is decisively better, and for a benchmark whose
purpose is to say which methods the instrument can tell apart, the unfavourable direction is
evidence of exactly the same kind.

All three are now generated in both directions: `RsBeatGrid` and `RsWorseGrid`, `RsSepUnresReg` and
`RsSepUnresWorse`, and `RsMOneWorstDir` reads its direction from the field rather than the sign.

## The low-budget deficit is a coverage failure, and the sweep had been recording it all along

**Verdict:** measured, and it replaces the explanation the manuscript carried for one draft.

The generated methods table showed seven of ten methods recovering the best design on exactly 29% of
low-rung cells. Seven identical figures is a signature, not a coincidence. The `distinct` column,
present in every row since `runner.py` was written and never once read, gives the answer.

At the bottom rung the budget is 16 missions on a design set averaging 8.3 designs. Mean distinct
designs actually evaluated: `grid` 7.4, `halving` 7.4, `random` 5.0, and every UCB, Thompson and GP
variant 2.3. Found-rates track coverage: 0.6874, 0.6541, 0.5559, and 0.2885 to 0.2908 for the seven,
against 2.3/8.3 = 0.28 for a method that returns the best design exactly when the best design is
among those it touched.

The low-budget deficit is therefore not concentration on noise among designs a method has assessed.
It is a method that never evaluates most of the design set. The manuscript had carried a different
explanation for one draft, that unequal sampling inflates the variance of the argmax. The coverage
column does not support that one and does support this one, so it was replaced rather than kept
alongside.

**It also sharpens CR-72 M1.** The registration reasoned that a bonus swamping the gap turns the
allocator into uniform sampling. It turns it into round-robin in intent, but a round-robin that pulls
in batches and runs out of budget before it runs out of arms is uniform allocation TRUNCATED after
28% of the arms. That is a different object, and it loses to the real thing by 39.9 points of argmax
recovery at the bottom rung.

**The general lesson, and it is the cheapest one in this project.** The column that settled this had
been written to every row of every sweep for weeks. Nothing read it. Before adding an experiment to
explain a result, read the columns already being recorded: an instrument that logs more than it
reports is one query away from an answer, and an explanation invented without that query is a guess
however plausible it sounds.

## CR-79, REGISTERED. THE BATCH SIZE, NOT THE ACQUISITION RULE, IS WHAT FAILS AT SMALL BUDGETS

**Verdict:** A, B and C all hold; the deficit falls from +0.3990 to +0.0023 and no repaired method
beats uniform allocation, which is what was NOT predicted.

Registered before any repaired method exists, because the repair is obvious enough that adopting it
first and reporting it afterwards would be indistinguishable from tuning.

`_ucb`, `thompson` and `gp_ei` all take `chunk = 8` missions per pull, and all of them seed by
walking a permutation of the designs, one chunk each, until the budget runs out. At the bottom rung
the budget is 16 missions on 7 designs, so the seed pass covers 2 of them and the search proper
never runs. Measured mean distinct designs evaluated at that rung: 2.3 against 7.4 for `grid`.

The constant is the defect, and it is the first genus again. Eight missions is a sensible pull when
the budget is thousands; carried into a rung of sixteen it silently redefines the method. The
comment at `methods.py:112` already says "a budget below n * chunk cannot seed every arm", so the
condition was known, and what was fixed there was the ORDER the arms get seeded in rather than the
fact that most of them do not.

**The repair, stated before it is measured.** Let the pull size fall with the budget:
`chunk = max(1, min(8, budget // n))`, so a method always completes one pass over the design set
whenever the budget affords one mission per design, and only then begins to adapt. Repaired variants
ship under new names beside the originals, which stay in the suite as controls.

**A.** The bottom-rung argmax-recovery deficit against uniform allocation shrinks by at least half.
It is currently 0.6874 for `grid` against 0.2885 for `ucb_pooled`, a deficit of 0.3989.
FALSIFIER: the repaired deficit exceeds 0.20. The repair would then not be about coverage and the
account above is wrong.

**B.** The repair costs nothing at the top rung, where the budget covers every design either way.
FALSIFIER: repaired `ucb_pooled` gates UP against unrepaired `ucb_pooled` on top-rung regret. A
smaller pull size would then be buying coverage at the price of a noisier per-arm estimate, and the
repair is a trade rather than a fix.

**C.** At the bottom rung the repaired methods become EQUIVALENT to `grid` on argmax recovery rather
than beating it, because a method that spends its whole budget on one full pass IS uniform
allocation. FALSIFIER: `grid` still gates up against every repaired method, which would mean
coverage was not the binding constraint after all.

**What is deliberately NOT predicted.** That any repaired method beats uniform allocation at any
rung. The coverage account says the repair should close the low-budget gap and do nothing at the
top, so `RsBeatGrid` should stay at zero. If it does not, the paper's headline changes and the
change will have been registered as a surprise rather than presented as the plan.

**How it will be run.** A separate sweep with the repaired methods added, so the shipped
`bench-r300.jsonl` and its balanced-cell intersection are untouched and the current manuscript stays
reproducible from the file it names.

## CR-79 scored: all three hold, and the one thing not predicted did not happen either

**Verdict:** A, B and C hold; `WmBeatUni` is zero.

Job `st-warm-9471928`, 756,000 rows in 1,954 s, fourteen methods at 300 replicates, written to
`$ROOT/search/bench-warm.jsonl`. The shipped ten-method sweep is untouched, so the main comparison
keeps its own provenance and this follow-up keeps its own.

**A holds by two orders of magnitude.** Coverage at the bottom rung goes from 2.3 designs of 8.3 to
7.4, which is exactly what `grid` reaches. The argmax-recovery deficit against uniform allocation
falls from +0.3990 to +0.0023 at worst and -0.0074 at best, against a falsifier that would have
fired above 0.20. Two of the four repaired methods now recover the best design very slightly more
often than uniform allocation does, and neither difference is anywhere near the tier.

**B holds, and more cleanly than expected.** Three of the four repaired methods are BYTE-IDENTICAL
to their controls at the top rung: effect exactly +0.0000, t exactly 0.00, sign consistency 0%
because every paired difference is zero. That is the strongest possible evidence the repair is
surgical, since `_fit_chunk` returns the original eight whenever the budget affords it. Only
`gp_ei_warm` differs, at -0.0018, t=-2.23, equivalent, because its warm start also changed: it now
fits the surrogate to the whole design set rather than to four of it.

**C holds.** All four repaired methods are equivalent to `grid` at the bottom rung on argmax
recovery, at effects of +0.0023 to -0.0074. A method that spends its whole budget on one full pass
IS uniform allocation, and the numbers say so.

**The claim deliberately not predicted did not fire.** No repaired method beats uniform allocation
at any rung. The repair removes a large artefact and changes no conclusion.

**What this does to the paper.** It strengthens it. Without CR-79 the low-budget result reads as a
finding about adaptive search, and it is not one: it is a batch size chosen for a different budget
regime. With CR-79 the paper says something sharper and more useful to a program office. Adaptive
search does not beat enumeration on mission-design problems of this shape at any budget, AND the
forty points of argmax recovery the standard implementations throw away at small budgets are a
fixable defect worth fixing, and fixing them buys parity rather than advantage.

**Why the controls earned their keep.** A benchmark that had not carried the unrepaired versions
would have reported the artefact as a property of the methods. A benchmark that had only carried
repaired versions would have had nothing to report. CR-72 registered those controls for a different
reason, to separate a broken estimator from a claim about adaptive search, and they paid off here
for a reason nobody anticipated when they were added.

## CR-80, REGISTERED. IF LEARNING HELPS MISSION DESIGN, IT IS THE ALLOCATION AND NOT THE SEARCH

**Verdict:** registered, not yet run. This is the RL question stated so it can fail.

Paper two's result is that on thirty mission-design problems no adaptive search beats enumerating
the design set, at any budget, and that the low-budget collapse people would have called a method
result was a batch size (CR-79). That is a clean negative, and it points somewhere specific rather
than nowhere.

**Why searching harder cannot be the answer here.** The design sets are 5 to 28 points and the
budget always affords a full pass at the rungs that matter. There is no search problem left: the
argmax over a fully sampled set is a lookup. What is expensive is knowing which entry of that lookup
is right, and paper two measures how expensive, a median 6.1 to 1 ratio between the spread and the
top-two gap, so that separating the best two designs costs at least 38 times what choosing a good
one does. The scarce resource is replication, not evaluation.

**So the decision worth learning is where to spend replications, not which design to try.** That is
a sequential decision problem with a state (what every design's estimate and its standard error are
now), an action (which design to replicate next and how deep), and a reward the answer key can score
exactly, since the optimum is known independently. Every ingredient an RL formulation needs is
already in this repository, and the stored mission pools make the environment replayable, so a
policy can be trained and evaluated on the same missions every method in paper two received.

**A.** A policy trained on a held-out subset of the thirty problems and evaluated on the rest beats
uniform allocation on normalized regret at the middle rung, at the paper's full tier.
FALSIFIER: equivalent or worse. The honest conclusion is then that the allocation decision on
problems of this shape is already solved by spreading evenly, which would be a second negative and
would say the same thing twice, which is worth knowing.

**B.** Whatever the policy learns transfers across the metric axis. Trained on delivered service and
evaluated on mission success and blue loss, it stays at least equivalent to uniform allocation.
FALSIFIER: it gates down against uniform on either transfer axis. The policy would then be fitting
the noise structure of one metric, and the result is about `served_per_step` rather than about
mission design.

**C.** The policy's advantage, where it has one, is LARGER on the problems whose answer key cannot
resolve its own top two, because those are the ones where replication is the binding constraint.
FALSIFIER: no ordering, or the reverse one. The mechanism would then not be the one claimed even if
A holds, and it ships as the weaker result.

**What is deliberately not predicted.** That the policy beats uniform allocation at the bottom rung.
CR-79 showed a budget that affords one pass leaves nothing to allocate cleverly, and a policy that
appeared to beat uniform there should be suspected of the coverage artefact before it is believed.

**Held out before anything is trained.** The evaluation problems are fixed now and written here:
`qcap_s12_loss`, `disc_s16_srv`, `reask_bound`, `qcap_s8_succ`, `ladder_span`, and `disc_s10_loss`.
Nothing may be trained on those six, and the split is recorded before a policy exists so that it
cannot be chosen after seeing which way the numbers went.
